# Frontier Insight — capability reference

> The long catalogue, for looking up an exact setting or how a feature works. It is not meant to be read top to bottom: the one-page overview is [capabilities.md](capabilities.md), and a first run is in [first-quest.md](first-quest.md).

What this doc is: a feature index for people evaluating FI or looking
for a specific knob to twist. README is the elevator pitch; USAGE.md
is the YAML schema; this file is the breadth catalogue.

## The 21-node graph

> Simplified happy path below — see ``core/engine.py:_build_graph``
> for the authoritative edge set. Conditional branches that aren't
> drawn: the design→implement vs design→auto_collect_data fork (no-
> simulation mode), and the ``broaden_lit`` arc from cross_check back
> to literature when analyze asks for a wider sweep.

```
START → clarify → ideate → literature ←─────┐ (broaden_lit)
                                │             │
                                ↓             │
                          select_skills       │
                                │             │
                                ↓             │
                              design ─────────┘
                                │
            ┌───────────────────┴───────────────────┐
            ↓ (computational)                       ↓ (no_simulation)
     implement_outline                       auto_collect_data   ‡ survey: design ──→ web_figures (skips
            ↓                                        ↓              auto_collect_data … web_plots) ──→ analyze
            ↓                                        ↓
        implement ──→ execute ──┐               wait_for_data
                                │                    ↓
                          execute_reflect ←─(retry)  data_load
                                │                    ↓
                                │                 web_plots
                                │                    ↓
                                ↓ (proceed)       web_figures
                              improve ──(a change kept: run it in full)──→ execute
                                │                    │
                                ↓                    │
                              analyze ←───────────────┘
                                ↓
                          cross_check ──┐ (re_experiment → design)
                                │       │ (broaden_lit → literature)
                                ↓
                          evidence_gate ─┐ (broaden → literature)
                                │ (write)
                                ↓
                              write
                                ↓
                          claim_check
                                ↓
                              review ──→ human_feedback ──→ END (accept)
                                │
                                ↓ (revise → design)
```

Feedback loops in this diagram: `execute_reflect → execute` (retry),
`improve → execute` (once, after a change to the simulation was kept),
`cross_check → design` or `cross_check → literature` (analyze re-
route), `evidence_gate → literature` (broaden), `review → design`
(revise). `evidence_gate` (between cross_check and write) weighs
assembled evidence before any paper is written; `claim_check` (between
write and review) grounds the paper's claims. The `human_feedback`
gate, when enabled, sits between review and END.

The design fork has a **third branch, `survey`** (‡ in the diagram), for a
descriptive history / overview synthesised purely from the literature — no
experiment and no dataset. It is a stronger form of no-simulation: after
`design` it routes straight to `web_figures` (license-clean illustrative
images) → `analyze`, skipping the experiment (`implement`/`execute`) **and**
the whole data-collection chain (`auto_collect_data` → `wait_for_data` →
`data_load` → `web_plots`). `analyze` synthesises the reviewed sources
directly. Survey mode turns on from `engine.survey_mode: true`, from the
"Literature synthesis" research approach in the interview, or automatically
when the clarify step classifies the topic shape as `survey` (a "history of
X" / "evolution of X" / overview topic).

Feedback loops:

| Loop | Trigger | Bound by |
|---|---|---|
| `execute_reflect → execute` | Script crashed (rc != 0 or no `RESULT_JSON`), a result broke its declared bounds, or (once per quest) a saved figure has a legend drawn over its data or a title over a panel title | `engine.exec_reflect_max_iterations` (default 3) |
| `improve` (rounds inside the node), then `improve → execute` once | A check of correctness FI counts is not met; a round changes the simulation once and is kept only when no check got worse; a kept version runs once more in full | `engine.improve_rounds` (default 3, for the whole quest; a rerun `--from` the run or earlier starts again) |
| `cross_check → design` | `analyze.next_step == "re_experiment"` | `engine.max_iterations` (default 2) |
| `cross_check → literature` | `analyze.next_step == "broaden_lit"` — re-enters literature with the design's hypothesis folded into the query | `engine.max_iterations` (default 2) |
| `review → design` | `review.verdict == "revise"` | `engine.max_iterations` (default 2) |

## Capabilities

Grouped for skimmability. If you want to know whether FI does X, this
is the index.

### Getting started

- **`python launch.py` sets up its own environment.** No `pip install` step is required first: a missing dependency (a bare `python launch.py --config ...` right after `git clone`, before anything has ever been installed) is caught at the top-level import in `launch.py` and handled by `launch._bootstrap_or_reraise` instead of a bare traceback, choosing one of three things depending on what was already running: a venv or conda env you *activated* yourself (`VIRTUAL_ENV` / `CONDA_DEFAULT_ENV` set) gets the missing package installed into it, in place — never a switch to a `.venv/` you didn't choose; plain system Python with `.venv/` already there from an earlier bootstrap is *silently* relaunched into it — no banner, no prompt, no reinstall, which is the entire cost of every run after the first, not the ask-and-install flow repeating on every single invocation; plain system Python with no `.venv/` yet, or one that's still missing something even after that silent relaunch (a `git pull` added a dependency), gets the full flow — a status line, `[Y/n]` when there's a person to ask, `python -m venv .venv` if needed, `pip install -e .`, relaunch. Proceeds without asking when stdin isn't a real terminal (CI, a piped command — there's no one to ask, and it's the same operation the README already documents doing by hand; an `EOFError` on the prompt despite `isatty()` saying yes — Windows reports a NUL-redirected stdin as a TTY, unlike POSIX's `/dev/null` — is treated the same way, not as a decline). The relaunch itself replaces the process on POSIX (`os.execv`) but not on Windows, where `os.execv` returns control to the caller reporting exit code 0 regardless of what actually happens next; there, the relaunch is a real child process instead, waited on, its exit code (and a Ctrl-C during that wait, mapped to 130) mirrored exactly. `FI_SKIP_BOOTSTRAP=1` turns all of this off entirely (falls straight through to the plain traceback); it also does nothing for an installed package with no `pyproject.toml` alongside it (nothing there to install from). Never attempts the heavier optional externals (pandoc/LaTeX, Marp, Docker) — those stay exactly as feature-gated and skip-with-a-warning as before.
- **`fi demo` is the first step** (`python launch.py demo` from a checkout; `core/demo.py`). It writes a small example quest, `fi-demo.yaml` (three numerical integrators on a damped oscillator: no data needed, `result_use: explore`, one design round, both pauses off), into the folder it is run from, never over an existing file (the next is `fi-demo-2.yaml`, then `-3`, ...). The example lives in the package, not in `examples/`, which is not in the wheel. It names the first provider set up on this computer (`openai`, `gemini`, `claude_cli`, `codex_cli`, `gemini_cli`, `ollama`, in that order; for Ollama the first downloaded chat model), checks each in turn with the no-cost readiness check below until one passes, and says what stops it when none does (with nothing set up it names `openai` and says to set `OPENAI_API_KEY`); then it exits 1, so `fi demo && fi --config fi-demo.yaml` does not go on to a model found not ready. Otherwise, in a terminal, it asks before running the quest for real; without one (a script, CI) it prints the exact `fi --config fi-demo.yaml` and stops, exit 0.
- **`fi --doctor` is quick; `--doctor --deep` goes further.** The quick one checks this computer only: no network, nothing loaded, a short time limit per check (3 s; a check that does not answer is reported and the next one runs), one line printed as each check starts, the time the checks took at the end, and the engine is not imported (the whole command takes a couple of seconds). The embedding model is looked for in the model cache (`core.passages.embed_model_cached`: the package found, a downloaded all-MiniLM-L6-v2 in `HF_HUB_CACHE`, `HF_HOME/hub`, `FI_MODELS_DIR/hub` or `~/.cache/huggingface/hub`), not loaded. The providers are listed as installed / key set. `--deep` says first that it will load the embedding model (downloading it the first time) and ask each provider's service, then does: the model loaded, and each provider's full readiness. Both exit 0 when something is missing: that is a diagnosis, not a failure.
- **Provider readiness, the same words everywhere** (`core/provider_readiness.py`). Not a READY yes/no (which only meant "a key is set" or "the command is on PATH") but a ladder, stopping at the first step not met: *not installed → installed → signed in (or "signed in: unknown") → reachable → model available*, plus *no key / key present / key rejected* for an API key and *not running / model missing* for Ollama. Each comes with one plain sentence and, when something is wrong, how to fix it. A CLI is asked with its own sign-in status command, with a 5 s limit and never a generation (`claude auth status`, reading only `loggedIn`; `codex login status`); the Gemini, Copilot and Antigravity CLIs have none, so they say "signed in: unknown" and the first call tells. Ollama is asked for its models (`GET /api/tags` at the address the quest will call: `provider.base_url`, else 127.0.0.1:11434; an Ollama in a container counts, the `ollama` command is not needed; before a launch with no model named, the provider's default model is the one looked for). An API key is "key present" until the service is asked, which only the launch check, `fi demo` and `--deep` do, with the provider's free model list (`GET /models`): a refused key (401) stops the launch; a key that may not list models (403) or a model the list does not show is not stopped, since the list can be incomplete, and the first call tells. A `provider.base_url` of your own with no `provider.api_key_env` needs no key (the same rule as the engine's start-up check) and its `/models` is asked without one. Shown in the CLI interview's provider list (local check only, so it appears at once), under the web interview's provider picker, on the web Settings page (status commands and a local Ollama, no request to an API-key service), and by `fi --doctor`. VS Code lists no providers: it uses the Copilot Chat picker's model, which VS Code checks itself.
- **The chosen model is checked before a quest is launched.** The CLI interview (`--new`) and the web form's Launch run the full readiness check for the provider and model the person chose (`provider_readiness.preflight`: sign-in, the service, the model; nothing paid for). When it would fail, the quest is not started, the sentence and the fix are shown, and the answers are kept: the CLI prints `fi --config <the draft YAML>` to run once fixed; the web page keeps every answer on the review screen (the server answers 409 with the sentence and the draft's path) so Launch can be pressed again. `vscode_extension` is not checked here (the bridge check covers it).
- **Bare `fi` offers three next steps.** In a terminal, `fi` with nothing after it lists *Start a new quest*, *Open the web app* and *Check my setup* (the same as `fi --new`, `fi --serve` and `fi --doctor`; Enter leaves). With no terminal (a script, CI, a pipe) it prints the short help on stderr and exits 2. `fi --help` stays short; `fi --help-all` and `fi tools --help` list the rest.
- **The web interview never strands you.** Every request the new-quest page makes first has a time limit and is checked; a server that answers with an error, drops the connection or does not answer gets a plain sentence and a **Retry** button in place of "Loading questions…". Your answers are kept as you type, in the browser and on the server (`/api/interview/draft/<id>`, the id is in the page's address as `?d=`), so a failed Launch, a closed tab or a restarted server keeps them; they are removed once the quest is launched. Launch can be pressed once at a time and comes back after any failure, with the reason; it carries a key, so a retry after a lost answer, or a double click, gets the same quest back and never starts a second one (the server keeps the answer for a week, also across a restart).
- **The web pages work offline.** The icon font, the text fonts and the Tailwind styles are served by FI itself (`web/static/vendor/`), so a machine with no internet access shows the pages styled and the icons as icons, never as words like "add" or "settings"; no page loads anything from another host.
- **Cancel stops everything a quest started.** Cancelling a quest or a tool job on the web page stops it with every program it started (an experiment script and its workers), not only the quest's own process; the quest still keeps running if the web server itself stops. A timed-out or cancelled `pip freeze` (the environment record) or slide renderer (Marp, pandoc) stops the same way.
- **The Settings page opens without starting the knowledge layer.** Its first view reads the knowledge layer's settings and store location only (`/api/knowledge/info`, in a worker thread, 3 s limit). Counting the documents opens the store and loads its search index, so it runs when asked: the *Inspect knowledge base* button (`POST /api/knowledge/inventory`, in a worker thread, 60 s limit). Under `--no-axon-sidecar` nothing on the page starts the knowledge layer by itself.

### Engine + execution

- **Async LangGraph engine** with 21 nodes and 4 feedback loops; see `core/engine.py:_build_graph` for the actual edges.
- **Curated progress, unfiltered `run.log`** — `core/engine.py:STAGE_PROGRESS` is a fixed map of graph-node name to one plain sentence; `Engine._progress_stage` logs it (deduped against immediate repeats) marked `extra={"progress": True}`, and `_ProgressOnly` (a `logging.Filter`) is the only thing that decides what reaches a person: the console `StreamHandler` and a second `FileHandler` at `.fi/progress.log`. `run.log`'s own `FileHandler` carries no filter and keeps every internal line, always. The web page's `/log` and `/log/stream` read `progress.log` when it exists (falling back to `run.log` for a quest run before this existed); VS Code's bridge reads the same filtered stderr as the CLI, so all three interfaces show the identical short list with no extra wiring per surface. `launch.py:_quiet_network_logs` similarly raises `httpx`/`httpcore` and the `fi.*` / `frontier_insight.*` support-module loggers to `WARNING` (never the per-quest `frontier_insight.<id>` logger, which always sets its own level).
- **One Python for everything (default)** — agent-generated Python runs on the same interpreter that runs FI (`execution.shared_interpreter: true`). Nothing is built under the quest's own output path, so a package installed once (`pip install -e .`, or an earlier quest) is simply there for the next quest, and a long Windows output path can no longer stop pip installing `torch`. The cost is no isolation: what a quest installs stays in FI's environment. Installs are serialised across processes by a file lock, since concurrent quests share one site-packages. On success the packages the quest asked for are recorded, pinned to the versions it ran against, in `.fi/requirements.lock.txt`. `run.log` starts with `[env] python=<path>` naming that interpreter.
- **Per-quest venv (opt-in, `shared_interpreter: false`)** — agent-generated Python is installed and run in `<quest_root>/.venv/`, which sees FI's own packages (`system_site_packages`, on by default) and is built from the interpreter matching `execution.python_version`. On successful quest finish the venv is frozen to `.fi/requirements.lock.txt` (including the FI-provided packages the quest asked for) and then removed to reclaim disk (typically 150–250 MB per quest). The lock file makes the environment reproducible: `python -m venv .venv && .venv/bin/pip install -r .fi/requirements.lock.txt`. Failed or paused quests keep their `.venv/` so you can poke at it. A failed package install is retried once; a timed-out one is not.
- **A stopped run leaves nothing running** — an experiment script that runs past `execution.timeout_s`, or is stopped because the quest is cancelled, is stopped together with every process it started (a subprocess, a `multiprocessing` pool, the real interpreter behind a venv's `python.exe` on Windows), and FI waits until they are gone, so nothing keeps computing, holding files or writing results afterwards. The same holds for a CLI provider call (`claude_cli`, `codex_cli`, ...) that runs past its time limit or is cancelled, and for a provider proxy when it is shut down. On Windows each program runs in its own job (which also ends whatever a finished program left running); on Linux and macOS in its own process group.
- **Only the packages the code uses are installed** — the code-writing step lists the packages it means to use, and FI checks that list against the scripts before installing (every `.py` in `code/`, sub-folders included, and the selected skills' own scripts). A listed package that no script imports is not installed, with one line in `run.log` (`the model listed scikit-image but the code does not import it; not installed`). An optional `try: import x` counts as a use, and so does the package or module name written anywhere in the code (`engine="openpyxl"`, `importlib.import_module("fitz")`). Always installed, whatever the scripts say: a package that is already installed (so `requirements.txt` and the lock file still list it), packages other libraries load by themselves (openpyxl, pyarrow, kaleido, tabulate, scipy, PyWavelets, torch, a Qt binding and a few more), a selected skill's name, and any package whose import name FI cannot tell (not a well-known one, not yet installed), so an unusual package is never left out on a guess. When a script does not parse, the quest's Python cannot be asked what it has, or the last run failed on a missing module, everything listed is installed, and `run.log` says why. A well-known package whose import name differs from its install name (`import skimage` is scikit-image, `cv2` is opencv-python, `PIL` is Pillow, `yaml` is PyYAML, `fitz` is PyMuPDF, ...) is installed under the right name when a script imports it, the list lacks it and the environment does not already have it (except in the Docker sandbox, where FI cannot check what is already installed, and except when a listed package FI cannot read may already be it, such as a git build or pillow-simd), and a list that names the import name (`sklearn`) is installed as the package (`scikit-learn`). The selected skills' own packages are always installed (`core/experiment_deps.py`).
- **A test import after installing** — FI imports the newly installed packages once before the experiment runs (a workaround for a Windows first-run failure). It imports only module names it knows (from the table above, or from what the installed package itself says it provides), never a name guessed from the install name, and a failure there is one plain line in `run.log`, not a traceback; the experiment runs either way.
- **Example files for the experiment** — `execution.inputs: [path, ...]` names files or folders the experiment should start from: a simulation setup, an input deck, a config, a script, a document — **any file type**. They are copied into `<quest>/inputs/examples/` (files dropped there by hand while the quest is paused count too, and a resume never overwrites a copy you edited unless the source itself changed). The design and the code-writing steps are shown their names and the text of the small ones (4,000 characters each, 16,000 in all; a binary file is listed, not shown) and told to combine them with the selected skills into a *new* simulation rather than re-run them; the experiment finds the folder in the `FI_INPUT_DIR` environment variable (an absolute path, read-only by convention). A path that does not exist, or more than 500 MB, stops the quest before its first LLM call. The older `inputs/data/` drop zone (tabular files, read only by the analysis) is unchanged. Web: an *Example files* control on the paused quest's banner (`POST /api/quests/<id>/upload` with `target=examples`); VSCode: the pause message (`NEXT_STEP.md`) names the folder, like the papers and data folders.
- **Background jobs (HPC, a cluster, anything longer than `timeout_s`)** — `execution.background_jobs: true` changes what `experiment.py` is: an idempotent driver that FI runs again and again rather than a script that waits. The first run submits the job and prints `RESULT_JSON: {"fi_job": {"status": "pending", "id": ..., "note": ..., "poll_s": ...}}`; every later run checks it (still running: the same line) and, once it is finished, collects the results and prints the real `RESULT_JSON`; a failed job exits non-zero and is repaired like any failed run. There is one fixed contract, not scheduler code in FI: the selected skill and the example files say how to submit, how to tell finished from failed, and how to read the results, and the prompts carry the contract. On a pending line the quest pauses and exits cleanly (rc 0), writes `.fi/pending.json` and a `NEXT_STEP.md`, and runs no pilot and no replicates (they would submit the job again). `fi --resume <id>` checks once. `fi --watch <id> --config <yaml>` checks on a timer (`--watch-every`, else the job's own `poll_s`, else 300 s; `--watch-max-hours`), prints and logs every check to run.log, and resumes the quest by itself when the script stops saying pending. Web: a *Watch automatically* button on the paused quest (`POST /api/quests/<id>/watch`). Starting the watcher does not mean it is still there a second later (`--watch` exits at once on a quest with no checkpoint), so the page then asks for its real status (`GET /api/quests/<id>/watch`: `state` running / exited / unknown / not_started, `returncode`, `last_line`, the last few lines of `log_tail`, and whether `.fi/pending.json` still exists), every 5 s while the tab is visible. It shows `Watching: <the watcher's last line>` while the process runs; `Watcher stopped (exit code N): <its last log line>` when it has exited, and the button comes back; and, when the web server was restarted after the watcher started and no longer holds its process, that it cannot tell whether it is still running. The exit code is kept in memory after the process is reaped, so another page polling the Jobs list does not erase it. VSCode: `@fi /watch [quest_id]`. Tested against a fake cluster; a real scheduler has not been run, the wait is only as good as the skill's status check, and `--watch` is a process that has to stay running (if it dies, `--resume` or a new `--watch` picks the same job up: nothing is submitted twice).
- **`code/` as a small research tool (the default)** — `execution.code_package: true` (default). For a quest that keeps two scripts, the code-writing step is asked for the model's equations in a package of their own, `code/<package>/` (the name comes from the quest's title): `model.py` holds the equations and nothing else (no grid values, sizes, seeds, thresholds or file paths), each function labelled with its equation (`# E1`). `simulate.py` stays the file FI imports and calls (`run_cell` / `run_trial` / `oracle`), now the scenario around the package, so the trial runner, the oracle gate and `run.py` work as before. FI writes the rest itself: `tests/test_oracles.py` (every check of the plan that has a number to agree with, as a unit test that runs the simulation on the check's case the way FI does and compares with the plan's value and tolerance; `python -m pytest tests` or `python tests/test_oracles.py`) and `METHODS.md` (each equation of the model and the function that carries its label); `run.py` is the sweep and the one command. The plan says what this costs, under *How the code will be laid out*: about 4 more files and a number of extra lines of code (the model's part and FI's part, from the plan's equations and checks). When it would need more than `execution.code_package_max_extra_lines` (default 400) extra lines, the quest keeps two scripts and plan.md and `run.log` say so. A reply that leaves the package out is asked for again (also on a later pass that writes simulate.py again), at most `code_package_max_extra_calls` (default 3) more requests in the whole quest; after that the quest keeps the code it has and says so in `run.log`. A package the reply wrote under a name of its own, and that simulate.py imports, is kept under that name. A quest whose code was written before it decided a layout (one begun before this existed, then resumed or refined) keeps its code as it is, and an extension of code kept as two scripts is not restructured. Before the run FI checks the layout (the package holds a function and `simulate.py` uses it, a unit test exists, `METHODS.md` exists, every `generates` equation is labelled on a function of the package, the package makes no random numbers the per-trial seed cannot reach, `experiment.py` does not import it): a missing part is a warning in `run.log`, and under `rigor_profile: research` the quest stops before the run until it is there (or `execution.code_package: false` is set), and a resume reads the folder again. A plan with no check that has a number to agree with has no unit tests to write, and that is not counted as missing. A changed equation in the package runs the trials again (under the trial contract and the older one alike). Every step that rewrites `simulate.py` sees the package too: a refine that extends the study is shown the package and gives it back unchanged unless the new number needs an equation changed (a package file given back with some of its functions, constants or imports missing is not written: the package is kept), the repair of a crashed simulation and the repair for a failed oracle check are shown the package and can fix an equation there (a fix in the package alone keeps simulate.py as it is), and a missing equation label is asked for in `model.py`, where the equations are. The loop that improves the simulation one step at a time may change the package's modules too (a change there is kept or undone like one in simulate.py). `requirements.txt` lists what the package imports as well. The no-simulation path, a survey, `--analyze` and `split_analysis: false` are unchanged.
- **`code/` is a project you can run on its own** — once the code is written, and again after every run, refine or extension that changed it, FI keeps `README.md` (what it computes, the one command, which file is which), `requirements.txt` (the packages FI actually installed for it, at the exact versions, plus any package the scripts import that the list lacks) and `run.py` in `code/` (`python run.py`: the simulation, then the analysis). `run.py` works in `run_output/` next to `code/` (or `output/` if the folder was copied elsewhere), so running it never touches the quest's own results; it copies the quest's `data/` there and points example inputs at `inputs/examples/`. For a simulation with `run_trial` / `run_cell` FI also writes `study.json`, the settings from the frozen protocol; `run.py` then runs one seed of the study itself, so its numbers can differ slightly from the paper's, which come from FI's several seeds (a setting that fails is reported as failed, as in FI). A cluster quest's simulation is not run by `run.py`. When the study does not use the two-script split, `run.py` does not run `simulate.py`. A refine edits these scripts rather than writing new ones, so the folder stays the one current copy, and it keeps its own history: every change is one commit in a git repository inside `code/` (`git log`; `git checkout <id> -- <file>` brings an old version back) and one entry in `code/CHANGELOG.md` saying what changed and why (a refine's request, a review round, a crash fix, or your own edits). Each entry names its kind of change, decided by the step that made it (never asked of the model): **Added** (the first code, a number a refine asked for), **Changed** (a redesign, a round of the improve loop, an earlier version put back, an edit FI did not make), **Fixed** (a crash fix, a repair by the check against known answers, a number the review found wrong) or **Tidied** (only files FI's own runs never read changed: `README.md`, `requirements.txt`, `run.py`, `study.json`, `METHODS.md`, `tests/test_oracles.py`). Each entry also has one line, "Did the results change": the checks of correctness before and after (from `.fi/criteria_history.jsonl`) and whether the study's results changed, by name only, never their values (a result that differs on every run, such as a run time, shows as changed each time). The run after a change is what measures it, so the line first says "not measured yet" and is filled in when a run of that code finishes with results (a run measuring several changes says so; a change undone before any run says that instead); nothing is guessed (`core/changelog.py`). Before the paper is written FI tries `code/` the way you would (a fresh environment, only `requirements.txt` installed, then `python run.py`; only with `execution.sandbox: venv` and without `background_jobs`) and says in the log and in `needs/CODE_PROJECT_CHECK.json` whether it ran; if it did not, that is only a warning, the results are not affected. The download zip of a quest leaves out `run_output/`. A project file FI keeps (`README.md`, `requirements.txt`, `run.py`, `study.json`) that you edited (or that was there before) is never overwritten (the scripts themselves are rewritten when FI repairs or extends them): FI records the hash of what it last wrote in `.fi/code_project.json` and leaves a differing file alone. When FI would change a file you edited it asks first: an interactive quest pauses with a plain note (the same card in the terminal, the web page and VS Code), an unattended one keeps your version and says so in the log. Delete a file to get FI's version back (`core/code_project.py`).
- **The run's data is kept in `data/results/`** — the scripts run from the quest folder, so a table a script writes lands beside `plan.md`. Once a run is accepted (it exited 0, and the run manifest check did not send it back or stop it, and it is not a background job still pending), FI copies every data table the run wrote there (`.csv`, `.tsv`, `.json`, `.npy`, `.npz`, `.parquet`, `.feather`, `.pkl`, `.h5`, `.xlsx`, `.txt`, `.dat`, ...) into `data/results/`, and for a two-script quest the whole `raw/` folder into `data/results/raw/` (replaced, not merged, and refreshed after the replicate seeds ran). The originals stay where the scripts and FI's records expect them. A file over 200 MB, or a `raw/` folder over 200 MB in all, is not copied (for `raw/`, the log says so); a secret or setup file (`.env`, `*.key`, `token.json`, `credentials*.json`, `requirements*.txt`, ...) is never copied; tables an earlier iteration left there are removed. A crashed, rejected or stopped run copies nothing. `data/results/` is not counted as data the quest was given; a layout-only refine redraws figures from it (`Engine._save_run_data`).
- **The simulation and its analysis as two scripts** — `execution.split_analysis: auto` (the default; `true` and `false` decide for every quest). `auto` turns it on for every quest that runs a simulation of its own, deterministic or random, so the simulation is always a function FI can call itself: `run_cell(cell)` for a deterministic study (called once per setting, no seed, no repeats and no interval machinery), `run_trial(cell, trial_id, seed)` for one with randomness. That is what lets FI run each oracle's `case` itself, so a deterministic quest can reach `independently_validated` too. A cluster job is treated like any other run. It is off for a study with no experiment (the no-simulation path), a literature survey and an analysis-only run. `split_analysis: false` goes back to one script and gives that up: the oracles are then answered by the script's own `FI_ORACLE=1` run, which never counts toward `independently_validated`. To see which a quest got, look for `code/simulate.py`: once the code is written, it exists for a two-script quest and not for a one-script quest (if the model does not return both scripts under `split_failure: warn`, the quest falls back to one). A stochastic study keeps the raw outcome of every run on disk, which is what an interval computed from the runs themselves, rather than from a few batch summaries, needs. The code-writing step is also asked to mark, in the simulation, where it implements each equation of the plan's model whose role is `generates` (a comment carrying the equation's id, `# E1`, on or above the code that computes it); when the reply leaves one out, FI asks the model once more to add the comments and keeps its answer only if the code is otherwise unchanged (the same syntax tree). Before every run, and again after an oracle repair rewrote the simulation, FI reads the comments and function docstrings of the simulation script and its helper modules in `code/` for each such id (`core/oracle_check.py::unlabelled_equations`, any case, a whole id or a range such as `# E1-E3`; the module docstring, names and strings in the code do not count). A missing one is a warning in `run.log` and a gap below `independently_validated` (the oracles test the code against the model, and an oracle that rests on E1 checks the code the simulation says is E1); under `rigor_profile: research` the quest stops before the run (`equation_labels` pause) until the label is there, and a resume reads the script on disk again. Only the label is read, not the mathematics. One script that simulates *and* analyses is repaired by writing it again and running it again, so a plotting mistake, a wrong interval or a mislabelled table costs the whole simulation a second time, which for a simulation that takes hours is a lost day. With the switch on, the code-writing step returns two scripts on the trial contract (see *FI runs the trials and keeps the record* below: `simulate.py` defines `run_trial`, FI runs every trial and gives the analysis its per-setting results through `FI_TRIALS`). A simulation that still runs its own loop (the older contract) is handled as follows: `code/simulate.py` runs the simulation and saves everything the analysis needs as files in the folder named by `FI_RAW_DIR` (a path relative to the quest folder: `raw/seed<K>/`, one folder per replicate seed), and `code/experiment.py` — the name every other part of FI reads — is the analysis: it reads only that folder, computes the statistics, draws the figures and prints `RESULT_JSON`. Each replicate seed is its own pair, simulation then analysis, so FI's replicates, confidence intervals, per-seed figure records and mean figures work exactly as for one script. Which raw files are still good is decided by the files, not by anyone's judgement: each seed's folder holds a `manifest.json` with the SHA-256 of the `simulate.py` that wrote it, the seed, and every file with its size (and its SHA-256 up to 256 MB). The simulation runs again exactly when that record does not fit: its script was rewritten, the seed differs, a file is missing or a different size, or nothing was written yet; otherwise only the analysis runs. So a crash in the analysis is repaired by rewriting `experiment.py` alone (the repair is shown what is in the raw folder) and run against the same files; a crash in the simulation rewrites `simulate.py` alone (a failure names which of the two it is in); a simulation that exits 0 but saves nothing fails as the simulation; and when a review names something the run computed, the code-writing step is told to give `simulate.py` back exactly as it is unless the problem is in the simulation, and a script given back with other line endings or trailing spaces counts as unchanged, so its raw files stay in use. The seed checks read the simulation (where the seed belongs), the topic's settings and run counts are checked against both scripts, and the critique reads both. `execution.raw_dir` puts the raw files elsewhere (relative to the quest folder, or an absolute path on a big disk or an HPC scratch area; FI records the path and each file's size and hash, and copies nothing); with `sandbox: docker` it must be relative, because the container sees only the quest folder. Limits: there is no pilot pass for two scripts; a reply that does not hold both scripts is asked for once more and otherwise the quest runs as one script and says so in the log, unless `execution.split_failure: block` (which `rigor_profile: research` sets) stops the quest instead; with `background_jobs` the study runs its trials on the cluster as a job array (below); a wrong result whose cause is in the simulation is fixed by the review's re-run rather than by the repair of the analysis; and each script gets the full `timeout_s`, so a simulation longer than that still needs `background_jobs`. FI removes a seed's raw folder only to write it again, when that seed's simulation runs again; otherwise a long simulation's files stay on disk.
- **Docker sandbox** — `execution.sandbox: docker` runs the experiment subprocess with network disabled, the quest mounted at `/work`, and the approved external skills the quest selected mounted read-only at `/fi-skills/<name>` (see *Skills from other agents* below). Each container is capped at `execution.docker_memory_gb` (default 4, swap included where Docker can limit it), `execution.docker_cpus` (default 2, lowered to what Docker has; numerical libraries get `OMP_NUM_THREADS` and the like set to it unless already set lower) and `execution.docker_max_processes` (default 1024 processes and threads), has every Linux capability dropped and `no-new-privileges` set, and runs as a non-root user (the quest folder's owner on Linux, a fixed id under Docker Desktop on Windows/macOS) once a write check in the quest folder passes; under rootless Docker, or when the check fails (Docker Desktop for Linux), it runs as the container's root, which `run.log` says. A limit this Docker cannot apply is left out and `run.log` says so. The host's system and Python paths (`PATH`, `HOME`, `PYTHONHOME`, `LD_LIBRARY_PATH` and the like) are not passed in, and quest-folder paths in the environment and the command are given as `/work/...`. Root-owned files or folders left in a quest by older runs are named in `run.log` with the `chown` that fixes them. A run stopped by the memory cap, or failing on the process cap, ends with a plain `[FI]` line naming the setting to raise, in `run.log` and in the stderr the repair step reads.
- **Provider matrix** — direct HTTP, proxy, CLI exec, and VSCode-extension transports (see provider matrix below).
- **Per-node model routing** via `provider.node_models`. FI never chooses the model; the interview's "Per-node model overrides" field takes `node:model` pairs on every surface, and in VSCode it is a menu of the models that VSCode offers with one entry for the light nodes. Five nodes (`cross_check`, `select_skills`, `literature_screen`, `slides`, `poster`) were measured on a cheaper model (one simulation topic, three runs per arm: about 15% fewer tokens, no drop in paper, slide or poster scores seen, which three runs can show only above roughly ten points); every other node is untested (see `docs/PROVIDERS.md`).
- **Reasoning effort** via `provider.reasoning_effort` (`minimal` / `low` / `medium` / `high` / `xhigh` / `max`; unset sends nothing, so each provider keeps its own default). Sent as `reasoning_effort` in the chat-completions body to HTTP providers, `--effort` to `claude_cli` and `antigravity_cli`, and `-c model_reasoning_effort=...` to `codex_cli`. A level a provider cannot take — Ollama and `agy` beyond low/medium/high, `claude_cli` at `minimal`, or a provider with no such setting (`copilot_cli`, `gemini_cli`, `vscode_extension`, the proxies) — is left out with one warning in the quest log rather than failing the call; an unknown value fails at config load. Set from the interview's advanced screen on the CLI, web and VSCode. See `docs/PROVIDERS.md`, "Reasoning effort".
- **Pre-flight `clarify` node** — slot survey before `ideate`; off / auto / interactive modes. Unset (the default), it asks when someone can answer while it runs (terminal `--interactive`, VS Code for a quest on `provider.name: vscode_extension`, the web page) and answers for itself when nobody can (a `--fleet` run never waits; if nobody answers within `pauses.timeout_s`, or the VS Code prompt is dismissed, the defaults are used; a VS Code quest whose YAML names another provider is not on the bridge, so it answers for itself, or with `ask` stops for `.fi/clarify_answer.json`). With `ask` and nobody to ask, the quest stops (`.fi/clarify_questions.json` holds the questions; write the answers as a JSON object to `.fi/clarify_answer.json` and resume, or answer on the web quest page). On the web page the questions appear on the quest page as a form, whether the web server runs the quest itself (`POST /api/quests/start`) or starts it as its own process (the interview's launch, the quest page's **Resume**): such a process writes them to `.fi/clarify_questions.json` (and its process id to `.fi/clarify_waiting.json` while it waits, so the page knows even after the server restarted), its status reads *Waiting for your answers*, and the page writes the answers to `.fi/clarify_answer.json`, which the waiting run reads without being started again (a Resume while it waits is refused). While it waits it still counts as a running quest against the server's `--max-concurrent`, and `pauses.timeout_s: 0` waits with no limit. If nobody answers in time it is the same as above: unset, the defaults are used; `ask`, the quest stops and waits, and answering on the page then resumes it. The interview leaves the key unset unless the person picks a mode (its default choice, *Ask me if I'm there, otherwise answer for itself*, writes nothing; `auto`, `interactive` and `off` are written as chosen, and `--update` keeps an unset key unset). The model writes the questions once: they are kept in `.fi/clarify_asked.json` until the quest finishes, so answering them (in the same run or after a resume) does not ask the model again, and a title picked by number is one of the titles you were shown. The first slots ask what you want to see at the end and what the study should be called (three suggested titles, which the terminal and VS Code read in the question's text and the web form offers as buttons that fill the answer box; the one you pick or type becomes the quest's title and the writer is told to use it; a `title:` in the YAML wins, and the interview always writes one, so a quest the interview wrote is not asked for a title). The slot set includes ``topic_shape`` (``experimental`` / ``survey`` / ``review`` / ``case_study`` / ``opinion``) so the engine recognises when a topic naturally wants a literature synthesis instead of a code experiment — ``survey`` (a "history of X" / "evolution of X" / overview topic) takes the no-experiment, no-dataset survey path. **Agent judgment beats the format heuristic.** Most `clarify_overrides` pinned by the interview are user *preferences* (study depth, venue, output kinds, baseline, metric, budget) and rightly win over the agent's self-answers — but ``simulatability`` and ``topic_shape`` are topic *judgments*. When the agent runs in `auto` mode and its judgment conflicts with the interview's pinned value (which is often just a paper-format heuristic), the **agent's value wins back** for those two slots, so a review-shaped topic the agent flags as no-simulation isn't force-run as a toy experiment by a stale `simulatability: "yes"` override. The hard `engine.no_simulation: true` YAML pin is separate and still absolute; there is intentionally no hard "force simulate" — the engine trusts the agent's "can a simulation actually answer this?" call. When the shape is non-experimental but simulatability still resolves to SIMULATE (the agent itself judged it simulatable), the engine logs a WARNING in run.log and the design stage falls back to a narrow illustrative experiment with weight shifted to literature synthesis (rather than a full comparative benchmark).
- **Execute-repair loop** — agent reads traceback, patches code, retries (`engine.exec_reflect_max_iterations`). Every patch is run, including the one written with the last attempt, so a paper is never written from an earlier run; a script a redesign wrote starts with a fresh budget (the old script's spent repairs used to carry over, so a redesigned script's first crash got none) next to code that never ran. A result the last attempt could not bring inside its declared bounds is the one written up, so it still gets its replicate seeds and error bars. A run that worked but drew a legend over its data is also sent back, once (see *Branded figures*).
- **Stale-figure purge on re-execute** — every `execute` clears `figures/` before running the experiment. On a `re_experiment` / `broaden` / repair loop the `implement` node rewrites `experiment.py`, which may emit a *different* set of figure filenames; without the purge the previous version's plots linger (they're not overwritten), get counted in `figures=N`, and pollute the paper/slides/poster with a mix of current and stale figures. Only figure-suffixed files are removed — `results.csv` and other artifacts survive.
- **Every figure the run drew reaches the paper** — the write prompt tells the writer to include each figure it is given, and a draft that ignores it used to be caught only by the reviewer, which forces a rewrite that spends one of `engine.max_iterations` — and on the last iteration there is none left to spend, so the paper shipped discussing "Figure 1" through "Figure 3" with no figure in it while the images sat in `figures/`. Which figures are missing is a set difference (planned by the design ∩ drawn by the run ∩ not embedded in the draft), not a reading of prose, so the write node puts them back itself the moment the draft is written — before the review, for no LLM call and no iteration. Each figure is placed after the paragraph whose prose already names its number, which is where the writer would have put it, and before the source lists when the prose names no number; it keeps its place in the design's plan as its figure number, or the next free number when a figure the draft did embed already carries that one. The caption comes from the figure's own record, written from the panel titles and never from the series labels, so an engine-written caption cannot name a series its figure draws flat or not at all (which is itself a forced hit); a figure drawn as the mean of the seeds, or from seed 0 alone, says so in its caption. A figure a later experiment version superseded is never forced back: the stale-figure purge above means `figures/` holds only what this run drew. The reviewer's `figure_missing` check is unchanged and stays as the second line of defence.
- **Numeric oracle** — a deterministic, non-LLM comparison of the numbers in `paper.md` against the ones the run actually produced. Every other correctness gate ends in a language model reading text — `claim_check` asks one call whether a number "appears" in the evidence, with no arithmetic anywhere — so a mis-transcription (2.14 computed, 2.41 written) passes all of them. The oracle looks for the *signature of a copy that went wrong*, in two forms. A **digit transposition** — the same digits in another order — is almost never a coincidence. A **last-digit slip** (`near_miss`) is a number printed with at least two decimals that is one unit of its own last digit from the correct rounding of a result: 2.12 for a stored 2.1259, which rounds to 2.13, or 0.184 for 0.18346, which rounds to 0.183 — the truncation or the mistyped last digit that stored quests really contain. It used to be any number within a quarter of a result; a result dictionary holds hundreds of numbers, so that reported nearly every number a paper prints, and almost none of the findings was a copy error. Numbers far from every result are assumed to come from the design or the literature and are ignored, as are figure/table/section references, code blocks and low-precision quotes; results tables are deliberately checked. Three more kinds of number that sit near a result without being one are left out too. **A confidence level** — the `95` in `95% CI`, `95% Wilson interval` or `Wilson 95% intervals`, which lands within the comparison band of any result between 72 and 126 and was the most frequent false finding; only a `%` followed by interval wording counts, so `93% of runs` and `94% of the intervals covered the truth` are results and are still checked. **A setting the run was given** — the topic's grid and the numbers in the design's `variables` and `method` (`R0 = 1.5` printed beside an outcome of 1.33, a 13.5 nm wavelength, `gamma = 1.0`; a percentage there also counts as the fraction a paper prints for it). The design's `hypothesis` and `expected_outcome` are deliberately *not* settings: they hold what the author predicted a result would be, and a paper that prints the prediction where the measured value belongs is the mistake this check exists for. A digit transposition is still reported for a declared number, and the blind spot is accepted: a result that really equals a declared setting and is misquoted as it goes unreported. **A number cut off from a longer one** — `97.5th` is a percentile rank, not the `97` the extractor used to back off to. A minus typeset as `−` (U+2212) now keeps its sign, so `−0.114` is compared as a negative number rather than as 0.114 against an unrelated positive result. Replayed over the 153 stored quests that kept a paper and a checkpoint, the rules above took the `near_miss` findings from 311 to 207 (16 confidence levels, 82 declared settings, 5 dropped signs, 1 percentile rank) and added none, and what remained was mostly a paper number landing near an unrelated result in a dense result set — a table cell, an interval bound, a quoted theoretical value, a difference the paper computed. So `near_miss` is the last-digit slip above, and six more rules keep the rest out. A number inside a citation bracket (`[6, 15]`) is a reference number. A result named like an identifier or a count of files (`id`, `index`, `seed`, `year`, `n_files`) is not a measurement. A result the paper also prints correctly rounded, to two decimals or more, is stated right, so a number one digit off beside it is another quantity: a sibling, a bound, a sum worked by hand. A repeating-decimal constant printed with three decimals or more (0.333, 0.667, 0.143) is a theoretical value more often than a measurement. A result that is only a shorter rounding of the number printed (0.667 against a stored 0.67) is read against that result alone, so an unrelated stored 0.2 clears nothing. And a declared setting, as above. Replayed over the 136 stored quests with results, that takes the `near_miss` findings from 197 in 59 quests (37 in the worst) to 20 in 9 (6 in the worst), and the four last-digit slips found by reading the findings against their results (a MEEF printed 2.12 for a stored 2.1259 and 2.00 for 2.0053, a NILS of 0.184 for 0.18346 — one point of a nine-point sweep — and an AUC of 0.9120 for 0.91205) are all still reported. Read by hand, about a third of the 20 are last-digit slips of the quantity the paper means; the rest are another quantity sitting one digit from a result, which nothing about the number's own surroundings tells from a slip. A hit is reported under the name of the signal that fired — `transposed` when the paper's digits are the result's in another order, `near_miss` when the last digit is one off the correct rounding — naming the value and the `result_json` path, in the log, in `paper/numeric_audit.json`, and in the human-review panel of the web UI and the VSCode chat, under both the single reviewer and a `review_panel`. Each finding is labelled by its own kind rather than by one verdict stretched over all of them, because the three are not the same claim and a label that argues with the sentence under it makes the reader decide which half to believe. It does not force a rewrite: the check is a regex over prose, and a forced revise cycle on a false positive costs more than a flagged number, so the verdict stays with the reviewer. A third signal does not read the paper at all: a **reference value that is exactly 0 at every setting of a sweep** is reported as a `trivial_reference` finding naming the leaf and the settings it covers — and it says so under that name rather than being called an unverified number, because that value was computed and exported exactly as the code asked; what is unverified is the bracket the root finder was handed. A closed-form reference — the deterministic limit, the theoretical prediction, the analytic solution — is generated code, and it fails in one characteristic way: a root finder bracketed so the useless root sits on the bracket endpoint (`brentq(f, 0, 1)` where `f(1) == 0`, or a bracket of `[1e-10, 1.0]`) returns the trivial root, so the reference comes out as 0 at every point and the paper then draws that flat line of zeros in a figure captioned as convergence to the deterministic limit. Nothing else catches it: the range gate only checks quantities the design declared a bound for, a design that names `final_size` does not cover a leaf called `deterministic_final_size`, and 0 is inside `[0, 1]` in any case. Only an identically-*zero* reference is reported, deliberately — a correctly computed deterministic final size does not depend on the population size, so a legitimately flat reference line across a sweep over N is exactly what a convergence study plots, and flagging "identical" in general would fire on correct work. Values a run carries as settings rather than results (tolerances, horizons, seeds, step counts) are excluded by name, since a tolerance of 0.0 repeated at every point is configuration rather than a computation; that exclusion is a name list, so a setting named outside it is not recognised. The finding needs at least three settings and some other quantity that does vary — a result in which nothing varies at all belongs to the degenerate-run guard. Like the oracle's other signals it is advisory, so it reaches the audit file and the human-review panel rather than forcing a rewrite. `paper/numeric_audit.json` is written on every run, clean or not, so silence never has to be read as "the check was skipped".

- **Topic coverage (advisory)** — a topic that names its settings often gets an experiment that changed them: on the graded SIR runs one drew its grid at R0 = {0.8, 1.2, 2.0, 3.0} for a topic that asked for {0.9, 1.5, 3.0}, and another drew five figures for a topic that asked for three, and no later step compared the experiment with the topic. After each draft the review reads the topic for the numbers that are settings (the members of a set written in braces with at least two numbers, `R0 in {0.9, 1.5, 3.0}`, and a count of runs, `300 stochastic runs`; a number in running prose such as a wavelength, a threshold or a page limit is left alone, and so is an integer below 10, which every script holds) and for the number of figures it asks for (`three figures`, `at least three figures`, `no more than 2 figures`), and compares them with `code/experiment.py` (its numeric literals and the values its `linspace`, `arange`, `logspace`, `geomspace` and `range` calls with literal arguments generate), the keys of `result_json` (`R0_1.5_N_1000`) and the figures the run drew. A number given with a unit or a percent sign counts at any power of ten, since a script works in metres or in fractions (193 nm is 1.93e-7). What differs is one line each in the review's `goal_coverage_notes`, in `run.log` as `[goal_coverage]`, in `paper/goal_coverage.json` (written on every run with what the check looked at, clean or not), and in the human-review panel of all three interfaces (the CLI gate, the web quest page and the VSCode review message) under "Topic coverage (advisory, not blocking)". It is never a must-fix hit and never sends the experiment back: a topic is prose, and a wrong note would cost a whole cycle. Skipped for a survey or no-simulation study, a run on the user's own data (`--analyze`) and a run with no results (a failed run, which other checks report). Measured over the 122 stored quests that have a script and results, 10 (8%) get a note: three for numbers (the {0.8, 1.2, 2.0, 3.0} grid with N = 5000 dropped; the same grid in one graded run; a percolation run that swapped the lattice size 256 for 96) and seven for a figure count (three asked; two, four, four, four, four, five and six drawn); read against the scripts and the topics, each is a real difference between the topic and the run, and it finds both differences the grading recorded (the changed R0 grid and the five figures). What it does not do: a setting written in prose ("between 0.5 and 3") or in square brackets is not read, a value the script computes rather than writes is not seen, and whether a difference matters is left to the reader.
- **Statistics claim check** — the numeric oracle asks whether a number *matches* a result and `claim_check` asks a model whether a sentence is *supported*; neither asks what a number **is**. That gap ships papers whose numbers are all correct and whose sentences are not: a Bonferroni threshold printed as a p-value, "Cohen's *d* > 16 for all pairwise comparisons" when a third of the run's comparisons fall below it, three-seed t-intervals labelled "exact binomial 95% CI", a figure's y-axis limits printed as modal bin counts, and a major-outbreak probability described as the chance the disease dies out — which the numeric oracle passes, because the number really is one the run computed. This check reads what a number is *said to be* and recomputes the claim against the run's own replicates. Five things it can prove: an **effect-size claim carrying a universal quantifier** ("for *all* pairwise comparisons") is re-derived from every pairwise Cohen's *d* the replicates support — uncapped, unlike the analyze aggregate, whose limit of 24 hid 12 of one quest's 36; a number written as a **p-value** that equals this run's `0.05/n` when the run computed no p-value at all, matched to one unit in the paper's own last decimal because `0.05/36 = 0.0013888…` gets *truncated* to `0.001388` and a rounding comparison would miss it; an interval labelled with a **named estimator** (exact binomial, Clopper-Pearson, Wilson, bootstrap, …) whose endpoints reproduce FI's own t-interval over the seeds — proving the label wrong needs no second estimator, only that the printed interval *is* the seed-to-seed one; a number presented as a **count of runs** that equals a recorded figure's y-axis upper limit and appears nowhere in the results (matplotlib leaves 5% headroom above the tallest bar, so an axis limit reads exactly like a bin count); and a **probability of extinction** whose value matches a result named for the opposite event and none named for extinction, reported with its complement. **False positives are the failure mode that matters**, so each check fires only when it can name the contradiction, and the guards are as load-bearing as the checks: a bracket holding only digits is a citation and never a measurement, a count must sit within 40 characters of the noun it counts, a number the run computed anywhere is assumed to be that result, an effect size that survives rounding to the paper's own precision is not a contradiction, a threshold the paper *calls* a threshold is correct, and "the outbreak probability vanished as N increased" is a correct sentence about a probability going to zero rather than a disease that vanishes. Measured over the graded SIR papers, it flags all five known-defective runs — naming 17 of 36 effect sizes below the claimed bound, the `0.05/36` threshold, six mislabelled intervals as one finding, three axis limits, and the reversed probability with its complement — and **zero of the 35 others**. A hit is a forced `mislabelled_statistic` must-flag under both the single reviewer and a `review_panel`, listed in `_TEXT_ONLY_HITS`: the experiment computed the right numbers and the paper described them wrongly, so the route is `rewrite` (back to `write` alone, with the finding in front of the writer) and a mislabel never sends the experiment back to be run again. `paper/statistics_audit.json` is written on every run, clean or not.

- **Number provenance** — the numeric oracle asks whether a printed number is a slip of a result (its digits swapped, or its last digit one off) without matching it, and deliberately ignores one that is far from every result ("assumed to come from somewhere else"); the statistics claim check asks what a number **is**. Neither asks **where a number came from at all**, and a graded paper shipped straight through the gap: its Table 1 printed eight cells — `0.7093 (0.6934–0.7252)`, `0.2974 (0.2721–0.3227)` and their neighbours — matching no value the run computed, no per-seed replicate and none of the three-seed aggregates. The oracle, in its earlier and wider form, saw those numbers as 38 advisory near-misses that blocked nothing; the paper shipped, and the run failed its "numbers are correct" gate on exactly those cells. This check asks every number in the paper body to trace to something the run can account for, reports the ones that trace to nothing, and **blocks** — the difference that matters, since the finding routes to `rewrite` alone. **What makes it possible is that a paper does not print `RESULT_JSON`:** it prints the *mean over the replicate seeds* and that mean's interval, and no single seed holds either — checked against `RESULT_JSON` alone, all eleven of that same paper's *correct* headline numbers come out untraceable too, so the naive form of this check flags correct prose. With the across-seed aggregates included the two classes separate cleanly: 11 of 11 accepted numbers trace, and 11 of 13 of the ones the paper was failed over trace nowhere — the other two are values the run really did compute, printed against the wrong cell, which is a mislabel rather than a number from nowhere and is the statistics claim check's question, not this one's. A number may trace to the results or any replicate; to the aggregates over the seeds (mean, 95% bounds, SD, standard error, min/max); to the length of an array the results hold, so "300 runs" traces to the 300 entries behind it; to a stratum label read off a result path (`by_R0.1.5`); to a recorded figure's own numbers, **axis limits included**, so an axis limit printed as a bin count is left to the statistics check that can name the contradiction instead of being reported twice; to a value the engine's **oracle pre-check** measured (`needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json`); to a number the run **configuration** or the **design** declared; or to arithmetic the paper writes out in full. Rounding *and* truncation to the paper's own precision both count, as do a scale change (`0.15` printed as `15%`) and a complement (`1 − p`). A power of ten is read as a reader reads it, whether written `1.25 × 10^{-5}`, `1.25×10^-5` or with superscripts (`1.25×10⁻⁵`, which was once read as 1.25 and flagged). **The guards are the check.** The load-bearing one is precision: a number carrying fewer than three significant digits — or two without four decimals — is a round number, a convention or a stratum label ("we ran 3 seeds", "R0 = 1.5", "95% CI", "the 0.05 level"), and is never asked to trace anywhere, which removes that entire class without a single hand-written rule about rhetorical numbers. Years and dotted version numbers are not measurements; a random seed is chosen and never computed; reference lists and citation brackets are stripped before a number is read; and a range written `0.3126--0.3548` is rewritten before tokenizing, because a number reader takes the dash as a minus sign and invents `-0.3548` — that one artifact produced the majority of all flags across six runs whose numbers were perfectly fine. Five more readings of that kind are corrected the same way, before the paper is tokenized: a minus typeset as U+2212 is one number with its sign and is compared with its sign (`−38.61` is no longer checked as `38.61`, and a bare `38.61` is still not `−38.61`); a dash right after a percent sign ends a range (`68.1%-85.5%`) instead of signing its upper bound; a number after `±` stands for both signs; a comma run whose later group is not three digits (`n=107,104,94`) is a list rather than `107104`, and so is a run of three-digit groups (`N in 100,250,500`) that traces to nothing as one number when every group is a value the run holds; and an integer of four or more digits after a capitalised acronym (`SPIE 11609`), or a year credited in the possessive (`Abbe's 1873`), is an identifier or a date rather than a result. An en or em dash is never read as a minus, and nothing in this paragraph is a derived-value allowance — a number the run never produced, beside a sign, inside a list or after an acronym, is still flagged. Over the 136 stored papers with recorded results, 13 of the 128 flags were these readings and are gone with none added, and every number of the fabricated table this check was built for is still flagged; the artifacts that remain (a value printed without the sign the run holds, and a well-formed `202,206,189` that is really a list) cannot be told from a real number by their text, and the check does not try. Where the paper shows no operator, as in a table column that is a difference, the operands must themselves be values the run computed, so three numbers standing near each other can never explain away a fourth. A run whose last `RESULT_JSON` holds no numbers at all goes **quiet** rather than flagging the whole paper. Measured over the 40 stored SIR runs and the 1,422 paper numbers in them, it flags **two runs, both of which failed the "numbers are correct" gate, and none of the other 38 — the eleven that passed that gate, the ten that failed it for other reasons, and the seventeen graded before it existed**. Two blind spots are accepted deliberately and are the price of that: a two-significant-digit textbook value quoted as the run's own result sits below the precision floor, and a number the design declared but the experiment never computed is treated as accounted for. A hit is a forced `unsourced_number` must-flag under both the single reviewer and a `review_panel`, listed in `_TEXT_ONLY_HITS`: the experiment ran and recorded its results, so the route is `rewrite` (back to `write` alone, with the finding in front of the writer) and an unsourced number never sends the experiment back to be run again. `paper/provenance_audit.json` is written on every run, clean or not.

- **Physics-plausibility gate** — the design declares what its outputs may legally be (`result_assertions`: a path, a min/max, a unit and the reason) and those bounds are enforced **in code** against `result_json`. This is the gap the degenerate-run guard cannot cover: that one fires only when every metric is ~0, so it catches a run that produced nothing and misses a run that produced the wrong thing convincingly — a unit error, a factor of two, a sign flip. A violation routes back through the execute-repair loop before any paper is written, and the repair is told which bound broke. A value sitting exactly on a non-zero bound is rejected too when the script itself caps values at that constant (`min(x, 10.0)`, `np.clip`, `if x > 1e6: x = 10.0`): a cap turns divergence into an in-range number the range check could not otherwise tell from a real one. Both halves are required, so a genuine perfect score is left alone. Every cap in the script counts for a value on a bound (so a softmax clipped to 1.0 still makes an accuracy of exactly 1.0 look capped: which variable a cap acts on is not traced), except a constant every cap of which is proven to be another value's: written in the printed result at another key's whole path, so a cap on `train.rmse` does not reject a `test.rmse` of exactly its declared maximum (`caps_of_other_values`); a cap whose place cannot be shown (a branch, a loop, an array capped before it is averaged, a cap on an enclosing key, a dict spread in with `**`, a value carried by `:=`, a result that is also read out of the dict it is printed in, copied, or written and read back) still counts; a name reached only through `globals()` or `eval` is not followed. A value exactly on an upper cap written right where that very value is reported (`{"rmse": min(rmse, 10.0)}` or `min(10.0, rmse)`, the upper bound of a clip or clamp given as its third argument or a `max`-style keyword, whatever the module is called; two positional arguments are read as no cap, since `x.clip(lo, HI)` and `torch.clamp(x, lo)` look alike) is rejected even inside the range (`plausibility.direct_caps`). Only a cap proven to be that value's counts: it sits in the result the script prints at the value's whole path (followed from each argument of `print` / `json.dumps`: a literal written there, a name bound to one in the same function or at module level, a literal a function of the script returns, and a name or such a call used as a value inside a printed literal, placed under that value's key), a key the script computes (an f-string, a variable, a comprehension's key) matching any one key and a list or comprehension entry any position; so a cap on `train.rmse` is not one on `test.rmse`. A cap that may be the value's but whose place in the result cannot be shown (a record built in a loop or appended, a result wrapped in a call the check cannot follow, a literal never printed) whose key chain matches the end of the value's path is a warning in `run.log` and the trace (`plausibility_unproven_cap`), never a stop. A floor (`max(1, n)`), a reduction with an axis (`x.max(1)`), a guard in a denominator, a value set inside an `if`, and a lone top-level key many quantities share (`mean`, `max`) are not taken for such a cap, and are left to the rule above. Not traced: which branch of a condition inside a printed literal applies to which key (both count), and a result filled in by assigning into an existing dict (`out["test"]["rmse"] = ...`). A cap inside a library or a skill's code is not seen. The same quantity exactly on one of its bounds in two or more settings goes back too, zero bounds included. On a bound of 0 only an exact 0 counts: a tiny non-zero value (a Wilcoxon p of 8.5e-28, say) was computed from the data and is not the trivial answer, though a value a hair below 0 still counts as on the minimum rather than under it. A count of failures that sits at 0 everywhere is the answer, not a trivial one, when the protocol's `failure_policy` names it and the run's own manifest lists no failed trial. A bounded quantity an earlier run reported that a later result leaves out altogether (renamed or deleted, not set to `null` with a reason) goes back too, asking for it under its name; a name the design and the script disagreed on from the first run is left alone, since nothing says which key was meant. A computation that returns a trivial answer (a root finder settling on the solution at the starting state, a sentinel) lands on the edge of the legal range, where a range check passes it: a real quest's deterministic model reported a final size of 0.0 at two reproduction numbers and 1.0 at a third, all inside [0, 1]. The repair is told to change the code only if that is the cause, and a single value on a bound is left alone. **A result equal to its bound is on that bound, not past it.** A bound is a number with a precision: a design that worked its maximum out as 2/3 declared 0.6666666666666666, the script that computed `1 - 1/R0` produced 0.6666666666666667, and an exact `>` called the run's correct answer a violation — the log read `branching_probability = 0.666667 violates [0, 0.666667]`, the same number on both sides of the word. The repair believed it, emitted `null` for every boundary value with a flag reading `exact_theoretical_boundary` — it knew the numbers were right — and the figure drawn to show that reference lost its line in two of three panels. The comparison the at-bound and clamped rules already used now decides the range test too, so a result can no longer be too large by an amount no measurement can resolve. **A bound the design derived is attainable.** "Exactly on a bound" is evidence about the computation only when the bound is a number a fault can return by accident: 0 and 1 — nothing and everything, which is what a saturating probability, a normalisation that divides a quantity by itself, an empty count and a guard branch all produce — or a constant the script itself writes down, which is where a bracket endpoint (`brentq(f, 0, 100)`), a sentinel and a cap come from. A bound the design derived for this experiment is neither: nothing returns 0.6666666666666667 by accident, so a result equal to it is the design's own prediction, arriving where the design said it would, and it is left alone — unless the quantity sits on that bound at *every* setting the assertion matched, which is a quantity that never varied with the sweep at all (a loop that captured one cell's value for all of them looks exactly like this) and is the trivial answer whatever the bound. **A correct zero is not sent back.** Below the epidemic threshold nothing takes off, so zero of 300 trajectories crossing a threshold at R0 = 0.9 is the answer rather than a fault, and a model told that a correct number is wrong will hide it rather than defend it — one run lost its replicates that way, one returned `null` and crashed its own plot, one spent a repair round and a skipped replication pass. Three things now separate a correct zero from a trivial one, all decided from the results themselves. **A setting is counted once**: a sweep reported under two groupings — once by R0 and once by N — lists the same cell twice, so a single zero looked like the two settings the rule needs; settings are identified by the coordinates in their path rather than by the path string, so the same cell reached by a different route is one setting, while two different cells whose paths happen to hold the same segments stay separate. **A zero below the critical point is expected**: where a path names the reproduction number, or an enclosing object carries it as a field, or a grouping key leads with it and packs its value alongside another coordinate, or a stratum label spells it with an `=` and packs the whole cell into one key (`strata.R0=0.9,N=100`, which leaves no numeric segment for the positional rule either), a zero at R0 < 1 is what the theory predicts. That excuses a zero only — a *one* below threshold is excused by nothing, and a run reporting a final size of 1.0 at R0 = 0.9 still goes back. **Zeros confined to one level of one coordinate are a regime**: where no name identifies the control parameter, the shape still does, so zeros that all sit at the same value of one sweep coordinate, in a minority of the sweep, with the quantity non-zero at the other levels, are read as a threshold effect — and a level explains them only when some setting outside it behaves differently. A quantity that is zero at *every* setting gets none of these excuses — that is the trivial answer the gate exists to catch. What still fails is reported together with the zeros that were recognised and the reason each was accepted, so a repair is told what has already been accounted for instead of being asked to change it. Values of exactly zero are checked against every bound, and the design is asked for the tightest range its guarantee allows (a small positive `min` for a quantity that cannot be 0). A value that really is out of range should be reported as `null` with a flag saying why, and the implement and repair prompts say so. Assertions state what physics or the definition of the quantity guarantees, never what the design expects to happen; bounding a result to the hypothesis would make the run unable to disagree with it. An absent assertion is not checked — silence means "nothing was claimed", never "everything is fine".
- **Simulation skills** — `engine.skills` lets a quest call tested code instead of re-deriving physics. A skill is a directory carrying instructions (`SKILL.md`), optionally an API surface and executable entry points, an executable `selftest.py`, and provenance including its own range assertions. The envelope follows the [Agent Skills open standard](https://agentskills.io/), so a skill written for another agent works here unchanged: bundled `scripts/`, `references/` and `assets/` directories are read alongside FI's own single-file `api_surface.md` and `skill.py`. Their *contents* never enter a prompt — the block names the files and their paths, and generated code opens what it needs, because a references directory can be larger than the quest itself. A skill shipping only `scripts/` is treated as a tool rather than a library, which is what puts "drive it" instead of "import it" in front of the model. What an experiment gets from the skills its quest selected (`core/experiment_deps.py`), in its own environment as well as FI's: each skill's recorded packages (`pip_requires` in its `provenance.json`, which the importer writes) are installed into the quest's environment, and a library skill's folder (and its `scripts/`) is put on the experiment's `PYTHONPATH`, the way its self-test runs — so a skill that passed its self-test can also be imported by the research profile's clean per-quest environment. The quest's own `code/` files are never handed to `pip`. When a requested package cannot be installed, the rest are installed one at a time instead of none, and the repair step reads what failed and why before the run's own error; a name that is a selected skill's (a tool skill such as a Node.js chart tool, which is run and cannot be installed) is explained as that skill rather than as a missing package. Maturity is derived from which files exist (notes → guided → adapter), so a skill hardens without migrating. **FI ships no skills** — not one, including any its own authors wrote: a skill that arrives with the install is a curated library again, and curation does not compound.
