Discovery covers `FI_SKILLS_DIR` (default `~/.frontier-insight/skills`, deliberately outside the repo, because skills accumulate per machine rather than being things you commit) and the `fi.skills` entry-point group, so a library can ship its own skill without anyone editing FI. **Two gates stand before use, and both must pass:** the self-test the skill carries must run green, and a person must have approved *that exact content* — approval binds to a content hash, so a skill rewritten by distillation lapses its approval rather than inheriting it. An unattended `--fleet` run therefore cannot adopt capability nobody signed off on; a skill that is not trusted is absent from the prompt, and the quest falls back to generating the physics itself. **A self-test that already passed is not run again for every quest.** Loading skills for a quest records each pass in `skill_selftest_cache.json` beside the approval ledger (`~/.frontier-insight/` by default; `FI_SKILLS_SELFTEST_CACHE` moves it), keyed by the skill's name and content hash, the Python version and executable, and a fingerprint of every installed package's name and version. Editing any file in the skill, switching interpreters, or installing, upgrading or removing any package tests that skill again at the next quest. Only passes are recorded: a failing or timed-out self-test runs again on every load, and a self-test that fails anywhere — `--skills`, an approval, a quest — removes that skill's recorded passes, because the key cannot see an external binary or a service a test depends on. A recorded pass stands in for the test and nothing else: approval is checked against the ledger on every load, as before. The self-tests that do need to run, run in parallel (up to eight at a time) in a worker thread, so the web dashboard keeps answering while a quest loads its skills. On one machine's 108-skill library, whose self-tests take 340 s one after another, a load with an empty cache took 112 s and the next load 2 s. Commands that check a skill on demand — `--skills`, `--approve-skill`, `--approve-all-skills`, and their web and VSCode equivalents — always run the self-test fresh. The file is a cache, not a record anyone relies on: a corrupt or unreadable one is treated as empty, and deleting it is always safe — the next quest simply runs every self-test once more, and approvals are untouched. It is never written inside a skill directory, since that would change the content hash and lapse the approval the test was checking. A **selection step** between literature and design decides which skills a quest actually carries. It reads a catalogue — one line per skill: kind, description, its *when NOT to use* limit, and its track record — and picks what fits, at temperature 0 so the same topic selects the same skills. This is not cosmetic: injecting every trusted skill into the four design/implement prompts cost ~465 tokens per skill per node, so a 50-skill library added ~93,000 tokens to every quest pass — the accumulation mechanism becoming its own largest cost. The catalogue for the same library costs about 13k tokens once per quest — measured on a real published skill, whose 845-character description is carried in full because the phrases that make it findable sit in its later sentences. Still an order of magnitude better than injecting everything, and it is paid once rather than on every pass. Selection can only narrow: a name it invents, or one belonging to an unapproved skill, is discarded and logged, so no model can talk capability into a quest. Choosing nothing is a normal answer and the quest then generates its own code. Selection also says what each chosen skill is for. An **experiment** skill goes to the `implement` stages with its instructions, API surface and selection reason, and to `design`, which decides which skill a method rests on rather than writing the calls, as only its description, *when NOT to use* limit and selection reason; either may decline one it judges inapplicable, saying why in `method`. A **writing** skill — structure, style, diagrams in the text — goes only to `write`, as its instructions and selection reason, below FI's own rules on honesty, citations and format. The skills' range assertions join the plausibility gate above. `engine.skills` narrows the candidate set when set, and means "every trusted skill is a candidate" when empty; `engine.skills_exclude` keeps named skills out, which also makes the A/B comparison easy. `python launch.py --why-skills "<topic>"` previews the whole decision without running a quest, and a quest whose review is accepted writes itself into the provenance of the skills it used, under a file lock so parallel `--fleet` runs lose no records. Manage them from the CLI: `python launch.py --skills` lists every skill with the reason it is or is not usable (running each self-test, since a status that did not execute the check would be a guess), and `--approve-skill <name> --approve-as <you>` records approval (`--revoke-skill` undoes it, and the skill stays listed as proposed). **`--remove-skill <name>` removes a skill from FI completely**: one in FI's own folder is deleted from disk together with its approval and recorded self-test passes; one another tool installed is read in place and FI never writes there, so it is hidden from FI instead (its files are left as they are) and `--restore-skill <name>` shows it again; one a Python package ships is not deleted and the message gives the `pip uninstall` to run. The Web skills page (a *Remove* button that asks first) and `@fi /remove-skill` do the same. Showing a hidden skill again is `--restore-skill` in the CLI only: the web server answers `POST /api/skills/<name>/restore`, but the page has no button for it, and VS Code has no command. There is no anonymous approver — the gate exists so a person decides. **All three interfaces carry the whole gate**: the web dashboard's `/skills` page and the VSCode chat panel (`@fi /skills`, `/scan-skill`, `/approve-skill`, `/revoke-skill`, `/import-skill`, `/teach-skill`) list, review, approve and revoke exactly as the CLI does. They render `launch.py --skills --json` and the matching web API rather than reimplementing discovery, hashing, the ledger or the scanner, so the surfaces cannot drift. Each keeps the ceremony intact: the approver is named every time — prefilled in VSCode, typed in the browser, never derived from a session — and a high-severity finding takes a second explicit confirmation. The UI surfaces list without running self-tests, since each can take up to ~2 minutes (a generated selftest's own timeout scales with how many scripts a skill bundles — see `core.skills.registry.run_selftest`), and both say plainly that the statuses shown are as last recorded rather than verified.
- **Skills from other agents, and skills a quest must use** — skills another agent installed are **found by FI itself**, with no path given, in the places those tools keep them, on Linux, macOS and Windows alike: every tool folder in your home directory (`~/.claude`, `~/.codex`, `~/.agents`, `~/.cursor`, ... any dot-folder) and its `plugins` tree, `~/.config`, `~/.local/share`, `~/Library/Application Support`, `%APPDATA%`, `%LOCALAPPDATA%`, the folders the tools' own variables name (`CODEX_HOME`, `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, `XDG_DATA_HOME`), and your project (the current folder and its parents, for `.<tool>/skills` and `./skills`). A folder counts when something inside it carries a `SKILL.md`; when a plugin cache holds several versions of a skill the newest wins. Add more with `engine.skills_dirs`; `engine.skills_scan_known_dirs: false` turns the search off, and `FI_EXTERNAL_SKILLS_DIRS` replaces all of it (empty means none). The two `engine` settings belong to the quest that names them: quests running in one process (`--fleet`) each look only where their own config says, so one quest never finds or uses a skill from a folder only another quest named. The skill commands take a quest's YAML: `python launch.py --config quest.yaml --skills` (likewise `--why-skills`, `--scan-skill`, `--approve-skill`, `--revoke-skill`) also looks in the folders that quest's `engine.skills_dirs` names, so a skill that lives only there can be listed, reviewed and approved. `--config` beside them starts no quest, and a config that cannot be read is a one-line error on stderr. Without `--config` they look in the usual places and `FI_EXTERNAL_SKILLS_DIRS` alone, and `FI_EXTERNAL_SKILLS_DIRS`, when set, outranks the config's folders too. The Web skills page and the VSCode chat panel take the same option: a *Quest config* field on the page (the full path of the YAML, kept in the browser, and a config that cannot be read is shown as the same one-line reason) and `--config <yaml>` after the command in chat (`@fi /skills --config C:\quests\a.yaml`, likewise `/scan-skill`, `/approve-skill` and `/revoke-skill`; a path with a space goes in quotes). It changes only where skills are looked for: what is shown, what is refused and what is recorded are the same, and bulk approval takes no config, as on the command line. They are read **in place** — nothing is copied or converted — and are searched and selected like FI's own. They carry no FI self-test, so the approval of their exact content is the only gate: `python launch.py --approve-skill <name> --approve-as <you>`, with `--config <quest.yaml>` for one that only that quest's folders hold (the Web skills page and `@fi /approve-skill` do the same, with the config path as above); edit the skill and the approval lapses. The listing marks them *external*. They are never approved in bulk (`--approve-all-skills` leaves them out), their approvals are namespaced so one cannot overwrite an FI skill's of the same name, an FI skill of the same name wins, and FI never writes into their folders. Their scripts run with your permissions when a quest uses them, called by absolute path. With `execution.sandbox: docker` the experiment cannot see host paths, so each external skill that is approved **and** selected for the quest's experiment has its own folder mounted **read-only** at `/fi-skills/<name>` (a name with unusual characters gets a stable, sanitised folder name; a skill selected only for writing the paper is never run, so it is not mounted), and the code-writing prompts that carry the skill's text and file list give that container path, in the bundled-file list and wherever the skill's own text names its host folder. Nothing else is added: not an approved skill the quest did not select, not an unapproved one, not a skills folder, the home directory or a tool's configuration folder. A skill folder that is a symbolic link (or a Windows junction), that resolves anywhere but a direct child of the folder it was found in, that has no regular `SKILL.md`, or whose path a bind mount cannot carry (a network path, a `:` other than the drive letter's) is refused; `run.log` says which and why, and the prompt then tells the model the skill's files are not reachable rather than naming a path the container lacks. The folder is checked again each time a container is created. A `--watch` re-mounts the same skills. A link inside a mounted folder is not followed by the mount (the container resolves it against its own filesystem), so one that points out of the folder finds nothing there. The other sandboxes see host paths as before. The mount was tested with a mocked docker client, not against a running daemon. `engine.skills_required: [folder-name, ...]` makes a quest use those skills whatever selection says; one that cannot be used (not found, not approved) stops the quest before its first LLM call and says why.

  Approval binds to the **whole** skill directory, not a list of named files: everything under it is hashed except `provenance.json` and Python bytecode. That distinction is load-bearing — a rule covering only the files someone remembered to name would let an approved skill's bundled `scripts/` be swapped with the approval intact, which is the human gate guarding the wrong thing.

  Because a skill's `SKILL.md` enters a prompt verbatim and its `selftest.py` is executed, `python launch.py --scan-skill <name>` statically reviews one before you approve it: instruction-injection phrasing and invisible characters in the prose, network access, `eval`, and environment reads in the code, fetch-and-decode chains in shell scripts. Nothing is imported or run — Python is parsed as a syntax tree, everything else by pattern. The scan runs every time a skill is evaluated, so hand-authored and entry-point skills are covered as well as imported ones — the one shortcut being a quest-time load that reuses a recorded self-test pass, which also reuses the scan recorded with it, taken of the same content by the same version of the scanner — and a high-severity finding **stops** `--approve-skill` unless `--despite-findings` is also passed, with the ledger recording that it was. The flag exists because approving past a flagged network call is often correct — what it buys is that reading the findings becomes an act rather than an assumption. **Findings never block a skill.** They are heuristics: a skill that legitimately drives a network tool will flag one, and quarantine stays reserved for a self-test that actually failed. For the same reason the report never says a skill is safe — it says how many findings came out of how many rules, because a payload no rule describes is unfound rather than absent.

  Because FI works in any field, the library is not filtered by domain — which would make every installed skill a candidate for every quest, the same accumulation problem one level up (~28,000 tokens for a hundred-odd skills, growing with the library). So the catalogue is **layered**: a skill with no domain tag is general and always a candidate, while tagged ones (`--import-skill --domains genomics,biomedical`) are admitted only when the quest's topic looks related. Relevance reuses the all-MiniLM cosine already used for passage ranking, blended with lexical overlap — no new dependency. Under `FI_OFFLINE` it loads the model from the local HF cache rather than skipping it (no network call either way); it degrades to lexical alone only when the model is genuinely absent — not installed, or not cached under `FI_OFFLINE`. If relevance cannot be computed at all, **every** domain skill is admitted and the selection report says why: selection can only narrow, so a catalogue that is too large costs tokens and is visible, while a silently too-small one changes results with nothing to show for it.

  Two commands teach FI a skill. `--teach-skill <name> --from <module>` drafts one from an installed library, reading its real signatures by introspection rather than generating them, and leaves the judgement calls — when it applies, when it does not, what its outputs may legally be — as TODOs for you. `--import-skill <path>` brings in a skill written for another agent, preserving the front-matter `description` that makes it findable and carrying its bundled directories; `--list-foreign-skills` shows what is available to import. An import **generates** a self-test rather than leaving the skill unusable: it probes that every bundled script is present and answers `--help`, which catches a broken, moved or incomplete install. That is deliberately less than a written test proves. A generated test cannot check that a tool still behaves the way `SKILL.md` describes — the thing a self-test exists for — so the generated file says so in its own text, `--skills` marks the skill, and approval repeats it. The marker lives in the file, so rewriting the test with real checks clears it; a flag in `provenance.json` would have gone stale at exactly that moment. For a prose-only skill with no scripts, the generated test states plainly that passing proves only that the files are on disk, unless the skill was imported from the starter list by `scripts/import_scientist_skills.py` with its library declared (`pip_requires` covering what `core/skills/known_requirements.py::PRESET_PIP_REQUIRES` lists for it; `--import-skill` declares none, so it adds no check): the test then also checks one or two values the library itself fixes (the speed of light in astropy, the reverse complement of ATGC in biopython, gamma(5) = 24 in SciPy; the table is `core/skills/known_checks.py::KNOWN_CHECKS`), so a missing or broken install fails instead of passing. Each check is written into `selftest.py` as a plain function a person approving the skill can read (never a string run with `exec`, which the scanner would flag high), and `ok` starts false, so a check that never sets it fails. A skill of your own that happens to share a starter name gets no such check. Re-running `scripts/import_scientist_skills.py` rewrites a self-test that is still the generated one (a hand-written one is never touched), and the skill must then be approved again, because its content changed.

- **Degenerate-run guard** — `engine.degenerate_run_guard` (default on) treats a script that exits 0 but emits an all-zero RESULT_JSON as a soft failure: the execute-repair loop gets a chance to fix the underlying bug (wrong grid/sampling, a threshold/edge finder returning a zero sentinel, bad normalization) before a paper is written. If the result is still degenerate after repair, analyze flags it and frames an honest failure note instead of reporting silent zeros as findings.
- **Bounded result_json in the analyze prompt** — the full `RESULT_JSON` stays in state at full fidelity (it feeds `results.csv`), but the copy spliced into the analyze prompt is compacted to a ~200 KB budget. A pathological experiment can dump raw arrays/images into its `RESULT_JSON` — one low-k1 DUV quest emitted a 36 MB block that blew past `codex_cli`'s 1,048,576-char turn limit and hard-failed analyze. The compactor keeps the structure **valid and representative**: long lists keep head + tail elements with a count-of-elided marker, long strings are truncated, and dict keys + scalar summary stats (the values analyze actually reasons over) survive intact — so the model interprets summary statistics instead of choking on raw pixel dumps. A WARNING fires when compaction happens, pointing at the real fix (have the experiment emit summary statistics, not raw arrays). Complements the provider-layer input-cap guard, which remains the last-resort net for any oversized prompt.
- **Analyze-driven re-route** — `re_experiment` re-enters design; `broaden_lit` re-enters literature with the hypothesis folded in.
- **Cross-paper check** — per-finding literature search + supporting / conflicting / neutral classification. Optional CoVe-style **verification pass** (`engine.cross_check_verify: true`, default off): a second LLM call per finding re-examines each non-neutral assignment with pointed verification questions and revises the classification — typical revision is "this 'supporting' citation is actually too topically thin, downgrade to neutral". Adds 1 LLM call per finding with non-neutral hits (typically +1–5 calls per quest); skipped when the first pass produced no non-neutral assignments. Verified output replaces the first-pass classification in `state['cross_check']`; the first-pass snapshot is preserved alongside under `first_pass` for audit, and `verification_notes` carries the question/answer trail.
- **Pilot pass** — `engine.pilot_run: bool = False` (opt-in). Off by default because honouring `FI_PILOT` is a prompt instruction the engine cannot enforce: a script that ignores it runs at full scale under a fifth of the timeout, so it times out and warns on every quest. Turn it on once a setup has shown its scripts comply. Before the real run, the engine executes the same `experiment.py` with `FI_PILOT=1` set (the implement prompt instructs the script to shrink whatever dominates runtime while keeping the identical metrics and `RESULT_JSON` keys) under a short timeout (`engine.pilot_timeout_frac`, default 0.2 × `execution.timeout_s`, floored at 30s). `execute_reflect` already repairs a script that *crashes*; the pilot catches the other failure — a script that runs cleanly and answers the wrong question, which otherwise costs the full timeout to discover. Its numbers are **discarded**; only its signal is used, and it never fails a quest: a pilot that crashes logs a warning and the full run proceeds (where `execute_reflect` can repair it with the traceback it needs), and a pilot whose values break the design's own `result_assertions` warns that the **design** is the likely fault (range, units) rather than the code.
- **Design-revision provenance** — `needs/DESIGN_HISTORY.json`. `review` and `cross_check` can both route back to `design`, so a hypothesis can legitimately be rewritten after its results are known. A finished paper looks identical either way, which is what makes the record necessary: entry 0 is the pre-registration (stated before any result existed), and every later entry is flagged `post_hoc` with the trigger that sent the engine back (`analyze.next_step`, or the reviewer verdict) plus whether the hypothesis text actually changed. The quest is not stopped, but the revision is no longer invisible. Each entry also says whether the experiment had run (or the data been analysed) by then (`after_results`; a design done again before anything ran is not counted). `core/disclosure.py` writes one paragraph into the paper's methods (*How this result was reached*, between `<!-- fi:attempts -->` markers; before the results when the paper has no methods section): how many times the design was revised after the experiment had first been run and why (in fixed plain phrases, never the record's raw text), how many times the whole experiment was run (`.fi/attempts.jsonl` `run` lines; a run includes all its seeds) and how many were discarded, by why (every run but the last, which is the one kept even when a check only warned about it), and that numbers from before the last revision are exploratory (not for a literature survey). With no revision it says so in one sentence; a quest with no run and no revision gets no paragraph. The write node removes anything between the markers, or under the paragraph's lead words, from what the writer returns before any other step reads the draft, and puts the engine's paragraph in last; the passage editor is told not to touch it and the page-limit trim never offers it. The numeric oracle, the statistics check, the number provenance check and the claim check leave the paragraph out (`strip_for_checks`, only when every word is the engine's grammar), and the reviewer is told it is FI's. On the evidence ladder a design revised that way is a gap of `publication_ready` unless a confirm run (`engine.phased`) finished after its last revision, read from `.fi/phased.json` (`design_revisions_at_confirm`), not from the setting; a revision an approved post-hoc protocol amendment made is that amendment's gap only; a literature survey has none; a data analysis closes it only with a confirm run on rows held back from one table it was given (*Explore, then confirm*), and otherwise cannot.
- **Multi-seed replication** — `engine.execute_replicates: int = 3` (default 3). A single run is not a result: one number has no error bar, so reporting it as a finding overstates what the experiment showed. Replication re-runs the generated SCRIPT only -- `implement` is not re-invoked, so it costs only local compute, with one exception: at most **one extra LLM call** per implement pass, spent only when the script ignores the seed (below). Set 1 to opt out on a slow experiment. When >1, the execute node runs the generated `experiment.py` N times: each with its own `FI_REPLICATE_SEED` — **the first run included** — so the script can deterministically seed every RNG it uses (the implement prompt instructs it to read this env var). **The seeds are spaced so no two runs can share a draw.** Consecutive runs' seeds stride by `engine.replicate_seed_stride` (a million by default), because a script rarely uses the base seed directly: it derives one seed per trial, and `base + counter` is the obvious way to write that. Handed bases a few integers apart — 42 for the unseeded first run and 1 and 2 for the replicates — a graded quest drew 259 of every 300 trials in common between one pair of its three runs and 299 of 300 between another, then reported the spread across those near-identical runs as a 95% confidence interval: printed `0.581–0.581` and `0.941–0.941`, and read back in its own limitations section as evidence of stability. A stride keeps the streams disjoint for any experiment drawing fewer than a stride of seeds per run (that quest drew 2,700), so what varies between replicates is variation the experiment produced. All N `RESULT_JSON` payloads land in `state['result_json_replicates']`; the analyze node aggregates every numeric leaf — **nested results included**, flattened to dotted paths like `by_h.0.5.RK4.trajectory_error`, which is the normal shape for a parameter sweep — with mean ± sample-std **plus standard error and a 95% confidence interval**. An interval stops at the values its metric cannot pass: 0 and 1 for a metric named as a probability or proportion (`probability`, `fraction`, `share`, `accuracy`, …) whose seeds all lie in [0, 1], and the range the design's or a skill's `result_assertions` declares for it. Metrics that were identical across every seed are omitted from the analyze prompt and counted in a note, so a crossed design cannot crowd out the analysis it is meant to inform and surfaces them to the writer so the paper reports `metric (95% CI lo–hi, n)` instead of a single-seed point estimate. **Line figures are drawn as the mean.** The plot-style bootstrap keeps each seed's lines; after the seeds run, a figure whose panels hold only lines, with the same labels and x values at every seed, is drawn again over the same file as each line's mean shaded with its 95% confidence interval (bounded like the results), by `code/replot_figures.py` in the quest's Python under the house style, and the writer is told the figure is the mean of N seeds. Its legend is set outside the axes, to their right (the saved image grows to hold it), when it has four or more entries or when the drawn canvas shows it over a line or a band: the engine draws that figure, so nothing else moves its legend, and of the 20 redrawn figures in the stored quests whose layout was measured, 6 (in 4 of 9 quests) had it over their data, a legend of six to eight entries in the middle of the axes. In a figure of several panels, one legend goes under the figure instead of one beside each panel, which squeezed the panels of a three-panel figure to slivers. The band is FI's interval over the seed means, so legend, title and axis text that named another interval (`Wilson 95% CI`, `± 1 SD`) is rewritten to `95% CI over N seeds`, and the writer is told the band is not the interval the experiment named. Redrawn again with this rule from the stored plans, under each quest's own house style and recorder, the 6 of 8 figures rechecked that had it over their data no longer do. Error bars count as part of their line (`errorbar` with y errors and a drawn line): each series is drawn at its mean, and the 95% confidence interval over the seeds replaces one seed's bars. A bar chart, a histogram, a scatter, or a figure with anything else on it (text, an image, a colourbar) goes back to the primary run's file and its record of what it draws (seed 0), since every seed draws over the same files; the writer, the analysis and the review are told it shows replicate seed 0 only (seed 0 of N when the number of seeds is known): a seed repeats the whole experiment, so the figure holds every run that seed made, not the mean over the seeds, and the text quotes seed 0's values for it or says it shows a single replicate (seed 0). An empty shaded band that draws nothing (seaborn leaves one per line when each x holds a single observation) does not count as anything else. The number check and the claim check compare the paper with each result's mean and 95% CI over the seeds as well as with seed 0's values. **Stops early only when the code has no randomness.** A script with no source of random numbers in its code or in the modules it imports from its own folder (an ODE integrator, a closed-form sweep) stops after the first extra seed whose `RESULT_JSON` is identical to seed 0's: a repeat can only return the same numbers. The scan is generous, so a script that reads `FI_REPLICATE_SEED` (as the implement prompt asks every script to) counts as able to draw random numbers even when it never does; a deterministic integrator written that way runs every seed. A script that can draw random numbers runs every seed asked for, even when the first two agree: a yes/no outcome, a rare event, a count or a rounded metric can come out the same on two seeds and differ on the third, and stopping there would report no spread for a study that has one. When every seed of such a script agrees, the seeds stay the replicates and analyze says that all of them gave the same numbers (what these seeds showed, not proof that the study has no randomness) rather than printing an aggregate of zeroes. A simulation that defines `run_cell` is run once per setting by FI itself and never gets here. **A script that never names its seed is sent back once, right after `implement`.** The implement prompts tell the model to read `FI_REPLICATE_SEED` and write no seed of its own, and four of six recent codex quests did anyway (`RNG_SEED = ...`, `SEED = 20260919`, `MASTER_SEED`), so every replicate was the same run. When replication is on (`execute_replicates > 1`), the quest is not a background job, and the script written by `implement` never contains the name `FI_REPLICATE_SEED`, the engine makes one repair call (`implement_seed` in `cost.jsonl`, so its cost is visible): the same request `execute_reflect` makes for a repair, with the whole script and one change asked for — read the integer from `FI_REPLICATE_SEED` (0 when unset) and derive every random generator's seed from it, keeping everything else as it is. The repair is written over `code/experiment.py` only if it parses as Python and now names the variable; a repair that did not, or a call that failed, is dropped with one `[implement]` warning and the script stays as written, so the fallback below still applies — unless `rigor_profile: research`, where a failed repair (either shape) pauses the quest instead (`replicate_seed_unrepairable`, a `supply` pause: fix the script and resume to retry the repair, or drop the profile). It never runs for a single replicate, a background job or a script that already reads the seed, and it does not use up the `execute_reflect` repair budget. Measured over the 132 `experiment.py` files in the stored outputs, 56 do not name the variable (15 of 66 in the recent SIR quests, 41 of 66 in older ones written before the instruction existed), including the four quests that motivated this. Only a source scan and a fake model have exercised that shape: no real model has been asked to make that edit. **The same call goes out when the script reads the seed and builds a generator the seed cannot reach.** Naming the variable is not obeying it. One graded quest read `FI_REPLICATE_SEED`, handed it to `np.random.seed`, and drew all 300 of its Gillespie trajectories a cell from an `np.random.default_rng()` built ninety lines earlier with no seed at all -- separate streams, so the legacy global's seed reached none of them. That failure hides better than the first: the replicates DO differ, so an aggregate, a standard error and a confidence interval are all published, and only reproducibility is gone. The engine parses the script and reports every generator built without a seed (`default_rng`, `RandomState`, `Random`, `SeedSequence`, the bit generators, and a bare `np.random.seed()`), counting an explicit `None` as no seed and resolving names through the script's own imports so a quest's own `Random` class is not mistaken for the stdlib's. A generator built in a `if rng is None:` fallback is left alone -- that branch is how a script says its caller hands it a seeded one, and rewriting a reproducible script costs a call and risks the script; the blind spot that buys is a fallback some call site actually takes. The request names the offending lines and says that seeding the legacy global does not reach a Generator, and the repair is kept only if the script then reads the seed AND builds no generator without one. Measured over all 188 scripts the stored quests generated -- 135 `experiment.py` and the 53 plotting scripts written beside them -- it reports exactly one: the Gillespie quest above. Both fallback idioms are in that corpus and both stay silent. Exercised once end to end on that quest's own script with gemma4: the model threaded one seeded generator through the simulation, after which the same seed produced the same results twice and a different seed different ones, where the script as shipped produced different results from the same seed. **A script that never reads its seed is not deterministic — it is unreplicated.** Two runs agreeing has two causes that are not the same fact about the experiment, and treating both as determinism is what let a hardcoded seed reach a paper: three graded quests shipped a script carrying its own `RNG_SEED` constant and 300 stochastic trajectories per cell, whose replicates matched to the last leaf (242, 314 and 333 of them) but for the ordinal FI itself injects. So when every seed agrees, the script's own source decides which case it is. If the seed it reads reaches its random generators, or it builds a generator with no seed at all (new numbers on every run), the runs are samples that agreed, as above; so are the runs of a script that reads the seed, or builds such a generator, in a module it imports from its own folder. If it never names the variable, or names it without the value reaching any generator it seeds (a hardcoded constant instead), it cannot have responded to it, so the runs are **one run repeated**: no replicate list is published at all, which leaves every downstream path — the analyze aggregate, the figure captions, the writer's "seed 0 of N" language, the number check and the claim check — on its honest single-run behaviour without any of them needing to know why, and analyze is told in as many words to report one measurement and to print no mean over seeds, no standard error and no interval. This is decided only after every seed has run, so it costs the full number of runs. A script with **no random source at all** (an integrator, a closed-form sweep) is the exception to the warning: FI says in plain words that the study is deterministic, that one run is the whole result, and that its trust comes from convergence, conservation and analytic-limit checks rather than repeat counts; analyze is told the same and is told not to list missing repeats as a limitation, and no seed-repair call is made for it. The check is a generous source scan, so any mention of a random module or sampler keeps the warning above. A script that never reads the seed and whose runs nonetheless differ is drawing from OS entropy — those are genuine independent samples, so the aggregate stands, with a warning that the run is not reproducible. **Runs after the plausibility gate, not before.** A result that violates the design's `result_assertions` is about to be regenerated by `execute_reflect`, so replicating it first would pay N× for an error bar on a discarded number. Failure isolation: a single replicate failure (NaN, OOM, transient) doesn't abort the rest — the aggregator skips the failed seed; if the *primary* run fails the engine skips replication entirely (fix the script first via the execute_reflect loop). Cost: N executions per design pass. Use 3–5 for variance reporting; 10+ for the rare case where a tight CI matters.
- **Statistical rigor — CIs, effect sizes, multiple-comparison guard** — beyond the per-metric 95% confidence intervals above, when results carry `by_<factor>` strata the analyze payload also gets, computed in pure stdlib (no numpy/scipy): a **95% CI per stratum**, the **pairwise effect size** (Cohen's d + a negligible/small/medium/large label) between strata for each shared metric (strata nest: in a crossed design such as `by_h` → step size → method → metric, every level below the `by_*` key whose children share one shape is compared too, so method A vs method B is reported at each step size under a dotted label like `by_h.0.5`; a level whose children are named for the protocol's declared metrics is not, since unlike groups such as `errors` and `timing` can share a shape too), and a **multiple-comparison guard** — the number of comparisons made and the Bonferroni-corrected α (`0.05 / N`), with a `many` flag when there are more than five. The analyze + write prompts are instructed to report the CI alongside each headline number, quote the effect size when claiming one group beats another, and not over-claim a single stratum difference that wouldn't survive correction; a single-seed run honestly reports "no replication → no CI." A small embedded t-table covers small-n replication exactly.
- **Stratification-by-factor across the implement/analyze/write contract** — when the experiment naturally has categorical strata (clip classes, methods, datasets, seeds, difficulty levels), the engine threads them through end-to-end. The `implement` prompt instructs the generated `experiment.py` to emit ``by_<factor>`` per-stratum breakdowns in `RESULT_JSON` alongside aggregates; the `analyze` prompt surfaces per-stratum findings as tagged bullets (``[by_clip_class:isolated_lines] ...``); the `write` prompt's honesty rules forbid collapsing those findings into an aggregate-only Results section unless the analysis explicitly notes the effect is uniform. Single-condition experiments are unaffected — aggregate-only stays aggregate-only.
- **Ideate self-reflection** — extra LLM call may swap the chosen idea (or `engine.ideate_tournament: true` for pairwise N-way comparison: C(N,2) calls, the idea with the most wins is chosen — win counting, not Elo, by one model family, so not independent judgements; whether it picks better ideas than the single critique has not been measured).
- **Design self-critique** — after the design is drafted (by the `plan` step, on the first pass), a second LLM pass audits it and emits an amended design BEFORE `implement → execute → analyze → write` spend compute on a bad plan. The checklist is the five patterns the methodologist reviewer hard-flags (circular evaluation, single-point eval, weak-baseline retention, dimensionless/grid-only metrics, natural-stratum collapse) and seven more: a precision that was chosen by cost rather than by the interval the claim needs (with the arithmetic: about 0.98/sqrt(n) for a probability near 0.5), the estimand and how its uncertainty is estimated (a spread across a few seeds is not a sample size), an arbitrary threshold the conclusion depends on, whether settings and runs share a random stream, numerical convergence and the invariant every run must satisfy, a declared oracle, and what becomes of failed runs; for a search for the best design, three more (a real, feasible baseline; ranges with a physical meaning where the model holds; check settings that really are finer than the search's), and circular evaluation asks whether the search and the check settings differ rather than for an independent model. An amended design keeps every key the draft had (the `protocol` included). Failure-isolated: a transient provider error or a reply that cannot be used keeps the original draft, never blocks the quest. Every pass is kept in `needs/DESIGN_CRITIQUE.json`: the objections in full, the design before and after, the keys that changed, and a `status` that says `failed` (with the reason) for an audit that did not run, so it no longer looks the same as one that found nothing; `plan.md`'s *Checks already made* says which keys the audit changed, or that it did not complete. The reviewer panel (`review` after the paper) is a separate, later check and is still empty by default: the interview recommends it (a 3-persona panel by default, and a 4-persona one that adds the reproducibility reviewer, worth it for a stochastic simulation study) and the engine leaves the choice, and the cost, to you.
- **Two-stage implement** — the `implement` step is split into `implement_outline` (writes a Python scaffold with function signatures, constant sources, and the `RESULT_JSON` template — typically 30–80 lines with `raise NotImplementedError` bodies) and `implement` (fills in each function body, consuming the outline as input). Splitting the work lets each LLM call be smaller; on complex topics that previously triggered ~9 min extended-thinking spans on Sonnet 4.6, the outline+body pair lands in 2–5 min total with progress visible between calls. Failure-isolated: an unparseable outline OR a transient outline-call error makes the body fall back to the legacy single-shot prompt (also used on pre-Phase-2 checkpoint resume). The body stage commits to the outline's function signatures, so an outline mistake surfaces at body-write time rather than after execute. Default `provider.node_cli_timeout_s`: outline → 600 s, body/legacy-fallback → 1800 s (the body call typically finishes in 90–180 s once the outline lands, but the same `implement` bucket also covers the legacy single-shot fallback that needs the full extended-thinking budget). An explicit `provider.cli_timeout_s` acts as a **floor**: raise it and leave the per-node map alone, and every built-in entry below it — the outline's 600 s included — is raised to match, so a quest that asks for 900 s everywhere actually gets it.
- **Prompts carry only what their step needs** — two prompts used to carry text their step does not use. `implement_outline` fixes a script's structure (its functions, constants and result template) and received every selected skill's full text, about 21,000 tokens that the body stage receives again when it writes the calls. It now gets the summary the design step gets (what each skill is for, where it does not apply, why it was selected) and the body stage still gets the full text. A run that submits a background job (`execution.background_jobs`) or carries example files (`execution.inputs`) keeps the full text in the outline, since there the skills say how a job is submitted and how the examples are combined. `analyze` interprets an experiment's results and received the whole reviewed-literature block, 7,000 to 13,000 tokens that the write step and the cross-check read on their own; when an experiment produced results it now gets the titles of the sources retrieved (under 1,000 tokens), and the whole block stays for a survey or no-simulation study, a run on the user's own data (`--analyze`), a run with no results and a degenerate one. Replayed from three stored quests of one topic with the real model: 18 of 18 outlines parsed, outlines from the summary named the same functions and result template as ones from the full text (the two agreed with each other as much as two outlines of one kind did) at 2,066 prompt tokens per call instead of 22,894; 6 analyses with only the titles matched 6 with the whole block (5.0 against 5.3 key findings, 2.0 against 2.5 supported claims, every `next_step` still `publish`) at 25,098 prompt tokens instead of 34,702. Together about 30,000 tokens a quest, 13% of the 232,000 a quest used on average. Not measured: a topic other than the one replayed, a background job, and whether a graded paper differs; the analysis's `broaden_lit` route is now judged from the titles and the results, and it appears in 3 lines of the stored run logs.
- **Reviewer panel** — N personas in parallel + moderator synthesis (`rigor_score` + `depth_score` axes). The built-in **methodologist** persona hard-flags four common-but-fatal patterns: circular evaluation (optimization objective shares model/simulator/distribution with the evaluation metric — "train-on-test"), single-point evaluation where a sweep is the field norm, admitted-weak baselines kept in the comparison instead of re-run, and metrics in dimensionless / grid-only units without a physical-grounding bridge. Each persona's response includes a ``must_flag_hits`` list (canonical names: ``circular_evaluation``, ``single_point_eval``, ``weak_baseline_no_rerun``, ``pseudo_units``); the aggregator unions hits across the panel. A non-empty ``must_flag_hits`` forces another revise pass **even when** ``engine.review_loop: false`` is set — the operator cannot accidentally short-circuit the design loop the must-flag block exists to gate. The persona must explicitly state non-applicability of each check rather than reach acceptance by silence. **New quests (via the interview, CLI `--new`, or VSCode `@fi /new`) default to a 3-persona panel** (methodologist + statistician + devil_advocate) instead of the cost-conscious single reviewer; the methodologist must fire for the non-bypassable must-flag enforcement to take effect. Single-reviewer mode is still selectable in the interview when cost is the priority.
- **Unified human-in-the-loop (`pauses:`)** — every place the quest stops for a human is one of two interactions — **ANSWER** (it asks; you reply) or **SUPPLY** (it needs files; you drop them) — configured under one coherent `pauses:` section: `pauses.clarify` (off/auto/ask; unset means ask when someone can answer, else auto), `pauses.papers` (bool), `pauses.supply` (never/after_literature/before_build/before_review/both/all), `pauses.plan` (off/ask), `pauses.review` (off/ask), plus `pauses.auto_accept_on_pass` and `pauses.timeout_s`. The legacy flags (`engine.clarify_mode`, `engine.human_feedback_gate`, `engine.pause_for_user_input`, `knowledge.pause_for_user_papers`, `engine.auto_accept_on_pass`, `engine.human_feedback_timeout_s`) still parse and are mapped in automatically. Whichever pause fires, the engine writes one **to-do card** (`core/todo.py`) as **`<quest_root>/NEXT_STEP.md`** and `.fi/todo.json`: why it stopped, what there is to decide, what FI recommends, the alternatives, exactly what to do, and the one command that goes on. Every pause kind has its recommendation and alternatives; a stop that knows better passes its own (after the protocol is frozen, the oracle stop offers going on with the failure recorded, not a plan edit that no longer counts). Under it the card lists everything else waiting that did not stop the quest: a change to the frozen protocol waiting for approval, a check set to warn that found differences, papers that could not be downloaded, literature sources that could not be reached, a failed run, and what stands between the result and the next evidence level. The CLI prints the card when the quest stops; the web quest page's **Action needed** banner and VS Code show the same card, and when a quest finishes `NEXT_STEP.md` is deleted and `.fi/todo.json` is kept only if something is still worth a look (shown on the quest page and after the run in VS Code). Resume is always `fi --resume <id>` (or the Resume button).
- **Human-review gate** — `pauses.review: ask` (the default) pauses the quest after the review node and waits for a human to accept / reject / refine the result before finalising. The snapshot includes verdict + score + must-flag hits + suggestions + the accumulated `feedback_history` of every prior refine round (so a later iteration honours every prior ask, not just the most recent). Three interfaces consume it:
    - **Web dashboard** — banner on `/quest/<id>` with Accept / Reject / Refine buttons + a textarea for refine feedback (Accept opens the list and the question, with a box for what you do not accept, which "Partly" needs, and an optional name box; the answer submits it, and the server refuses an accept without one, or a "partly" without its note); in-process spawn paths resolve via a Python future, subprocess paths write `<quest_root>/.fi/human_review_answer.json` and auto-trigger a resume.
    - **Before a person accepts** (all three interfaces): FI shows what the result does not guarantee (the blind spots of the evidence level it reached, at most two) and its most important evidence gaps (at most three, the next level's first, never one twice; the rest are counted and left in `needs/EVIDENCE.json`), then asks one question: *Have you reviewed the evidence record, and do you accept these claims and the limits listed?* — Yes / Partly (say what you do not accept) / No / I did not check (`core/acceptance.py`, the snapshot's `before_accept`, also on the review card `NEXT_STEP.md`). **Only an explicit "yes" lets the result reach `publication_ready`.** "I did not check" lets the quest finish and export, with the gap "no person reviewed the evidence before accepting". "Partly" needs a short note saying what is not accepted (an accept with "partly" and no note is refused on every interface, and is not a decision in a staged answer file); the note is recorded and kept as a gap ("a person accepted the result only in part; not accepted: …") until the paper changes (the limit written into the claims, or the problem fixed, through a refine) and a person accepts the new paper with "yes". Either gap is also said on the one-line evidence summary when another gap comes first. The answer goes through with the accept and is recorded as a receipt: `acceptance` in the quest's state and in `needs/EVIDENCE.json` (`accepted_by: person`) and a `result_accepted` event in the decision trace, each with who (the name the interface gives: `--approve-as` or the login name at the terminal, the optional name typed on the web page, the login name in VS Code; "not given" when there is none), when (`at`), which interface (`via`), the question, the answer, the note, the sha256 of the paper (`paper_sha256`) and of the evidence record that was shown (`evidence_sha256`: each interface sends back the fingerprint of the record whose limits it displayed; when the record worked out as the quest resumes differs, the receipt also keeps that one as `evidence_sha256_at_accept`, with its limits as `limits_at_accept`, and `run.log` says so), and the limits that were listed, word for word (`limits_shown`). A note given with any answer but "partly" is refused by the CLI and the web rather than dropped (use "partly" to keep it). An accept of an earlier paper does not cover a changed one, and an accept recorded as an answer to the earlier question ("do the main numbers match what you expected?") leaves the gap "the person who accepted it answered an earlier question …, not whether they reviewed the evidence". **"No" does not accept**: the terminal and VS Code offer a refine (say what is wrong) or stopping to look again, the web page points at Refine, `--accept no` is refused (exit 2), and an accept without an answer, from any interface or a staged answer file, is not a decision (the quest stops with the review kept). The earlier question about whether the numbers match what you expected is gone: it invited confirmation of an expectation, and a number that does not look right is what "partly" with its note, or a refine, carries.
    - **CLI** — `python launch.py --config <yaml> --interactive` prompts on stdin (`accept` / `reject` / `refine` + one line of feedback; an accept then shows the list above and asks the question). For a headless run that paused, decide in **one command** with the resume flags — `--resume <id> --accept yes` (or `--accept partly "what you do not accept"` / `--accept not-checked`; the list is printed before the answer is staged; `--accept` alone, or `partly` with no note, asks at a terminal, and with no terminal prints the list and the commands and exits 2; `--approve-as <name>` records that name instead of the login name), `--resume <id> --reject`, or `--resume <id> --refine "your feedback"` (these write `human_review_answer.json` for you, no hand-editing). The pause message prints these exact commands. A terminal **`--accept` / `--reject` is guarded**: it's refused (exit 2) unless the quest is genuinely paused awaiting review (`pause.json` with `kind=review`), and it will not overwrite a *different* decision already staged (e.g. a `refine` just submitted from the web UI). This stops a stray CLI accept from silently bulldozing an in-flight refine and auto-finalizing past it. A **`--refine` is never blocked** — it's the user adding work.
    - **VSCode** — the chat panel renders the verdict + must-flag hits + suggestions; QuickPick collects the action; refine opens a second input box for the feedback text; accept shows the list in the chat and asks the question in a second QuickPick ("No" offers Refine or Look again; "Partly" opens an input box that will not take an empty note).
    - **An answer with no decision in it** — an empty answer, a missing or unknown action, a closed VS Code prompt (Esc), Ctrl+C or end of input at a terminal prompt (a mistyped action is asked again; Enter still means accept, and so does an empty refine typed at the terminal or in VS Code, as their prompts say), or a staged refine with no notes, or a staged `human_review_answer.json` without accept / reject / refine — is never taken as accept (`--resume <id> --refine ""` is refused with exit 2) and never asks again in a loop: the quest stops cleanly with the review snapshot and `NEXT_STEP.md` kept, and `run.log` says the answer had no decision (a staged file without one is removed so the next resume does not read it again). Resume with a decision as above.
  An explicit **refine is always honoured** — `_route_after_human_feedback` routes it to a real revision pass even when `iteration >= max_iterations` (that cap bounds the *unattended* review loop, not the user's deliberate click); the refine goes to the writing step first: the writer answers the notes in the text and lists any point that needs a new or different experiment (`NEEDS_EXPERIMENT:` lines, taken out of the paper), and only such a point sends the quest back to the design (`_route_after_write`). Two smaller kinds of point never redo the study: a point that only lacks a number (`NEEDS_DATA:`) extends the existing `code/` script(s) with the model told to change as little as it can, keeping the frozen protocol, the figures and the settings, and runs them again (analysis and write follow); a point that only asks for the figures to be arranged or drawn differently (`NEEDS_LAYOUT:`) redraws the named figures from the numbers the run saved under `data/results/` and `data/` (not the collected or literature files) with `code/replot_layout.py` (the script may run at most `execution.timeout_s` or 300 s, whichever is shorter) (`replot_layout`, shown as “Arranging the figures again.”) and does not run the experiment again. The model writing that script is shown what each saved file holds (the array keys of a `.npz`, the columns of a table, the keys of a JSON file; `core/data_shape.py`, which reads headers only and never unpickles), and a script that fails, or runs and changes no figure, is written once more with its error. When that fails too, the figures stay as they were, `run.log` says the request was not applied, and the review says so first, in `NEXT_STEP.md`, the web review panel and the VS Code chat review (`layout_not_redrawn` in `.fi/human_review.json`; a passing review is then not accepted automatically); a figure the script fails on stays as it was, and so does everything the redraw changed or added that it did not name (a redraw cut short by a kill is undone when the quest resumes). A number added this way goes beyond the frozen protocol on purpose: the protocol stays as it is, the difference is recorded (`needs/PROTOCOL_CHECK.json`, status `extended_by_person`), the run-manifest check and the goal-coverage note do not send back what the person added (a later change to the frozen protocol that they did not ask for is still reported), and the difference stays in that record. A quest with no simulation collects the missing data again for exactly what was asked (`auto_collect_data`). When the number could not be added (the extended script did not come back, so the earlier scripts are kept; or nothing new was found) the paper says so in its limitations. When it was added, every write after the run (until the next refine) checks, with no model call, that the paper reports what the extension added for the request (`number_provenance.unreported_results`: a new result name, or more numbers in a list the results already had; reported means one of its values printed at the paper's own rounding that no earlier result also has, or, for a new name, a sentence or table that says it in words with a number (not a heading, a figure, table or section number, a citation, a restatement of the request or a "not computed"), which is what counts when the new values equal ones the paper already shows under another name); a draft that leaves it out is written once more with the request and the computed values named (once per extension), and if that one leaves it out too, `run.log` and the review pause say "your request … was computed (…) but is not in the paper" (also as `not_in_paper` in `.fi/human_review.json`, and such a paper is never accepted automatically). A two-script quest with `execution.split_failure: block` stops instead of keeping the old scripts. A point that needs a new experiment takes any missing-number points with it into the design. Otherwise the claim check and the review run as always, the review is shown the notes (a point the paper leaves unanswered is the must-fix finding `user_feedback_unaddressed`, a text fix), and the quest returns to the gate, so the user can refine again or accept. So a note about one sentence rewrites the paper and leaves the experiment and its frozen protocol alone. Which way each refine went is in the trace (`route_decision` of `write`, with `refine_scope`: `paper`, `data` (a missing number), `layout` (a redraw) or `experiment`). A refine with no notes (FI re-opening a finished quest for `--rerun` or `--update`) goes to the design instead, as before (`_route_after_human_feedback`). A finished quest can also be **re-opened for another pass** — the quest page's **Re-run** button (or `fi --rerun <id>`, or `POST /api/quests/{id}/resume?rerun=true`) re-enters the pipeline even when the LangGraph checkpoint is at its terminal state (`next=()`): the engine `aupdate_state`s back in at the human-review gate's edge so the graph re-routes through `design → … → review → human_feedback`, applying any saved `feedback_history` (so a refine that landed past the cap finally runs) and re-running whatever stages an Update invalidated. Without this a finished quest's checkpoint has no pending task, so a plain resume was a no-op. **Rerunning from a step** (`--resume <id> --from <step>`, the menu beside the quest page's **Resume** — also while the quest waits at the review, where choosing a step shows **Redo** — `@fi /resume <id> --from <step>`) does the quest again from `ideas`, `literature`, `plan`, `design`, `skills`, `code`, `run`, `figures`, `analysis`, `crosscheck`, `evidence`, `writing`, `claims` or `review` (`core/rerun_from.py`; the raw graph node names are accepted too): the engine finds, in the checkpoint history, the latest state taken just before that step ran and continues from it, so everything decided before it is kept. What that step and the later ones made is first moved to `.fi/previous/<time>/`, so old and new outputs never mix. **`--from skills`** picks the quest's skills again from the current YAML (`engine.skills`, `skills_exclude`, `skills_required`) before the code is written: one selection call, no literature call, and the plan, the design and the frozen protocol are not rewritten (`Engine._repick_skills` updates only the pick and the outline). A skill the new pick drops is named with its reason, is left out of the pip list and told to the code-writing step as not available; a skill the plan names that was dropped is reported and the plan left as it is. A skill whose own packages cannot be installed in the quest's environment is dropped from the pick the same way, with a line in `run.log`. **`--from ideas`, `literature`, `plan` and `design`** restart before the experiment was designed, so they replace the plan and the frozen protocol: they need `--approve-as <you>` (the CLI stops with a plain message without it, the engine also refuses, and the web resume refuses them and points to the command line), the old protocol, the design's audit and everything the run and the writing made from it are moved to `.fi/previous/<time>/` (the amendments stay), the `rerun_from` audit event records who approved and the sha256 of the replaced protocol, `needs/DESIGN_HISTORY.json` is carried back into the state first (a missing or unreadable one counts as one earlier design) so the new design is recorded after the old ones as post hoc, and the replacement is written as an amendment made after the protocol was frozen (`needs/PROTOCOL_AMENDMENT_<n>.json`, approved by the `--approve-as` name), so the evidence level holds back `publication_ready` and the paper says so. The round counter goes back to 0, so `plan` writes plan.md again and waits; `design` takes the design in plan.md as it is. Search-query history, the audit trail and the approved-plan record are left alone. The other new steps are `figures` (a quest with no simulation), `crosscheck`, `evidence` and `claims`; each moves only its own files and the later ones. Not offered, and why: `clarify` (redoing it is a new quest), `pause_after_literature` and `wait_for_data` (pauses that write nothing), `execute_reflect` (the repair loop inside `run`), `human_feedback` (a person's decision). A quest that never reached a step is left as it is. **Which steps can be chosen** is the quest's own history, on the line it is on now (steps only a line left by an earlier `--from` reached are not offered): `rerun_from.reached` lists the steps some checkpoint was about to run (`Engine.rerun_steps`, no model call), shown by `--resume <id> --from` with no step, by `GET /api/quests/{id}/rerun-steps` (the web menu hides the steps not in it) and by `@fi /resume <id> --from`, each step with the sentence `rerun_from.REDOES` gives for what doing it again does. `Engine.rerun_steps()` returns every step, in graph order, as name, block (`Ideas & literature`, `Skills & plan`, `Design`, `Code`, `Run`, `Analysis`, `Writing`, `Review`), sentence, whether it needs approval, whether the quest reached it, and the files that would be moved aside. **The quest map** (the web quest page's map, hidden while the quest runs, and the VS Code **FI: Quest map** panel and `@fi /map <quest_id>`, both drawn by the one script `web/static/quest_map.js` and stylesheet `quest_map.css`, which the extension copies to `media/` at compile time) shows every graph node in seven blocks (`rerun_from.MAP_BLOCKS`), each node with a plain title, its own name in small type, one sentence, what it reads and writes, a note when the graph can loop back from it, and a status from the checkpoint: finished, where it stopped, not reached, or off (the other path). Where it stopped is the node the checkpoint is about to run: the one a pause waits in (the review decision, the papers or plan pause, the clarify questions) or the one that failed. A quest counts as finished only when its checkpoint has nothing left to run and no pause or failure is waiting (`.fi/pause.json`, `NEXT_STEP.md`, `quest_failed.md`); `frontier_insight_summary.json` does not count, since a pause writes it too (`Engine.stopped_at`). `Engine.quest_map` builds the data with `rerun_from.map_payload`: `blocks`, `finished` and `nodes` (from `rerun_from.node_map`), returned with `config_path` by `GET /api/quests/{id}/rerun-steps` and printed by `launch.py --resume <id> --from --json`. A node is clickable only when it resolves to a step that has its backup list and sentence, the quest reached that step, the node is on the quest's path and the quest got as far as that node itself (two nodes can share one step); the others are grey. The VS Code panel opens next to the tabs already open, never in a new split (`quest-map.ts::tabAreaColumn`; opening it again for the same quest brings that tab back), and reads the map from `--from --json` when the tab is opened or shown again (it does not follow a running quest); the web map is hidden while the quest runs. The side panel shows what a restart keeps and redoes, the terminal command, and a **Restart from here** button (web: `POST /api/quests/{id}/resume?from=<node>`; VS Code: `@fi /resume <id> --from <step>` in chat). From `design` backwards it says the frozen protocol has to be approved again and disables the button, since a restart there needs `--approve-as` (the command is shown instead). The skills node names the config key (`engine.skills_exclude`) that changes the pick.
- **Human-review timeout** — `engine.human_feedback_timeout_s` (default 1800 s; `0` disables) bounds how long a wired callback (web POST, VSCode QuickPick) is awaited. If it elapses, the engine stops waiting and falls back to the headless path (on-disk `human_review_answer.json`, else clean pause-exit with the snapshot saved + a `--resume` hint), so an orphaned UI prompt can never park a quest forever.
- **`--auto-accept-on-pass`** — auto-accept the human-review gate for *clean* papers only (a reviewer's own accept AND no must-flag hits). Flagged or revise-verdict papers still pause for review, and so does a paper no reviewer gave a verdict on. Intended for `--fleet` so the production runner doesn't block on every clean quest. Also exposed as `pauses.auto_accept_on_pass: true` in YAML and the engine fixture path used by tests. An auto-accept ends the review pause, but **no person looked at the result**: it is recorded as automatic (`accepted_by: automatic` in `needs/EVIDENCE.json`, a `result_accepted` event with `by: automatic` in the decision trace) and the evidence stops one level below `publication_ready` with the gap "no person reviewed the result before it was accepted" (the one-line evidence summary says "Not reviewed by a person" even when another gap comes first). The same holds for a quest run with `pauses.review: off`, and for a person's accept of an earlier version of the paper (a re-run that wrote a new paper is not covered by it).
- **Multi-model ensemble** — fan out a node's LLM call across multiple models in parallel and merge. Per-node `provider.node_ensemble` with `models` / `merge` (`tournament` | `synthesize` | `vote`) / `moderator`. Interview surfaces four preset profiles (`off` / `cross_check_only` / `ideate_and_check` / `full`, cost multipliers 1.0× → 2.5×); a profile chooses which nodes fan out, never which models — you name at least two, and FI adds none. `analyze.merge: synthesize` is rejected at config load.
- **No-simulation decision** — `engine.no_simulation` → clarify `simulatability` (an answer in your own words counts by its first word: *yes, but more than 100 lines* is yes; *no idea* is not a no) → legacy `empirical_vs_theoretical` → `engine.clarify_overrides.simulatability` when the answer is missing or unreadable (also with clarify off); routes through `auto_collect_data → wait_for_data → data_load → web_plots → analyze`. In this mode the design stage produces an **analytical / observational study** (a derivation, comparison, or synthesis — no executable experiment), and the paper is written as such: it never frames a "planned experiment that wasn't run" or narrates the engine that produced it.
- **Web-derived plots** — `engine.web_derived_plots` (default on) gives the no-simulation path figures even though it runs no experiment: an LLM charts the concrete quantitative data found in the collected web/data sources and writes a self-contained matplotlib script (data inline), run in the same sandbox the simulation path uses; each figure is stamped with its source. It charts **only numbers a source actually reports** — never the model's own categorization turned into a count (no fabricated "4 of 6 → 66.7%" pies) — and emits `NO_PLOT` honestly for qualitative topics with no real numbers, rather than forcing a chart. The script is prefixed with the **Frontier Insight house figure style** (Palatino-style serif, teal-anchored palette, warm off-white ground, hairline grid, no top/right spines) so the charts match the paper / poster / slides identity instead of the default matplotlib look.
- **One downloaded-sources folder** — retrieved sources are written once to `data/literature/` (the citation record, every quest). On the no-simulation path the analysis reuses that folder directly instead of duplicating it; `data/auto_collected/` holds only the *additional* agent-collected evidence (Axon hits, dataset-adapter tables) that isn't part of the cited literature, so the two folders never hold the same files twice.
- **License-clean figure enrichment** — `knowledge.fetch_web_figures` (off by default) embeds up to `knowledge.web_figures_max` (default 2) *illustrative* figures so a study carries a visual point of view beyond its own charts: a real figure from a **cited CC-licensed arXiv paper** (the default arXiv license forbids reuse, so those papers are skipped) plus **Wikimedia Commons** diagrams under CC / public-domain. Only ever embeds free-licensed images (CC BY / CC BY-SA / CC0 / public domain — NC/ND rejected); an LLM relevance gate drops keyword-match misfires; each figure is attribution-stamped (Source + license) and recorded with its caption + license so the writer credits it and the references log its provenance.
- **Quest plan (`plan.md`)** — after the literature and skills and before the design, the `plan` step writes `<quest_root>/plan.md`: what the literature says (each source named), the gap, the model behind the numbers (for a quest that runs code: see below), success criteria, risks, what the quest will not do, the methodology audit's objections, and the design as a fenced YAML block under *The design (used as written)*. The design step adopts that block exactly (validated by `core/plan.py`: a hypothesis is required, `result_assertions` need a `path` and numeric `min` ≤ `max`), so a person's edit is what runs; without a plan file, or on a later revise pass, the design is drafted as before. The plan call is the design call with a plan directive appended, so the first pass makes the same number of model calls as before (the methodology audit still runs on it). `pauses.plan: ask` (interview question **Stop to read and edit the plan**; default `off`) stops the quest once the file is written; a `plan.md` whose design block cannot be read stops it again on every resume, with the reason. To change the plan without editing it: `launch.py --config <yaml> --resume <id> --revise-plan "<request>"` (web: the quest page's **Plan** panel; VSCode: `@fi /plan <id> <request>`), which rewrites the file through the `plan_revise` prompt, keeps the old one, and refuses a rewrite whose design block cannot be read. Before refusing, a block that is not valid YAML is repaired where that changes no key and no value (a value with `: ` in it, written as a folded block; indentation made only of tabs), and otherwise the model is asked once more for the block alone, told the line, column and parser message and shown the lines around it (only the block is sent, not the plan again); every rewrite FI asks for itself goes the same way, so one rewrite makes at most two calls. Versions live in `.fi/plan_versions/` and `needs/PLAN_HISTORY.json` (who: model / request / user); `needs/DESIGN_HISTORY.json`'s first entry names the plan hash. Web routes: `GET/PUT /api/quests/{id}/plan`, `POST/GET /api/quests/{id}/plan/revise`.
- **A measurement, or a search for the best design (`study_type`)** — `core/optimisation_plan.py`. The plan says which kind of study it is: `measure` (how a result changes over settings chosen in advance; the protocol has a `grid`) or `find_best_design` (the fin spacing and thickness with the lowest heat-sink temperature, the lightest truss cross-section under a stress limit, the controller gain with the least overshoot). When the topic's words leave it open (a word such as *best*, *optimal*, *minimise* or 最佳 without a plain "find the best …", or beside a word of measuring such as *how does* or *effect of*; *maximum likelihood* or *best response* do not count), the clarify step asks one plain question, through the same form as its other questions on the CLI, the web page and VS Code: 1 = measure how the result changes, 2 = find the best design, or leave it and the plan decides; `engine.clarify_overrides.study_type` answers it in advance when the agent answers the setup questions itself (when you are asked them it is only the suggested answer, and with `pauses.clarify: off` it is not used). A search's protocol has an `optimisation` block instead of a `grid`: `objective` (one `quantity` and its `direction`, `minimise` or `maximise`; a second goal is written as a constraint), `design_variables` (each a `name`, a `low`–`high` range and a `kind`: `continuous`, `integer`, or `choice` with its `values`), `fixed` (conditions the search may not change), `constraints` (a `quantity` and a `limit` such as `<= 120`), `baseline` (the design to beat: a value for every variable, inside its range, and its `source`), `numerical_settings` (each setting's `search` value and the finer `check` values the best designs and the baseline are recomputed at, each level at least 1.1 times finer; left out, FI uses half, then a quarter, of the search value, and plan.md says so), `evaluation_budget` (`starts` × `per_start`, a limit; optionally `seconds_per_evaluation`), `search_method` (`bounded_local`, `global_then_local`, `exhaustive`, or a library method written `scipy:<function>` / `optuna:<sampler>`, for when the quest's environment has it), an optional coarse `grid` scanned first, an optional `improvement_tolerance` (in the objective's unit, or a fraction with `mode: relative`, 0.05 = 5%; left out, a design counts as better only when it beats the baseline by more than the numerical error of the two designs) and an optional `target`. The block is checked like the rest of the protocol (a model's draft keeps its usable parts and plan.md says which were left out; a sweep beside the block becomes its coarse scan) and frozen with it. plan.md shows it in plain words under *What is being optimised*, with the number of evaluations worked out before anything runs (the search's limit, and the check's own count apart from it: the best designs and the baseline at each finer level plus two nudges of each variable that takes numbers) and, when the plan estimates one evaluation's time, the time. The design self-critique checks the baseline, the ranges and that the check settings really are finer, and asks, instead of an independent model, whether the search and the check settings differ. A search plan is never run as a plain sweep. The engine runs the search (next entry); a quest whose search cannot start stops before anything is written or run, every time it is resumed, with a card saying why: the plan has no `optimisation` block or no `evaluation_budget` (also when the person answered "find the best design" and the design does not itself say `study_type: measure`, and when a new plan is written for a topic that plainly asks to find the best design, "find the fin spacing that minimises …", not "find the lowest error of …", but the draft says nothing of its kind: the plan then says `study_type: find_best_design`, with a note), the quest keeps one script (`execution.split_analysis: false`), or its trials go to a cluster (`execution.background_jobs`). The rules for writing a search are added to the plan prompt only for a topic with a word of looking for the best, or that answer.
- **The engine runs the search for the best design** — `core/optimise.py` (FI's side: processes, budget, record) and `core/optimise_search.py` (which design comes next; standard library only). The simulation only says what one design gives (`run_cell(cell)`, or `run_trial` for a study with randomness): `cell` holds the design variables, the conditions held `fixed` and each numerical setting at its `search` value, and the returned dict holds the objective and every constrained quantity by name. FI decides which design to try next, runs each through the same harness as the trials, in a process of its own (with randomness: `runs_per_evaluation` runs with FI's seeds, the same seeds for every design, and their mean), counts every evaluation against `starts × per_start` (a design already evaluated costs nothing), and stops the scan and the search together at `execution.timeout_s` (an evaluation cut off by the clock is not a failed design). The baseline is always evaluated first, at the search's settings, before the coarse scan. The search stops when the budget runs out, when every starting point has converged or used its share of the budget, or at the time limit; the record says which. Methods: `bounded_local` (a Nelder–Mead simplex inside the ranges from each starting point, each with `per_start` evaluations; the first starting point is always the baseline, the others the best points of the coarse scan and then a Latin hypercube sample from the quest's seed), `global_then_local` (the coarse scan, or without one a differential-evolution search with half the budget, then local searches from the best points found), `exhaustive` (every combination, when every variable is whole-numbered or a choice and all fit the budget; otherwise `bounded_local`, and the record says why); `scipy:<function>` (`minimize`, which uses Nelder–Mead, or a `minimize` method such as `Powell`, and `differential_evolution`, `dual_annealing`, `direct`) and `optuna:<sampler>` (`tpe`, `random`, `cmaes`, `qmc`, `gp`) run in the quest's own Python, one step at a time, only when that environment already has the package (FI never installs it; without it the built-in `bounded_local` runs and the record says so), and never evaluate anything themselves: FI is told which design they want. The block is read through the plan's own check first (a limit written `< 120` or `≤ 120` means `<= 120`; a limit that cannot be read is never counted as met). A design that breaks a limit is recorded and never chosen; a failed evaluation (an exception, a missing or non-finite number) is recorded and never chosen; ten failures in a row before any design worked (the coarse scan's included) stop the search as a broken simulation and send `simulate.py` to the repair step. A coarse scan (`optimisation.grid`) runs right after the baseline through the trial runner itself (`raw/trials.json`, `FI_TRIALS`, so the analysis can draw it as a map), counted apart from the budget.
