FI alone writes the record, after the last evaluation and from its own memory: `raw/optimisation_ledger.jsonl` (a first line describing the search, then one line per evaluation: `n`, `stage` — scan, baseline, global, local, exhaustive or library —, `start`, `design`, `objective`, `constraints`, `feasible`, `status`, `reason`, `method`, `elapsed_s`) and `results/best_design.json` (the best design that meets every limit or `null`, the baseline evaluated at the same settings, the improvement and its fraction of the baseline, and, when the plan gives an `improvement_tolerance`, whether it is beyond it (without one, whether the difference is more than numerical error is left to the check at finer settings, next entry), the method asked for and used, the evaluations used against the budget, why the search stopped and whether the budget ran out, each starting point, the seed, the search settings and the finer check settings the plan names, time taken against the plan's estimate, and, once FI has checked the best design, `checked_at_finer_settings: true` and the check's verdict under `check`). A simulation that writes either file during the search is overwritten; an analysis that changes one (or FI's record of them in `.fi/optimisation/`) afterwards gets FI's copy put back from memory, with a warning in `run.log`, and all three are watched by the audit trail. The analysis (`experiment.py`) reads them through `FI_BEST_DESIGN` and `FI_OPTIMISATION` and draws the progress of the search; FI adds its own numbers (the best design, its objective, the baseline's, the improvement) to the analysis's result under `fi_search`, and lists there, and in `run.log`, any number the analysis reports about the best design, the baseline or the improvement that FI's record does not hold. The search runs once (no replicate seeds, and its result is not treated as pooled trials) and is kept while the simulation and the modules beside it, the optimisation block and the seed are unchanged, so a repair of the analysis does not spend the budget again; a search in which no design worked is never kept (so one that failed for a package installed since runs again), one the time limit stopped is kept only for a time limit no longer than its own, and `--from run` (or an earlier step) runs it again. A simulation whose own code imports or calls an optimiser (`scipy.optimize.minimize`, Optuna, ...) is named in `run.log` and in `best_design.json` (`simulation_may_search_itself`): a warning, not a stop, since a physical model may minimise on its own (an equilibrium by its least energy). `run.log` says the evaluations used, why the search stopped, the best design, the baseline and the difference at the search's own settings, and that FI checks them at finer settings next. `code/run.py` repeats the same search without FI (not the check at finer settings) (`code/fi_search.py` is FI's search code, `code/study.json` holds the block, the method, the seed FI used and, when the time limit stopped FI's search, where it stopped) and writes the same two files in its own output folder. A scipy or Optuna method replays its steps in a new process each time, so a large budget with one takes noticeably longer than the evaluations alone; plan.md says so.
- **FI checks the best design at finer numerical settings** — `core/optimum_check.py`. A search scores every design at its own, cheapest settings, and a search is good at finding where those settings are wrong (a coarse mesh that underestimates the temperature at one fin spacing makes that spacing look best). So after the search and before the analysis, FI (never the simulation's script, which is called exactly as during the search and cannot tell it is being checked) evaluates again, each design in a process of its own through the same harness: the best design of each starting point (up to three) and the baseline, at each finer level of the numerical settings (the plan's `check` values, or FI's fixed rule, half then a quarter of the search value, twice then four times for a setting where larger is finer; the record says which). The best design is chosen at the finest level; each design's numerical error is estimated from how its value changes from level to level (the grid-convergence index: the observed order and a safety factor of 1.25 with three levels, 3 with two, 3 as well when the values are not yet converging or swing back and forth). Six checks, each with a plain sentence and its numbers: **finer settings** (every design evaluated again at each level); **limits** at the finest level with `constraint_margin` (when the search's best breaks one there, the next best that meets every limit is reported, and it says so; a design sitting on a limit is named); **better than the baseline** at the finest level: by more than the plan's `improvement_tolerance` when it gives one, otherwise by more than the numerical error of the two designs added (whether it beats that error is recorded either way); with randomness the baseline and the best design are run again with `check_runs` fresh seeds the search never used (by default as many as `runs_per_evaluation`; the same seeds for both, compared run by run), and the lower end of the improvement's 95% interval is what counts; **starting points** (whether their best designs agree at the finest level; one starting point is "not checked"; a different design within the numerical error means several designs are about as good); **nearby designs** (each variable that takes numbers nudged both ways by 2% of its range, or one whole number: a better neighbour means the search had not finished; a nudge that changes nothing means the simulation may not use that variable; a design at the edge of its range may be better outside it, and widening the range is a change to the plan); **search budget** (whether the search converged, or its budget or time ran out first). One verdict: `verified` (the improvement holds at finer settings), `improvement_not_shown` (for example the improvement over the baseline disappears at finer settings), `infeasible` (no design found meets every limit there), `not_local_optimum`, or `unverified` (the best design could not be checked at finer settings: the check's own time limit, `execution.timeout_s`, ran out; the baseline or the best designs failed at the finer settings; the numerical error could not be estimated, because a level gave no value or, with randomness, there is only one finer level; with randomness, fewer than two fresh runs per design; or the plan names no numerical setting: a search whose plan names none is never `verified`, even with randomness and fresh runs). When the design reported is not the one the search chose (that one breaks a limit, failed, or is worse at the finer settings), the verdict's sentence says so and names it. The check never stops the quest, under any rigor profile: a check that fails is the study's result, and limits the evidence level (see the evidence ladder entry). Its evaluations are counted apart from the search's budget (plan.md shows the most it will use). FI writes `needs/OPTIMUM_CHECK.json` (the settings of each level and where they came from, every design's objective at the search's settings and at each level, its numerical error and how it was estimated, the constraints with their slack, the improvement with its threshold or error, the interval with randomness and the seeds used, each check's status and sentence, the verdict, the evaluations used against the plan's count, the hash of the search's record it checked, and what the check cannot rule out: an error of the model itself); `results/best_design.json` gets the verdict, its sentence and the numbers at the finest settings under `check` (`improvement_numerical_error` is the two designs' errors added, `best_numerical_error` the best design's own, and `record_sha256` the hash of `needs/OPTIMUM_CHECK.json`); the analysis reads the file `FI_OPTIMUM_CHECK` names, and gets the sentence and checked numbers under `fi_search.check` in its result, to report beside the search's. An analysis, or any other script run through the search's runner, that changes the file (or FI's kept copy in `.fi/optimisation/check.json`) gets FI's copy put back; a new search removes an earlier search's check, as does `--from run` (or an earlier step); a check file that no longer matches the hash in `results/best_design.json` is a gap in the evidence level; the check is kept, like the search, while nothing that decides it changes and its kept copy is the one FI recorded (a check that did not finish runs again). `run.log` says the verdict, the checked best design, one line per check, where the finer settings came from and the evaluations used. The paper's own section on the best design is the next entry.
- **What a search for the best design hands over** — `core/best_design_report.py`, `core/optimise_refine.py`. **The paper** gets a section *Best design found* that the engine writes after the writer has finished, from FI's own records only (`results/best_design.json`, `needs/OPTIMUM_CHECK.json`), before the discussion: what was optimised and its limits; a table of each design variable (its range, the baseline, the best design); a table of the objective for the baseline and the best design at the search's settings and at each finer level, with the improvement at the search's settings and at the finest one with its numerical error (or its 95% interval with randomness); the limited quantities at the finest settings; how the search ran (method, evaluations used of those allowed, starting points, why it stopped, and each round asked for later); the check's verdict and each of its six checks in plain words; the target and whether it was reached at the finest settings; and the limits of the result (when not every check passed it is "the best design the search found", not one shown to be the best there is). Whatever the writer puts in its place is taken out; the writer is told the section is there and to discuss it, not restate it. The number-provenance check reads the two records, so every number in the section traces to the run (and a number in it that is not FI's is flagged like any other); the claim check leaves the section out only when it is exactly the text FI writes. **Searching further:** a refine of a search for the best design is read without a model first. "Push further", a target value of the objective ("at most 46 K"; for a quantity made as low as possible, "at least 6 K" or "6 K better than the baseline" means an improvement of that much), or more evaluations ("100 more evaluations") continues the search: the search before it is replayed from FI's own record (nothing is evaluated twice while the simulation and the plan are unchanged), then a local search runs from the best design so far with the added evaluations (one start's share, `per_start`, unless the note gives a number; what it leaves goes to new starting points), stopping as soon as a design that meets every limit reaches the target; the check at finer settings runs again, and the section, run.log and the one line say whether the target was reached there. The rounds are kept in `.fi/optimisation/continue.json` for the plan they were asked under, the evidence ladder allows the plan's budget plus what was added, and `code/run.py` continues the search the same way. A refine that asks to change what is optimised, its limits or the ranges is a new study: nothing is changed, the note is not kept, and the review pause says so (and is never accepted for you automatically); a number FI cannot read for the objective, or a target the best design already reaches at the finest settings, is asked again the same way. A number counts only when it is tied to the objective (its unit right after it, its name or meaning next to it, or "than the baseline"), and a change counts as a new study only when it names the study's own quantities or design variables, so a note about the paper ("compare the 3 designs in one table", "minimise jargon", "change the y-axis range of Figure 2") is an ordinary refine. A refine that asks to search further is answered by the search alone (send a change to the text as a refine of its own); the search's own target is the person's moved by the gap the check measured between the search's settings and the finest ones. **The files:** `results/best_design.md` (the section as a file of its own), `results/best_design.json`, `raw/optimisation_ledger.jsonl` and `needs/OPTIMUM_CHECK.json`. At the end of a run the CLI prints `[FI] best design: ...` (the best design, its improvement with its numerical error, the check's verdict, the target) and `[FI] best design files: ...`, and `frontier_insight_summary.json` has them under `best_design`; the web quest page shows the same line with a link to each file (the quest API's `best_design`), and the VS Code chat shows both lines. Every review pause of such a study says how to ask for more.
- **Experiment protocol (`protocol` in `plan.md`)** — the plan's design block may carry a `protocol`: `grid` (each parameter and every value it takes: numbers, or names such as `method: [euler, rk4]`; one parameter is all numbers or all names; a grid FI cannot read is reported when the plan is drafted, in plain words, and a script that reads its setting is not run without one), `runs_per_setting`, `thresholds`, and the prose `seed_policy`, `ci_method` and `acceptance` (`core/plan.py::normalize_protocol` checks the numbers' shape; other keys are kept). The plan directive asks for it and says the topic's own numbers must appear in it, and lists the plan mistakes that stopped real quests before they are made (a classifying threshold written as a grid axis, a range written as two grid values, an oracle tolerance below the method's own error, an order read outside the asymptotic regime, two methods compared on different quantities, slow oracle checks, a relative tolerance around 0); `core/protocol_check.py::plan_notes` lists under *Checks already made* any number the topic sets (braces, run counts) that the protocol does not hold. When `implement` has written the script (and after the seed repair), `_enforce_protocol` compares it with the protocol using only the AST, and reports a difference only on evidence: a **grid** axis is a list, tuple, numeric range or `for` loop over literals that the script names after the axis (`R0_LIST` for `R0`) or, unnamed, shares at least half its values with it, and differs when none of those lists holds exactly the protocol's values; **runs per setting** differs when the script names counts of runs (`NUM_RUNS`, `n_samples`, `TRIALS`, ...) and none is the protocol's (a count of zero is not read as one: it is where a tally starts, `n_runs = 0` ... `n_runs += 2`); a **threshold** differs when its value (or that value as a percentage) appears nowhere. Assignments under `if os.environ.get("FI_PILOT")` (the engine's smoke test, which discards its numbers) are not read, in either polarity. A difference sends the script back for up to `engine.protocol_repair_attempts` (default 2) repairs, one script at a time, made with the `execute_reflect` template under the node name `implement_protocol` (so each is visible in `cost.jsonl`); a difference that names no script (the script holds no such list at all) goes to `simulate.py` in a two-script quest, where the design's loops are, and a repair call that got no answer (a provider timeout) is tried once more without counting; a rewrite is kept only if it parses and has fewer differences than the script it replaces. If it still differs: `engine.protocol_check: block` (default) stops the quest (an interrupt with `protocol_stage`, pause kind `protocol`, its own `NEXT_STEP.md` naming each difference; `warn` logs and records it and goes on; `off` never looks). On resume a script that now agrees with the plan (edited by hand, or after a plan edit) is adopted as written and not generated again; one that still differs is written again. Every attempt is recorded in `needs/PROTOCOL_CHECK.json`, and the review's advisory notes (`goal_coverage_notes`) repeat any difference the final scripts have, which catches a constant changed by a later repair after a failed run. Nothing is checked without a `protocol` in the design, for a no-simulation or survey study, or in `--analyze` mode. What it does not do: it reads literals only (a grid built from a computed expression is no evidence either way), it cannot prove a script that agrees, and a repair by `execute_reflect` after the script is written is reported to the reviewer but does not stop the run. Fixture: the three stored SIR runs (`tests/fixtures/protocol_runs/`); the checker flags the one that swept `R0` over `{0.8, 1.2, 2.0, 3.0}` and `N` over `{100, 500, 1000, 5000}` for a topic that asked for `{0.9, 1.5, 3.0}` and `{100, 1000, 5000}`, and passes the other two.
- **Precision from a target, streams that are not shared by accident, and a grid dense enough for its claim** — the protocol's `precision` (`target_half_width`, optionally a `metric` and a `reason`; `core/plan.py::normalize_protocol` checks the shape) says the 95% half-width the headline probability needs. `core/stats.py::trials_for_half_width` gives the trials a probability near 0.5 needs (`z²·p·(1−p)/h²`: 1068 for ±0.03, 385 for ±0.05) and `wilson_half_width` the width trials give. The plan's *Checks already made* (`core/protocol_check.py::precision_notes`) compares them with the trials the protocol plans (runs per setting times the seeds the engine runs, `engine.execute_replicates`) and says how many runs per seed the target needs; a protocol with runs and no target is told what those runs give. After the run, `_annotate_precision` puts each pooled proportion's `ci_half_width`, the target and `precision_reached` in the analysis prompt, and a `precision_note` (which the analyze prompt turns into a limitation) when a target was missed. `grid_notes` says when a parameter the design's hypothesis, method or expected outcome makes a claim about (convergence, scaling, a threshold, a transition, a limit) has fewer than five values. The protocol check gained a fourth kind of difference, **`rng`**: `rng_reuse` finds a function with arguments that builds a random generator (`default_rng`, `RandomState`, `Random`, `np.random.seed`, `SeedSequence`, ...) from something that does not depend on them, and is called from a loop or more than once, so every call restarts the same stream and the settings share their random numbers (one stored run did exactly this, its `run_stochastic_experiment` called for every cell); it goes through the same repair and stop as a grid difference, and does not apply when the protocol's `seed_policy` says common random numbers, paired, a shared stream or coupled runs. On the three stored SIR runs only the one that rebuilt its stream is flagged. Not done: sequential stopping (adding runs until the target is reached), and a generator that varies with a value the script computes without using its arguments is not detected.
- **OpenAI-compatible models that fix their sampling** — `provider.fixed_temperature` (0 to 2) is sent on every HTTP call in place of the per-node temperature (0 for the deciding nodes, 0.2 for the others), and `provider.extra_body` is merged into every HTTP request body before a per-call `extra`; both ride on the resolved endpoint, so every client built from it (the engine's, the slides / poster / speech generators') applies them, and the fallback chain resets them (they belong to the primary's model). Moonshot's Kimi K2.6 and K3 need this: they answer any temperature but one with HTTP 400 (1 with thinking on, 0.6 with it off) and `extra_body: {thinking: {type: disabled}}` turns the reasoning off (one measured reply: 32 output tokens with thinking on, 4 with it off). `examples/kimi_moonshot/config.yaml` is a working example; the key comes from the environment variable named by `api_key_env`. Calls to Moonshot's API (`api.moonshot.ai` / `.cn`) are streamed and put back together, because long requests sent whole stalled on that route until they timed out while the same requests streamed completed; the step's timeout still bounds the whole call, so a stream kept open by keep-alives or trickling for longer than that is stopped and tried again, and an error sent inside the stream is stopped at once when its type or code names a class retrying cannot fix (a content filter, a key or permission problem, an exhausted quota or balance, a malformed or too-long request), whatever its message says, and is otherwise tried again (a rate limit, an overload, a 429 or 5xx code, or anything unrecognised). The choice is made by the host alone: a relay or proxy in front of Kimi (another `base_url`) is sent the request whole, like every other provider. `stream` cannot be set in `extra_body` (the config is refused when it loads): FI decides it.
- **An answer the model did not finish is never used as if whole** — every HTTP answer (streamed or not) is read for why it ended (`finish_reason`, kept with its cost in the call's line of `.fi/model_calls.jsonl`, and in `.fi/cost.jsonl` for an attempt that was cut off, since it was paid for). An answer cut off at its output limit (`length`, or `max_tokens` from some relays) is never passed on: a partial script, plan or paper would look whole. A step can be given its own output limit, `provider.node_max_tokens` (the same step names as `node_models`; unset, no limit is sent, as before); a call cut off at a limit it set is asked once more with twice it (at most 65,536 tokens; a model that refuses the larger limit is treated as cut off, not as an error). A model that refuses a limit (a 4xx about the size) is named the same way, as a refused limit. The stop happens in the review steps too (the reviewers, the moderator, the claim check, the cross-check): a cut-off there is not read as a reviewer outage. The card suggests twice what the cut-off answer used (at most 65,536), lists the step names (`plan`, `implement`, `write`, `review`, `review_panel`, `claim_check`, `cross_check`, `analyze`), offers another model rather than a limit for a CLI provider, notes that Ollama may also stop when its context window (`num_ctx`) is full (not checked), and says to approve a `node_models` change with `--update`. The output limits are recorded in the seal's provider settings. Still cut off, or cut off at the model's own limit, the quest **stops for you** (a `model_output` pause, on the CLI, the web quest page and VS Code alike) naming the step and saying what to change: a larger `provider.node_max_tokens.<step>` or another `provider.node_models.<step>`; resuming runs that step again with the new setting. An answer the provider withheld with its content filter (`content_filter`) stops the same way (another model for the step, or different wording), without asking the same provider again (a fallback provider, when set, is tried first). Neither counts against a provider's health: the fallback chain does not set it aside for them. Any other reason is taken as finished and logged. The `claude` CLI resumes an answer it cut at its limit by itself; one whose last turn still stopped at the limit stops the same way. The output limit is not sent to the CLIs or the VS Code bridge, and the other CLIs and the bridge do not say why an answer ended, so there it cannot be checked.
- **Another model than the one you chose is never accepted silently** — `core/provider.py::LLMClient._ask_the_asked_model_again`. The `claude` CLI can answer with another model on its own: when the model you named declines a request it hands the request to another model (a Haiku quest's `ideate` call was answered by a Fable model at about twelve times Haiku's price), and with a fallback model set up for the CLI it also switches when the model is unavailable. FI reads which model wrote the answer from the CLI's own stream (the model of the answer's message, not the model that wrote the most tokens on the side), records it in `.fi/model_calls.jsonl` (`served_model`, and `switched_from` when the answer kept is another model's), and says so in `.fi/run.log` every time it happens: which model declined or was unavailable, which answered, and which answer was kept. After a refusal it asks the model you named once more with the CLI's switching turned off (`CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_REFUSAL_FALLBACK=1` for that one call): its answer is kept when it gives one (for a quest step, the other model's answer is recorded as a paid attempt that was not used, with its token counts in `.fi/cost.jsonl`); when it declines again, the other model's answer is kept and the line says the call cost that model's price. After any other switch FI does not ask again (the CLI would only switch again) and keeps the answer with the same line. A reply that is only a refusal, with no other model answering, is never passed on as an answer and is not asked again: the quest stops for you (a `model_output` pause) to pick another model for the step. This holds on every rigor profile.
- **A required check's unreadable answer is asked for once more, short** — `Engine._chat_json`, `agents/json_reanswer.md`. The design's methodology audit, the evidence gate and the claim check each need one JSON object back. The reply is read for that object even when the model wrote several (a real audit reply printed the amended design alone and then the asked-for object); when none can be used (no such object, an empty reply, a gate verdict FI does not know), the same request is asked once more with a short instruction: one object of the asked-for shape, printed once, no fence, no prose, each objection or reason in at most two sentences (a short first reply is shown back so the same points can be restated; a long one is not repeated). A second unusable answer is a check that could not judge, as before: under `rigor_profile: research` the quest stops once for it. The audit's own prompt also asks for the object printed once. On an HTTP provider, `provider.node_max_tokens.design_self_critique` (and the other step names) caps a step's answer length; FI sends no limit of its own, since a model that only takes another limit field would refuse one.
- **Numeric warnings fail closed** — `core/numeric_warnings.py::scan` reads a run's stderr and its printed results for the warnings that mean the numbers may not be what the script claims, and only these: `RuntimeWarning` overflow / invalid value / divide by zero, `IntegrationWarning` / `OptimizeWarning` / `ConvergenceWarning` / `LinAlgWarning` and their text ("maximum number of subdivisions", "not making good progress"), an argument that "has no effect for a chosen solver" or is unexpected, unrecognised or ignored (SciPy's `solve_ivp` given `abs_tol` instead of `atol` runs with its default and warns), `ComplexWarning`, and a NaN or infinity in a result. `DeprecationWarning`, `FutureWarning`, font, legend, layout and matplotlib warnings and "Mean of empty slice" do not count; each warning is named once and at most 8 are kept. `_node_execute` scans the primary run (`exec_result["numeric_warnings"]`), logs each as `[numeric]` and appends it to `needs/NUMERIC_WARNINGS.json`. With `engine.numeric_warnings: block` (default) `execute_reflect` treats the warnings like a crash or an implausible value: the script is sent back with the warnings and a request that names the SciPy tolerance arguments, forbids silencing the warning (`warnings.filterwarnings`, `np.seterr(all='ignore')`) and allows an `np.errstate` guard only around one intended operation, with a stated reason, all inside the same `exec_reflect_max_iterations` budget. If the warnings remain when the budget is spent, the quest stops instead of proceeding to analyze (an interrupt with `numeric_stage`, pause kind `numeric`, its own `NEXT_STEP.md`), with the hash of the scripts recorded in `.fi/numeric_stop.json`: on resume a script that changed is run again (and may stop again), and one that did not is accepted as it is (`numeric_warnings_accepted` in the state, cleared by the next run of the experiment), the warnings staying on record. `warn` only logs and records; `off` does not look. It reads the primary run only (replicates' stderr is not scanned), and it reads no warning it has no pattern for. If the scanner itself raises, that is recorded as `{"error": ...}` in `NUMERIC_WARNINGS.json` rather than as a clean `[]` — a crashed scan is not the same claim as "no warnings found", and `core/evidence.py` names it a gap toward `protocol_runtime_matched` regardless of `engine.numeric_warnings`, since a check that never ran cannot stand in for one that ran and passed.
- **Oracles the script must pass before its main run** — the plan's protocol declares `oracles` (each a `name`, a `check` saying what is compared with what on which small case, a numeric `expected` and `tolerance` the engine judges it by, and optionally `tolerance_mode`, a `kind`, a `reference`, a `case` and `measure` FI runs itself, and an `order`; `core/plan.py::normalize_protocol` checks their shape; the plan directive asks for at least one, of one of six kinds, with a `reference` saying where its expected value comes from: see *The model behind the numbers* below). For a quest that keeps one script (`split_analysis: false`) the implement prompts give the contract (`core/oracle_check.py`): when `FI_ORACLE` is `1` the script does not run its sweep, it runs each declared check on a small fast case, prints one `ORACLE_JSON: {"checks": [{"name", "value", "diagnostics"}]}` line and exits 0 (it measures; the engine judges, see the next entry). `_oracle_gate` runs that at the start of `execute`, before the pilot and the main run (on `simulate.py` for a two-script quest whose simulation is still a whole script with no `run_trial` / `run_cell` function (a simulation that has one gets no `FI_ORACLE` run of the whole script: FI measures its oracles through that function, see the next entry), given its own dedicated, disposable `FI_RAW_DIR` — never a real seed's raw folder — since the split-experiment directive's own worked example reads that variable unconditionally at module level, before the script has even checked `FI_ORACLE`; a live campaign found two of four real quests failing this pre-check on a bare `KeyError` for exactly that reason, see docs/audits/16_live_red_team_campaign.md), and reads the answer: a problem is any of (a) the protocol declares no oracle, (b) no `ORACLE_JSON` line, (c) a declared oracle not among the checks, (d) a check whose value is missing, not a finite number or outside the protocol's tolerance (or that the script itself reports as failed), (e) a non-zero exit, (f) a timeout (the pilot's). A protocol with no oracle first asks for the plan to be rewritten with one (the same rewrite as `--revise-plan`, so the version is kept and `plan.md` is what is read afterwards); a missing or failing check asks for a repair of the script, one at a time, with the `execute_reflect` template under the node name `implement_oracle` (given the oracle run's own stderr, so a crash elsewhere in the script is seen, not only "no `ORACLE_JSON` line") (kept only if it parses and honours `FI_ORACLE`, or, for a quest whose `simulate.py` defines `run_trial` / `run_cell`, defines `oracle()` unless every oracle has a `case`; the request forbids loosening a tolerance, skipping a check or hard-coding its answer, and asks which of the simulator and the check is wrong; when the repair judges the check itself wrong -- a tolerance below the method's own error at that step, an order read far from its asymptotic regime -- it may return `oracle_change` with the numbers and the reason, which is recorded in `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json` (`proposed_changes`). It is never applied (the repair saw the run); FI corrects a check only when two workings independent of the measurement agree (the plan's own arithmetic and another model's blind recheck). When every check still failing is a disputed one, no more repairs are spent: `block` goes on with them marked unconfirmed; `warn` goes on with that script; either way the record lists them under `disputed`, and the analysis note (and so the paper) says the check failed and that its expected value is disputed. When other problems remain, the next request lists only those, names the disputed checks and says not to change the script for them; its fix is applied (a repair that only repeats an already-recorded dispute, or disputes a check that passes, is still used), but if it makes a disputed check pass (one that was failing or not measured when it was asked), the script is put back as it was before that repair (`reverted_disputed_changed`). A proposal about a check that was passing is shown to the person but does not excuse that check failing later. The engine's own verdicts reach `analyze` as a note above the run's output, so a script that re-judges its oracles in `RESULT_JSON` with an out-of-date tolerance is not what the paper reports. Up to `engine.oracle_repair_attempts` (default 2) plan rewrites and, separately, as many rewrites of the script (a repair call that got no answer, a provider timeout, is tried once more without counting); then `engine.oracle_check: block` (default) never stops the quest to ask: after FI's repairs and corrections it goes on with each check still failing marked unconfirmed (see *A known-answer check never stops the quest to ask a person*), `warn` logs and records it and goes on, `off` never looks. On resume the checks run again before anything else, against the script on disk and the protocol in `plan.md`. Every attempt is recorded in `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json`. Nothing is checked without a `protocol` in the design, for a background job that is still one script (its driver would submit the job; a two-script one is checked through its simulation's functions), for a no-simulation or survey study, or in `--analyze` mode. The protocol (for the oracle gate and the implement-time protocol check) is now read from `plan.md` on the first pass, so an edit made to it while a quest was stopped for the protocol takes effect on resume. What it does not do: it cannot tell a strong oracle from a weak one (a check the script can always pass is asked not to be written, not detected), and a repair by `execute_reflect` later does not rerun it.
- **Oracles are judged by the engine** — `core/oracle_check.py`. A protocol oracle is `{name, check, kind, expected, tolerance, tolerance_mode (absolute|relative), reference}`; by default FI runs the simulation on each oracle's `case` itself (see *FI runs the trials* below), and in a one-script quest (`split_analysis: false`), with `FI_ORACLE=1` the script measures each one and prints `ORACLE_JSON: {"checks": [{"name", "value", "diagnostics"}]}`, and `oracle_check.problems` computes `abs(value - expected) <= tolerance` (or `tolerance * abs(expected)`) from the frozen protocol's numbers. A `passed`, `expected` or `tolerance` in the script's line is not a verdict (a script that reports a check as failed itself is still a problem); a value that is missing, not a finite number or outside the tolerance fails the oracle. `oracle_check.unjudgeable` names an oracle with no numeric `expected` and `tolerance` (or a relative tolerance around 0): it is not run, the plan is rewritten to give it numbers while it is a draft (`_declare_oracles(incomplete)`), `plan.md` says so (`protocol_check.oracle_notes`), and after the freeze it stops the quest. `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json` has `judged_by: engine` (and `contract: trial` for a quest whose `simulate.py` defines `run_trial` / `run_cell`) and, per attempt, `judged` (value, expected, tolerance, limit, `passed_by_engine`, `script_said`, and `measured_by`: `engine` when FI ran the simulation on the oracle's case, `script` when the value came from the script's `oracle()` or `FI_ORACLE=1` run); an oracle's case runs with the environment a trial of the main run gets (never `FI_ORACLE`), so the simulation cannot tell it is being checked (`trial_runner.measure_oracles`); `core/evidence.py` does not count an oracle record without `judged_by: engine` (a quest begun before this), and never counts a value the script reported itself toward `independently_validated`, whatever the layout: an oracle without a `case`, and every oracle of a one-script quest (measured by the script's own `FI_ORACLE=1` run), is a gap that names the oracle and says what makes FI measure it (the simulation in its own script, which `execution.split_analysis: auto` gives every simulation, and the oracle given a `case` and a `measure`; the plan is asked for a `case` and a `measure` on every oracle of a deterministic simulation; for a random one a case is one trial, so only a check one trial shows exactly gets one, and a check that needs many trials, such as a probability, stays the script's own `oracle()` and keeps the quest below this level). A record that says `ok` is not taken on its word either: its last attempt must hold at least one oracle value FI measured by running the simulation and passed (`oracle_check.engine_passed`), none that got no verdict or failed (`oracle_check.not_passed`), and every oracle the protocol declares among the ones measured and passed; otherwise the gap says the oracle check judged no value, or names the oracle that was not passed or not measured.
- **A known-answer check never stops the quest to ask a person** — `Engine._oracle_gate` with `_correct_expected_values` and `_goes_on_by_itself`. Whether a check or the simulation is wrong is FI's to work out. A check FI's own look (next entries) finds wrong is corrected only when two workings independent of each other agree on the new value within the check's own tolerance (`Engine._may_correct`, `_second_source`): the plan's own arithmetic, worked out by FI, and another model that never saw the result and whose own working adds up. Either alone never changes a check: it is set aside from the repairs and goes on unconfirmed. A correction is made in `plan.md` before the freeze, at most once per check: only its `expected`, never its tolerance, never to the measured value; a plan version by the engine, an audit event (`oracle_correction`), `.fi/oracle_corrections.json`, and one console line ("FI corrected an expected value the plan had worked out wrongly: ..."); the checks are then measured again. The simulation is repaired when it is what is wrong (a check set aside as wrong is never "fixed" in the simulation). Otherwise the quest goes on by itself, every quest including `rigor_profile: research`: each check still failing is marked *unconfirmed* (one that measured nothing too, `accepted_checks.went_on_entries`), recorded as automatic and bound to the check and the code's version, the result counts as exploratory and below *independently validated*, the paper says so, and `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json` carries `explained` (`leaning`, `why`) and the console says why in one line. A recheck by the model that wrote the plan is never counted as independent evidence.
- **The card when a known-answer check stops the quest** (a stop written by an earlier FI; the card's `leaning` and `why` now explain the automatic decision) — `core/oracle_card.py`. When the oracle gate stops the quest (no check, a check with no numbers, a value that could not be measured, a value outside its tolerance, a check a repair called wrong, or a failure after the freeze), FI writes one structured card. It goes into `.fi/pause.json` (`card`, with the gate's `problems` and the repairs' `proposed_changes`) and `.fi/todo.json`. `core/todo.py::card_lines` renders it into `NEXT_STEP.md` and the terminal (printed when the run ends). The web quest page shows the same text and adds a button per action: **Let FI try again** resumes, a change to a check fills the Plan box, and **Mark the check unconfirmed and go on** opens the name box. The VS Code chat shows it with the same buttons, which put `@fi /resume`, `@fi /plan` or `@fi /accept-checks` in the chat box. Per check that did not pass, the card shows: its plain name (from `check`), id and kind; the expected value and its `reference`; the measured value and who measured it (FI calling `run_cell` / `run_trial` on the check's case, the script's own `oracle()`, or a one-script run with `FI_ORACLE=1`); the tolerance; the gap (absolute, times the tolerance, and the measured value as a multiple of the expected one); the case and measure; and the file and line where the script computes the number, with a short excerpt when it can be found. A reported value that is not a finite number (nan, inf) is shown as it was reported. What the gate found about a check with no number is listed under it, and anything it found about no listed check (a passing check the script reports as failed, a non-zero exit) under *Also found*. The numbers carry enough significant digits to show a gap the tolerance cares about (0.36787977 against 0.36787946 with a tolerance of 1e-12, not 0.36788 against 0.367879), also in the gate's own problem sentences, which now say "FI measured" or "the script measured" as it was. When nothing was measured, the card shows the last exception and the file and line in the quest's code, and says when the same error came back after every repair. The most likely causes come from what FI already has: a repair's proposed change with its reason (and whether accepting it would simply make the measured value pass), the test run's size verdict (`oracle_forms.mismatch`: about 100 times off, exactly 0, the other sign), two checks or two reported values under one name (`oracle_check.duplicate_names`, also a warning in `run.log`; the verdict is unchanged), or, when nothing points either way, both possibilities with the source to read first. The card also lists what FI already tried (each repair and its own summary, kept as `patch_summary` in `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json`) and two or three ways on. *Let FI try again* (not offered when every failing check is disputed or set aside, since a resume keeps that finding) says that a resume repairs the script up to `engine.oracle_repair_attempts` more times (for a check with no numbers it asks the plan instead, and after the freeze, where that would only stop again, it is not offered). *Fix it yourself* names the file and line. *Have the check worked out again* (before the freeze) fills in a `--revise-plan` request that asks for the expected value to be worked out again from its source, never set to the measured one; with a proposal, the card offers that change instead. *Mark the check unconfirmed and go on* is offered whenever every problem the stop found is a check that was measured and failed (`core/accepted_checks.py::offer`, the one rule the gate and the card share), before or after the freeze and under `rigor_profile: research` too: `--accept-checks <id> --approve-as <you>`, the web button, `@fi /accept-checks <id>`. Its text repeats the check's expected value and tolerance, case, measure, the measured value, and the script with its version; when it is not offered (nothing was measured, a check has no numbers, the run has another problem) a note on the card says why. It replaces the earlier advice to set `engine.oracle_check: warn`, which on a quest the interview wrote stopped the quest a second time to approve the changed setting; that advice remains only for a check with no numbers after the freeze, where it is the way to the review's amendment, with `--update` named up front. The repair request on the two-script layout states that layout's contract (FI calls the simulation; there is no `FI_ORACLE` and no `ORACLE_JSON`), and the console line no longer tells you to edit `plan.md` when the script crashed. Nothing on the card changes a verdict.
- **FI's own look before a known-answer check stops the quest** — `core/oracle_triage.py`, run by `Engine._oracle_gate` before the first repair of each failing check. (1) The expected value is worked out again by another model (prompt `agents/oracle_recompute.md`, node `oracle_review.recompute`, answered by `provider.node_models.oracle_review`, else the planner's model, which the record says), from the check's statement, case, measure and reference, never the measured value. One call per version of a check, kept in `.fi/oracle_triage.json`. A value that disagrees with the plan's and lies near the measurement (within the tolerance, or within 1.5 times where the plan is ten times or more away) disputes the expected value; the worst violation of a rule, which expects 0 by its kind's form, is never disputed this way. A call that got no answer is not kept. It becomes a proposal with the check's own tolerance, and the script is not rewritten for it. (2) Under the trial contract (FI runs the simulation on the check's case itself), a deterministic check whose case sets a step (`dt`, `h`, `dx`, `n_steps` …) is run at half and a quarter of the step (a case that sets both a step and a count of steps is not), which gives the observed order; the value is extrapolated to step 0 (Richardson, with the declared `order` when there is one). When the observed order matches the declared one (within 0.5), or, with none declared, is 1.5 or more on a rule's worst violation expecting 0, and the limit agrees with the expected value (within the tolerance, or the smaller of a tenth of the last step's change and a thousandth of the expected value, or of the gap when 0 is expected), the tolerance is tighter than the method's own error at that step, and the check is set aside from the repairs. Another order, a convergence with no order declared on any other check, another limit or no convergence are said on the card and set nothing aside. A case that sets a step together with a count of steps or grid points is not run. (3) A random check is run with three more seeds; a spread larger than the tolerance, with the first trial's gap within three times the spread and the mean within three standard errors of the expected value, is one trial's noise and is set aside the same way (a check set aside as noise that passes by chance after a repair to another check does not undo that repair). A rule every trial must keep exactly whose trials differ is called the fault. (4) `oracle_forms.unit_multiple` names, on the card, a value at least ten tolerances off that would pass after ×/÷100, 1000, 10^6, 10^9, 2π, the reciprocal or a count of the case (`n`, `n_*`, `*_count`, `agents` …); the test run before the study sends the plan only the fixed factors. (5) The run's last exception goes into the repair request and the card, and the same exception after a repair stops the repairs early (only when no check with a measured value is left to repair). (6) A run of the checks that runs out of time is run once more at twice the time. Each finding is recorded under `attempts[].triage` in `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json`, shown on the card (*Most likely*, *What FI already tried*), and counted (`self_checks`; one line in `plan.md`). Disputes are read back from that record on a resume while the check's fingerprint is unchanged (`fingerprints`). A failing check still fails: nothing here changes a verdict.
- **Going on although a known-answer check failed** — `core/accepted_checks.py` (`offer`, `accept_failing`, `went_on_by`, `gap`, `failing_disclosure`), honoured by `Engine._oracle_gate` (`_chosen_go_on`, `_goes_on_by_itself`). The gate goes on by itself (see *A known-answer check never stops the quest to ask a person*), recorded as `automatic`; a choice a person made at a stop an earlier FI wrote is still honoured: a person marked a check that was measured and failed *unconfirmed* and went on, with a name: `--accept-checks <id> --approve-as <you>` (the same flag as for checks with no stated source; which stop it answers is read from `.fi/pause.json`), the quest page's **Go on with it marked unconfirmed** button (`POST /api/quests/{id}/plan/accept-checks`; the quest payload carries `failed_checks`), or `@fi /accept-checks <id>`. The card, the web box and the VS Code command show the check's conditions and the script with its version before the name is given; the CLI prints them again when it records the choice. The stop writes the offer into `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json` (`go_on`: the checks with their fingerprint, the script and `script_version`, a hash of the script the checks ran, the helper modules beside it and the model's package in `code/`); the choice goes into `needs/FAILED_CHECKS_ACCEPTED.json`. On the next run of the gate the choice is honoured right after the checks are measured, before any plan change or repair, and only while the check's fingerprint (name, expected, tolerance, its mode, case, measure) and the code's version are as they were; otherwise `run.log` and the console say the choice no longer applies and the check is judged again. Not offered when nothing was measured (the card says why). Every quest goes on by itself after FI's repairs and corrections, recorded as `automatic` (a check that measured nothing too: `went_on_entries`), and the to-do card lists it under the things worth a look. Either way `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json` gets status `went_on_failing` and `went_on` (who, or automatic), the audit trace's oracle entry names who, the evidence keeps a plain gap below `independently_validated` ("<name> chose to go on although the known-answer check '<check>' failed (...)", or "FI went on by itself although ..."), so the result is never publication-ready, the analysis still gets the engine's verdicts, and the writer is told to say the check failed.
- **The protocol check ignores the known-answer checks' own runs** — `core/protocol_check.py`: run counts and lists inside `oracle()`, in the `FI_ORACLE` branch, named for the oracle (`ORACLE_RUNS`), or read only by `oracle()` are not compared with `runs_per_setting` or the grid.
- **The model behind the numbers, and where each expected value comes from** — a sweep's results have no ground truth; what can be checked is the model that produces them. For a quest that runs code, the plan states it in the protocol (`protocol.model`, frozen with the rest): `summary` (what model produces the numbers, in a sentence), `assumptions`, `holds_for` (the range where it holds) and `equations`, each with an `id` (E1, E2…), the `formula` as the source writes it, its `role` (`generates`: the simulation computes the data with it; `analyses`: it is used on the results) and its `source` (a source this quest retrieved, by the [n] the plan's literature list uses, or `derivation` with the steps written in `derivation`). `plan.md` shows it as a readable section, *The model behind the numbers*, above the design block, after a numbered list of the sources the quest found (*The sources this quest found*, the [n] a check or an equation cites; not read back), with each check against a known answer and where its expected value comes from; the block is what counts, and an edit belongs there. `core/plan.py::normalize_model` reads the block (an equation a draft wrote that cannot be read is left out and the plan says so; one without an id is given the next free one). Each oracle's `kind` is one of six (`special_case`, `invariant`, `symmetry`, `second_implementation`, `convergence_rate`, `published_value`; the older `closed_form`, `limiting_case`, `exact_small_case` and `independent_implementation` are read as the kind they mean, and the plan keeps what was written), and the engine now reads its `reference` (`core/oracle_check.py::source_gaps`): it must be `derivation: <how the value follows>` with at least one equation (`=`, or a relation such as `≈` or `<`; `integral of S over the domain = 1, because S is normalised`; a value true by definition is written the same way, `sum S = 1 by definition`; steps that take the value from a book, a table or an author and year the quest did not retrieve do not count), a retrieved source by its [n] (a group such as [1, 3] or a range [1–3] counts; an index such as y[10] does not, nor a list written as code, `y0=[1, 2]`, `t_span = [1, 10]` or `solve_ivp(f, [1, 10], y)`, nor a group with a 0 in it such as the interval [0, 1]; a number that is not in the quest's list is named with the numbers that are, "[0] is not one of the sources this quest found (they are [1]–[14])"), its DOI, or its title when it has five words or more, an equation of the model whose own source counts, or, for a second implementation, a sentence saying what code it does not share (a statement FI requires, not one it can verify). An empty reference, or one that names a source the quest did not retrieve (one recalled from memory), is noted in the plan's *Checks already made*, warned about in `run.log` and on the console, and is a gap below `independently_validated`. What a model writes in other shapes is read, not lost: a reference under `citation`, `justification` or `derivation`, under `source` or `basis` only when what they hold looks like one (a `[n]`, a DOI, a derivation, an `E1`: in optics the `source` is the thing simulated), or inside the check's text (`(derivation: ...)` or `(reference: ...)`, never `source:`); a quest frozen before its sources were kept is still judged on `reference` alone; a kind under `type`, or in words such as "normalization" (an invariant) or "horizontal symmetry"; a kind or reference written as a list; a model whose parts are under `description`, `assumes` or `governing_equations`; equations written as lines (`E1: ...`) or as a mapping of E1 to its formula (`core/oracle_check.py::reference_of`, `kind_of`, `model_view`; the plan keeps what was written, so a frozen protocol hashes as before). One check a draft wrote that cannot be read is left out with a note naming it, not the whole list (`core/plan.py::repair_protocol`). Under `rigor_profile: research`, before the quest stops for this (or for a model left empty) FI asks the plan step once (`.fi/plan_sources_asked.json`) to fill exactly that in (`Engine._fill_plan_sources`); a rewrite that changes anything else (a check's name, numbers, case or measure, the grid, the hypothesis) is put back, and a source it wrote into a check after the person read the plan is named in the frozen protocol's approval line (unless the person reads the plan at a stop again first). If something is still missing the quest goes on by itself, each such check marked "source not confirmed" (`accepted_checks.go_on_by_itself`, recorded as `automatic`); nothing is asked of a person. Each such check stays a gap below `independently_validated`, marked "source not confirmed" (in `needs/UNSOURCED_CHECKS_ACCEPTED.json`, the audit trace and the frozen protocol's `approved_by`), and the writer is told to say so plainly; a check added later, or one whose `expected`, `tolerance`, `case` or `measure` changes afterwards, is marked again (`accepted_checks.fingerprint`). A person's choice made at a stop an earlier FI wrote (`--accept-checks <quest> --approve-as <you>`) is still honoured. A rewrite of the plan (`--revise-plan`) is told what FI reads in the checks and the model and what is missing (`agents/plan_revise.md`), its *The model behind the numbers* section is shown again from the rewritten block, and the one-shot command then prints, check by check, whether each now says where its value comes from. `@fi /resume <quest> --revise-plan "..."` rewrites the plan like `@fi /plan` (it used to resume into the same stop), and any other flag after `@fi /resume` is named and nothing runs (`vscode-frontier-insight/src/resume-args.ts`). The check runs when the plan is written or resumed and again right before the protocol is frozen (the oracle gate may have added checks); the freeze record keeps the sources as they were numbered then (`sources` in `needs/FROZEN_PROTOCOL.json`), so a check's [3] means the same source after the paper renumbers its references. What is not checked: that a derivation or the reading of a source is right, and whether the code a `# E1` label marks really computes E1 (only that the label is there; see the two-script entry above).
- **One numeric form per kind of check, and how its number is computed** — `core/oracle_forms.py`. An invariant, a symmetry or scaling law and a second implementation are measured as the worst violation (absolute, or relative when the formula divides by the reference) and expect 0; a special or limiting case, a published value and a convergence rate are measured as the quantity itself. An oracle's `measure` is how its number is computed from what `run_cell`/`run_trial` returns on its `case`: one returned name (every oracle written before keeps working), or a formula of them (`abs(P_out - P_in) / P_in`) in a small language (`oracle_forms.evaluate`: numbers, the returned names, `+ - * / ** %`, parentheses, `abs sqrt exp log log10 log2 sin cos tan asin acos atan atan2 sinh cosh tanh hypot floor ceil min max sum` and `pi`), parsed with `ast` and walked, never run as code (a dot, an index, any other call, a comparison, a string are refused; every constant is a float so no power can hang). The formula is part of the frozen protocol; `trial_runner.measure_oracles` applies it to what the simulation returned (a returned name alone is read directly; a name the formula uses that was not returned is a problem for the script's repair; a formula that gives no finite real number on its case, a division by zero or a negative number under a root, is recorded as `formula_problem`), and each engine-measured check in `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json` carries `computed_as` and everything the simulation `returned` on its case. At plan time (`Engine._guide_oracles`, once per quest, marker `.fi/oracle_guidance.json`, right after `plan.md` is written and before the plan stop; also for a plan already on disk that was never looked at, whose changes then go through the stop before the freeze when the person had already read it) `oracle_forms.enforce` rewrites a violation-kind check that expects a non-zero value for a formula to `abs((formula) - expected)` expecting 0 (verdict-preserving; a relative tolerance becomes the relative violation). It rewrites only when FI runs the check itself (two scripts), the formula is not possibly one returned name written with hyphens, the name is not shared by two checks, the result stays readable, and no criterion is judged on the check's number; otherwise, and for a violation-kind check whose number is a returned name alone or the script's own, a convergence rate that does not expect an order, a formula that cannot be read, and an expected value written to 5–12 significant digits (not a whole number or a short binary fraction) with a tolerance below half its last digit, it sends one request to the plan (`plan_revise`). A formula with no returned name in it (`0`, `pi - pi`) is sent back at plan time and refused by `measure_oracles` (not a measurement). `Engine._revise_checks_only` splices into the plan as it was only what the answer changed in `protocol.oracles` and in the criteria that read a changed check, and puts back everything else (design block and prose); a failed call is said in the section, never a crash. Every engine change to the checks after the plan was written is merged into `.fi/oracles_added.json` by `Engine._note_engine_change` (checks, `removed`, `reason`), and a `--from plan`/`literature`/`ideas` rerun moves `.fi/oracle_guidance.json` aside, a rerun from the design on `.fi/oracle_dry_run.json`, so a new plan is looked at again. Rewrites, requests, what the plan changed and what it left are written under *A second look at the checks against known answers* in `plan.md` (never read back), and *The model behind the numbers* shows each check's formula and case. FI never changes the value a check is judged against itself, other than to put it in its kind's form without changing its verdict.
- **A second opinion on the checks** — `core/oracle_review.py`, `agents/oracle_review.md`, node `oracle_review` (`Engine._review_oracles`, called by `_hold_oracle_forms` in the same once-per-quest look at plan time, only when the protocol declares checks). One call reads the protocol's `model` and every oracle and returns, per check, `appropriate`, `discriminating` (with the plausible bug it tried), `well_defined` (with a note) and `better`, plus `equations_tested` / `equations_not_tested` and up to two checks to `add`. `oracle_review.parse` keeps only checks and equation ids the plan declares (an answer naming none of its checks is not used), reads a qualified yes or no ("no, mostly"), treats "none"/"N/A" as nothing, and keeps at most two proposed checks with only a check's own fields and a name the plan does not already use; a plan with more checks than fit is shown whole checks only, and then what no check tests is not read. `findings` turns what should change (a check that may not test the model, would miss a plausible bug, or has an ill-defined number; an equation no check tests; a check to add) into sentences that go back to the plan in the SAME request as the form requests (`_revise_checks_only`), framed as a reader's findings to apply only where right and never removing a check without a replacement; a better check alone is shown, not a rewrite. So the look costs at most one `oracle_review` call and one `plan_revise` request (two calls when its first reply cannot be read), a resume included: the answer and how far the look got are kept in `.fi/oracle_review.json`. `plan_lines` writes which model read the checks and its verdict on each into *A second look at the checks against known answers*; the reviewer is taken from the model that answered when the connection named it (else the one asked for, and the section says the connection did not name it). The reviewer is `provider.node_models.oracle_review` (temperature 0, like the other verdict nodes; the same time limits as the methodology audit); when that is unset or the planner's own model, the section and `run.log` say it was the same model's second look (or that FI cannot tell), and under `rigor_profile: research` how to name another. A failed call, or an answer the provider withheld or cut off, is said and never stops the quest. Whether a check's `reference` names a source is left to the source checks, not to this review. In VS Code the node-model picker lists `oracle_review`. Under `rigor_profile: research` the review is a condition of `independently_validated` (`oracle_review.independence_gaps`, called from `Engine._independence_gaps` when the evidence is written): it counts only when it gave a usable answer (`.fi/oracle_review.json` has `verdicts` and no `error`), it judged every check of the frozen protocol as it stands (`oracle_review.fingerprint`: name, expected value, tolerance, case and measure, not the wording, kind or reference; the record keeps `read`, the checks as shown, and `after_look`, the checks as the same look left them; a check the reader proposed itself counts only with the numbers it proposed, and a check not among the ones shown never counts), and `.fi/model_calls.jsonl` names the model that answered its call (the record's `call_id`; a record without one is matched only to the one reading on record) and the model of every answered call of a step that writes the checks (`plan`, `plan_revise`, `design`, `design_self_critique`), and the reader's model is none of the writers' (`oracle_review.same_model`: lower case, no vendor or cloud prefix, no Ollama tag, no date / `-preview` / `-latest` / `-001` / `-v1` stamp, `.` and `_` read as `-`, so the same model under an alias or on another provider is the same). A call whose connection did not name the model (HTTP APIs, `claude_cli` and VS Code with a picked model do; a proxy provider, `oracle_review.PROXY_PROVIDERS`, is not taken at its word) cannot show two models, so it is a gap too. The gap is one sentence starting *the plan's checks were not reviewed by a second, different model*, with the reason and, when `provider.node_models.oracle_review` is unset, how to set it (and `--from plan --approve-as <you>` for a quest already past its plan). A failed review still never stops the quest; `run.log` and the plan section say it keeps the result below `independently_validated`. Outside research nothing changes.
- **A check at a setting the code never saw** — `core/hidden_check.py`, `rigor_profile: research` only (`Engine._hidden_check`, called in `execute` after a two-script run is accepted: not one the run-manifest check sends back or stops, nor a job still pending). For up to three checks whose expected value holds at every setting (`invariant`, `symmetry`, `second_implementation`: worst violation, expecting 0) with a case and a judgeable tolerance (`hidden_check.candidates`) for which a setting can be found, FI draws a case no prompt named (`hidden_check.derive`: a decimal step size of the case `dt`/`h`/`dx`/... divided by a factor drawn in [1.7, 3.3]; else a decimal grid setting moved to a value drawn between the grid's settings that is none of them; else a whole-number grid setting moved to another grid value, labelled as such; never another check's case), runs it with `trial_runner.measure_oracles` (FI calls `run_trial`/`run_cell` itself, the main run's environment), removes the harness's copy of the case (`.fi/trials/oracle.json`), and judges it with `oracle_check.judged` against the plan's own expected value and tolerance. `needs/HIDDEN_CHECK.json` keeps the cases, verdicts, what was not covered and why (`{name, why}`), `code_sha256` (every file in `code/` but `analysis.py`/`experiment.py`/`web_plots.py`, FI's `replot_figures.*`/`replot_layout.py`/`run.py`/`submit.py`/`fi_search.py`, `*.md`, `.git`, `__pycache__`) and `checks_key`; its own SHA-256 goes into the trace as a `hidden_check` event, and a record is reused only when it is the one the trace names, for the same code and checks. `hidden_check.evidence_gaps` (below `independently_validated`): a check that failed or could not run at the hidden setting, no record, a record the trace does not name (written by the simulation's code), a simulation FI cannot call on one case, code or checks changed after the record, a covered check missing from it, an earlier `hidden_check` event with status `failed` (a value outside the tolerance; a case that gave no value is `could_not_run` and runs again) on the same code and checks. Events are read only from a trace whose hash chain verifies (`Engine._hidden_check_written`), and a reused record adds none. Not covered, and not a gap: special or limiting cases, published values, convergence rates, a check with no case, a case with no step size and no grid setting, a search for the best design. It never stops the quest; `improve` and `--from` treat the record like `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json`.
- **A test run of the checks before the study** — `Engine._revise_after_dry_run`, from the oracle gate. The gate's first measurement of the checks, while the protocol is not frozen, is read by `oracle_forms.mismatch`: a violation check that expects a non-zero value (with a tolerance under a tenth of it) and measures about 0, one that expects 0 and measures 1 unless FI computed it with a formula wrapped in `abs(...)`, a value 100 times or more from a non-zero expected value, exactly 0 where a non-zero value is expected, the same size with the other sign, or a formula FI computed that gave no number on its case (text a script reports is never read). Any of these sends ONE request to the plan (`oracle_forms.dry_run_request`: look at each named check's definition, keep one that is right, never set an expected value to the measured number; each sentence says the cause must follow from the check's `reference`, not from the number), recorded in `.fi/oracle_dry_run.json` and on the attempt as `test_run_mismatches`; no repair of the script is spent on it, and only the checks can change (`_revise_checks_only`). When the plan changed or removed a check, the gate measures again, the change is written into `plan.md` (with a plain warning when the changed check now passes on the test run's own returned values, `oracle_forms.passes_on`) and recorded in `.fi/oracles_added.json` with its `reason` and any `removed` checks, so `pauses.plan: ask` (set by `rigor_profile: research`) stops once more before the freeze with a card that says FI changed the checks after a test run, and without the stop the freeze record says nobody approved them. A value simply outside its tolerance is not a mismatch and goes to the usual repair.
- **How we will judge whether the code got better** — for a quest that runs code and has a protocol, the plan's protocol also declares `criteria` (`core/criteria.py`): two to five checks of correctness that FI computes itself after every run, so that a later version of the code can be judged better or worse against a yardstick fixed before any change. Each takes its number from exactly one of: a check against a known answer the protocol declares (`oracle: <its name>`, with `use: error`, how far its measured value lands from the value it expects, the default, or `use: value`, the measured value itself; the number is the one the oracle gate recorded in `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json`, no second measurement; a run FI makes itself is an oracle with a `case`, so a criterion has no `case` of its own and one that names a `case` or `measure` is refused: the number it is judged against is then always the protocol's, never one the simulation computes about itself); or FI's own record of the trials (`trials: <a number every trial returns>`, whose value is how fast the standard error of its mean shrinks as trials are added: batch means in trial order, pooled over the settings, at least 8 batches per point, bias-corrected and weighted; 0.5 for independent trials, less when trials repeat or depend on each other; it needs 256 trials or more in settings of 32 or more, where 1 run in 250 of independent trials lands more than 0.15 from 0.5; a sweep of many small settings is named as not measured, with the counts). Each has a `direction` (`lower`, `higher`, or `target`), an optional `target` (the bar that counts as met; required for `target`) and its own `tolerance` (how close to the target counts as met, and the change a later version may show before it counts as worse; no global percentage). `core/criteria.py::normalize` refuses a criterion that takes its number from the script's results (`result`, `path`), names a check the protocol does not declare, or is about a headline number of the study (an id in `protocol.metrics` or the metric of `protocol.precision`, compared without case, spaces, dashes or underscores; a per-trial number an id is only built on, `final_size` under `mean_final_size`, is not refused, since a `trials` criterion measures how its standard error shrinks, not the finding): judging the code by its finding would reward bending it; a person's edit is refused with the reason, a draft keeps the usable criteria and the plan says which were left out. At most five; YAML's `1e-8` (read as text) is taken as the number it says. The criteria are part of the protocol, so they are frozen with it, covered by its hash, and changed only by an amendment (a redesign's amendment request that carries a criterion FI cannot use has it left out, and its reason says so; one whose only change was such a criterion changes nothing and does not stop the quest). The methodology audit's receipt covers the design without its criteria (`core/receipts.py::design_core`): the audit judges the study, the criteria are checked by `core/criteria.py`. `plan.md` shows them in plain words as *How we will judge whether the code got better*, after *The model behind the numbers* (not read back; edit the block). FI runs the checks itself only when the quest keeps two scripts (the trial contract): a one-script quest's plan says so and `run.log` warns, and nothing more is asked. When the model's draft names none that FI can measure itself (none, or only ones resting on a check the script's own `oracle()` answers), the plan step searches the literature once (`Knowledge.asearch`, a few results, no routing call) for how such studies are checked and asks the model again (`agents/plan_criteria.md`, node `plan_criteria`) with the quest's own sources and what the search found; if there is still none, `pauses.plan: ask` (and so `rigor_profile: research`) stops at the plan asking the person to write one (a resume without one goes on), and otherwise `run.log` and the console say so and the quest goes on with none (never a made-up one). After every `execute` the engine computes each criterion (`Engine._record_criteria`) and appends a row to `.fi/criteria_history.jsonl`: `n` (one per run of the code), the frozen protocol's run (`run_1` until an amendment), version and SHA-256 (rows are compared only within one protocol), the id of the run's record in `.fi/attempts.jsonl`, the commit of `code/`'s own git history that ran (the code is recorded first when a repair changed it since), and per criterion its value, whether it was met, and whether it counts; a number the script's own code measured (an oracle with no case) is shown and never counted, and a criterion with nothing measured says why. `run.log` gets one line (`[criteria] run N: …`). The improve loop (next entry) decides from the rows. What it does not do: a one-script quest's checks are measured by the script, so there its criteria are shown but none counts; the node `plan_criteria` has no entry in the VS Code interview's per-node model picker (set it in `provider.node_models`); the rows of `.fi/criteria_history.jsonl` are not shown on the web page or in VS Code (open the file, or read the `[criteria]` lines of `run.log`).
- **Improving the code against its checks (the improve loop and the ratchet)** — `core/improve.py`, graph node `improve` between `execute_reflect` and `analyze`. After a run with a result, when a criterion FI counts is not met, FI changes the simulation one step at a time, at most `engine.improve_rounds` times in the whole quest (default 3; `0` turns it off). Each round: the model (`agents/improve.md`, node `improve`, one call a round) is shown the criteria in plain words with the known-answer check each rests on (its case, expected value and reference), where they stand for the version kept so far, the changes already tried and what came of them, and the simulation's code; never the study's own results (`RESULT_JSON`, the analysis, `protocol.metrics`, contrasts or the precision target). It answers with ONE edit: a piece of text found exactly once in `simulate.py` or a helper module beside it, and what replaces it (`{"file", "find", "replace", "why"}`). The edit is refused before anything runs (`improve.check_edit`) when it names another file (the analysis script `experiment.py`, FI's `run.py`, anything outside `code/`), is not found exactly once, no longer parses, changes nothing the simulation computes (the syntax trees are compared with comments, layout, docstrings and `print` / logging / `sys.stdout.write` statements taken out, so changing only a printed number is not a change), gives back a version already tried, writes a number a check expects, or the target of a criterion aimed at a value, into the code (small round numbers such as 0, 1 or 0.5 are not counted as such), changes how a number a check reads is worked out where the simulation returns it (the expression beside that key in the returned dict, an `out["error"] = ...` line or a `dict(error=...)` argument, compared as syntax: what a check measures is fixed with the protocol, so the edit must fix what feeds it), or adds a comparison that reads a setting a check runs on (`cell["dt"]`, `cell.get("dt")` or a variable named `dt`) against that check's value of it (`if dt == 0.1:`: a check must be computed, not recognised). A refused edit is a spent round with nothing run; it is still an entry in `code/CHANGELOG.md` (and a commit) saying why. An accepted one is written and FI measures every criterion itself (`Engine._improve_measure`): the known-answer cases the criteria name, run by the engine through the trial harness (`trial_runner.measure_oracles`; `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json` is not touched), and, when a criterion is about the trials, every trial of the frozen protocol (`trial_runner.run_trials`; FI's record of the first run's trials, `raw/ledger.jsonl`, `raw/trials.json` and `.fi/trials/run.json`, is put back byte for byte afterwards). There is no smaller grid for a round: the protocol has no such setting, so a round runs only what the criteria need. Before and after the round's run FI hashes the files it judges by (`needs/FROZEN_PROTOCOL.json`, `plan.md` (the protocol before the freeze), `.fi/criteria_history.jsonl`, `needs/ORACLE_CHECK.json`, and its own `core/criteria.py`, `oracle_check.py`, `trial_runner.py`, `improve.py`), every file in `code/` (its `__pycache__` and git's objects aside, git's settings and hooks included) and the loop's own saved copies in `.fi/improve/`; a round whose run changed any of them is aborted: `code/` is put back as the round started it (every file and git's settings and hooks; a link or junction the run made is removed, never what it points at; FI's own commits never run a git hook, never read a system git config or (with git 2.32 or later) a personal one, and never look above `code/` for a repository; when the run replaced, linked or deleted `code/.git` itself FI does not rebuild it and makes no commit for that round), the change the round ran is committed as the record of what was tried, the quest's records and the loop's saved copies are written back from memory, the version kept so far is restored, nothing it measured is recorded, and no further round runs. FI's own files cannot be put back by FI: the log says they were changed and that FI should be reinstalled. The ratchet (`improve.compare`, `improve.verdict`) compares each criterion FI counts with the version kept so far, on its own and by its own `tolerance` (lower, higher, or distance from the target; never a total): a round is kept as the best only when at least one criterion got better by more than its tolerance and none got worse by more than its tolerance. A criterion measured before and not now counts as worse; when every one is lost the change is taken as having broken the run, not kept, and not a regression. One measured now and not before counts as better only when it meets its bar. A round that made a criterion worse is flagged in `run.log` (`[improve] round N made … worse by more than the tolerance`), in the audit trace (`improve_regression`) and in `code/CHANGELOG.md`; under `rigor_profile: research` the quest then stops (pause kind `improve`, `NEXT_STEP.md`), with the version kept so far already back in `code/`, and a resume goes on with the version in `code/` (run once more in full if it differs from the first) and makes no more changes in that pass; otherwise it is a warning and the loop goes on from the version kept. The loop stops when every criterion FI counts is met (one with no bar set is never met), when a round makes no criterion better by more than its own tolerance, when the rounds are used up, or when a round was aborted, and `run.log` says which in one line (`[improve] stopped: …. Kept …`), with the rounds spent and the model calls made. FI has no spending limit for a quest, so the rounds are not counted against one: the budget is `engine.improve_rounds`, shared by every pass of the quest (a redesign gets what is left; a rerun from the run or an earlier step with `--from` goes back to before the rounds were spent, so it starts the budget again, and the loop's old record is moved aside with the run's outputs), and each call is recorded in `.fi/model_calls.jsonl` under the step `improve`.
