Each round that ran is a commit in `code/`'s own history, its `CHANGELOG.md` entry naming the round, the reason the model gave and every criterion's value with better / worse / same; a round not kept is followed by a second commit that puts the kept version back. Each round is also a row in `.fi/criteria_history.jsonl` with an `improve` block (`round`, `kept`, `compared_with_round`, `better`, `worse`, `broken`). The loop's own record is `.fi/improve.json` (rounds, outcome and reason of each, the version kept, why it stopped, the row of the full run it started from, and the loop's earlier passes in the quest), with the first and the best versions' files (byte for byte, whatever their encoding) and a copy of the first run's trial record in `.fi/improve/`. A loop cut short (the process stopped mid-round) is undone when the step runs again: the version the last full run used and its trial record go back, a commit says so, and its rounds stay spent; a stop or a loop cut short over code that has run again since (a rerun from an earlier step) is set aside instead, and a rerun `--from` a step first puts back the version the last full run used when a loop was cut short (its `.py` files; another file a mid-round run wrote into `code/` stays). The paper is told about the loop when the results it is written from are that loop's run; after a rerun `--from` the run or an earlier step the loop's record is moved aside with the run's outputs, so the paper does not mention it even when `code/` still holds a version the loop kept (after a research stop, say), and `code/CHANGELOG.md` is then the only account of the rounds. When a changed version was kept, the quest goes back to `execute` once: that version runs the ordinary way (every check, the full set of trials run again rather than taken from the earlier run, replicates, the repair budget of a new script), its row is the next one in the history, and its run is the one analysed and written up; if it produces no result, the first version is put back and run once more (and the paper's note says so), and if that fails too the quest goes on without a third run. Whether the study's results changed after that run is written in `code/CHANGELOG.md` by the names of the results that changed only, and it never chose the version. The writer is told what the loop did (`improve.write_note`, added to the evidence note) so the paper's methods say it. The plan's *How we will judge whether the code got better* section says the loop exists. The progress line (console, web live log, VS Code chat) says when a round runs and why it stopped. Nothing runs when the quest has no criterion FI counts, every one is already met, the run produced no result, the simulation is not on the trial contract (one script, a background job), the quest runs no simulation, the study is a search for the best design (FI's search makes its runs), or in the confirm run of `engine.phased`. What it does not do: it does not check a change against a hidden variant of each check (another step size or seed the model never sees), so a change fitted to the visible checks alone in a way none of the refusals above names is not caught: writing the known answer into a variable the checked number is computed from (`y = math.exp(-1)` just before the return), recognising a check's setting without a comparison (a lookup table keyed by it), or tuning a constant until the check's one setting comes out right; the rounds do not run a smaller grid; and a round run in a fresh process after a restart does not have the library skills' paths the quest's own run gave the simulation.
- **Evidence status** — `core/evidence.py::assess` reads a quest's records and names the highest of six ordered levels it reaches: `executed`, `internally_reconciled` (the paper-vs-results audits agree), `protocol_runtime_matched` (frozen protocol intact, the script held to it, the run manifest matches, no numeric warning accepted), `independently_validated` (the engine ran the simulation on each oracle's case and judged what it measured, and every declared oracle passed; a value the script reported itself never counts, on one script or two; under `rigor_profile: research` also a usable review of the checks by a second, different model and the checks held at a setting the code never saw, see the two entries above), `statistically_adequate` (metric specs cover the headline metrics, precision targets reached, failed trials have a `failure_policy`), `publication_ready` (review accepted, no post-hoc amendment, the evidence gate / design methodology audit / claim check each left a receipt in `needs/receipts/` (`core/receipts.py`: status `pass` | `fail` | `unknown` | `not_applicable`, start and end times, the step that wrote it, SHA-256 of what it judged and of its verdict) that says it passed, the claim check's for the final draft; a receipt that is missing, unreadable, malformed or for an earlier draft is a gap, and so is an evidence-gate receipt made for other inputs than the ones there are now (each input it names but the sources, which the writing step trims to the ones cited, and always the analysis and the cross-check, are hashed again and compared: `receipts.stale_inputs`), as is a check turned off, and the evidence gate did not judge the evidence `insufficient` or `broaden` before the paper was written. When the statistics coverage or the whole assessment crashes, that is a gap too, and `needs/EVIDENCE.json` is replaced by a record that claims no level). A search for the best design (`study_type: find_best_design`) reaches the top level only when FI's check at finer numerical settings passed (`core/optimum_check.py::evidence_gaps`): FI's record of the search missing, changed after the check, holding more evaluations than the budget or a design outside the ranges is a gap below `protocol_runtime_matched`; the check missing, not finished, changed after FI wrote it, with no numerical setting to refine, a design that failed at the finer settings, or a limit the best design breaks there, below `independently_validated`; an improvement not shown (it disappears at finer settings, it is within the numerical error of the two designs, under the plan's threshold or, with randomness, not more than chance), one that could not be tested (no estimate of the numerical error, fewer than two fresh runs), or one above the plan's threshold but within the numerical error, below `statistically_adequate`; a better design nearby, a design at the edge of its range, a variable that changes nothing, starting points that disagree or a single one, or a search that ran out of budget or time before converging, below `publication_ready`. Each gap is the check's own plain sentence, and the ladder adds the blind spot that the improvement is one within the plan's model. It returns `ladder` (per level: `assurance_claim`, `known_blind_spots`, `evidence_artifacts`, `gaps`) and `rigor_profile`, and `gaps` for the next level; a check that was turned off is a gap, and so is one that ran but could not be evaluated — that distinction (`status: "ok"` vs `"unknown"`/`"unreviewed"`, and a review panel's per-persona receipts) is what keeps a failed call from reading as a pass. A review the model could not be asked for at all (a usage limit, a provider that is down) is not recorded as anything: the quest stops (`review_unavailable`) with the paper written, and the resumed run asks the reviewer again. It used to be recorded as `accept, score 3`, which the stop showed, an automatic accept took, and a write-back to Axon trusted. It is written to `needs/EVIDENCE.json` when the quest ends, needs no model, and never touches the quest; the run summary (`evidence`), `/api/quests/<id>` (through `evidence.upgrade`, which reads a record written under the older names `internally_consistent` / `validated_against_oracle` as `internally_reconciled` with a `legacy` note), the quest page banner (hover a level for its guarantee and blind spots) and the `[FI] evidence:` line in VSCode show it.
- **What the result is for** — `result_use` (top level: `explore`, `research` or `decision`; the interview writes it). A config that says neither it nor `rigor_profile: research` is an exploration (`Config.effective_result_use`): it runs, its paper opens with a note that the result is preliminary (`PRELIMINARY_NOTE`, taken out again if the quest later becomes a study), and its evidence level never reaches `publication_ready`.
- **Rigor profile** — `rigor_profile: research` (top level of the config, default `default`) fills in what the config leaves out and refuses what contradicts it: `pauses.plan: ask`, `execution.split_analysis: true` and `split_failure: block`, `execution.shared_interpreter: false` and `system_site_packages: false` (a fresh per-quest environment, not FI's own shared interpreter — outside `research` the shared default is only recorded as a gap in `needs/ENVIRONMENT.json`, not forced), `engine.protocol_check` / `oracle_check` / `numeric_warnings` / `run_manifest_check: block` (they cannot be turned off), `engine.cross_check_verify: true`, `engine.audit_trace: true` (the decision trace; missing or no longer checking out, it is a gap toward `publication_ready`) and a review panel (`methodologist`, `statistician`, `reproducibility`, `devil_advocate`; a panel of your own is kept) with the reviewers not all on one model (`provider.node_models["review_panel.<role>"]`): a panel all on one model is one model's view in several roles, so the quest stops before anything runs and says how to give one reviewer another model, which you choose. Each reviewer's result records the model it was asked on (`requested_model`) and the provider and model that answered as the provider reported it (`actual_provider`, `actual_model`, `actual_model_reported`, read per call from `core/provider.py::LAST_CALL`, so reviewers asked at the same time do not overwrite each other's record); reviewers set up on different models but reported as answered by one stop the quest (`review_models_collapsed`) until the other model can be reached. A reviewer whose connection does not say which model answered (`actual_model_reported: false`, as from an older VS Code extension or a CLI that does not say) does not stop the quest, but whether the reviewers really were different models is then unproven. The VS Code extension names the chat model it selected and sent each call to (its `vscode.lm` id, with the vendor beside it: as VS Code reports it, not an independent proof). An extension that does not send the model's name, or a chat picker set to *Auto* (which names no model), is read as unreported: the evidence level lists *the review's independence is unverified* and the result is not `publication_ready`. `claude_cli` usually says which model answered (its own `modelUsage` record, read per call); a call where it does not is unverified like any other. Now that it reports, reviewers set up on two names that reach one model stop the quest as described above. With only one model, `engine.one_model_review: true` runs the panel on it: no stop, and a gap toward `publication_ready`. The interview asks for that model up front when the result is for research or a decision (below), so a quest it writes does not stop for it; its answer *I only have one model* writes `engine.one_model_review: true`. A panel is a way of finding problems, not an independent replication. It also makes a replicate-seed repair failure (below) pause the quest rather than only warn. "Kept" means checked for the three roles that carry the profile's must-flag enforcement (`config.REQUIRED_REVIEW_ROLES` = `methodologist`, `statistician`, `reproducibility`; `devil_advocate` is not required) — a panel that is present but missing one of those is refused by name, the same as an empty one, since a non-empty list does not by itself mean the roles that matter are on it. The `--new` / `/interview` / `@fi /new` interview sets it from *What is the result for?* (research or a decision turns it on); when it is `research`, an empty `review_panel: []` (which would contradict the profile) is left out entirely, and the one specific incomplete panel the interview itself could otherwise write — its own three-persona smart default, which predates this requirement — has the missing role added rather than being written as a config the profile would refuse. That completion is scoped to exactly that preset (`core/interview.py:REVIEW_PANELS[0].value`, mirrored in the VS Code extension as `SMART_DEFAULT_REVIEW_PANEL`): a panel built any other way (round-tripped through `--update` on an existing quest, say) is written as given and, if still missing a role, refused the same as any hand-edited config — completing a choice someone actually made, rather than one the smart default made for them, would take it away without saying so; the initial `research` choice itself is made when the quest is created and is not editable by `--update`. A config that sets one of these to the opposite fails to load with the key named (`Config._apply_rigor_profile`, `_RESEARCH_PROFILE`).
- **An audit trace** — `core/audit_log.py`. Every quest writes `.fi/audit.jsonl`: each step (started, done, failed, stopped for a person), who accepted the result (`result_accepted`: a person's receipt — who, when, the interface, their answer and note, the paper and evidence record hashes and the limits listed — or automatically), every check's verdict with the record's sha256, each route with the facts it read, the files written with their hash, and the model's own stated reasons (the idea it chose, with the pairwise comparison's and the self-critique's reasons when they ran, the skills it chose or declined — never a skill the config requires, which is the engine's decision —, the design's `rationale` and alternatives it weighed, the methodology audit's objections, how each finding sits against the literature, a script repair's `patch_summary` or its reason for giving up, the reasons of the setup answers it chose, the review's verdict with a one-line `why` it is asked for, its weaknesses, a review panel moderator's rationale, the writer's one-line `REFINE_WHY` for how it answers a person's refine notes, the evidence gate's verdict and gaps — only when a model actually decided the gate, never for a rule-decided or unevaluatable one — and analyze's stated reason for its `next_step`) as `model_claim` events kept apart from the checks (`Engine._audit_claims` / `_audit_stated_reasons`; a reason is recorded only under a call made during that run of the step, never one carried over in the state). `model_changed` records a change of the quest's model: the VS Code chat panel's model replacing the config's, or a run whose calls were answered by another model than the earlier runs'. Each of these also carries `provider` / `model` (whichever actually answered, even after a fallback took over — `core/provider.py::LAST_CALL`, the task's own record of the call, with the client's shared `last_provider`/`last_model` only as a fallback) and `prompt_hash` / `response_hash` of the exact call it came from, so a claim can be checked against the real call rather than taken on faith; `describe()`'s one-line text shows the claim and, when present, what it decided and why, not this provenance (open the raw event for that). Events are numbered and hash-chained; values that look like credentials and the home folder are removed. `python launch.py --trace <quest>` prints it and verifies the chain (`--trace-node`, `--trace-detail summary|checks|debug`); the web quest page has a **Trace** panel and `GET /api/quests/{id}/trace`; in VSCode, `@fi /trace <quest_id> [--node <name>] [--detail summary|checks|debug]` prints the same lines in chat — all three read the one `core/audit_log.py::describe()` decides a line says, so they cannot drift from each other. `engine.audit_trace: false` turns it off (not under `rigor_profile: research`). A finished quest's last event is a seal (`quest_finalized`), written after everything else the quest keeps: how many events came before it, how many could not be written over every run of the quest (kept beside the trace in `audit.jsonl.lost`, so a pause does not reset it), how many attempt records and model call records could not be written, which steps completed, and the hashes of the paper, `needs/EVIDENCE.json`, `.fi/attempts.jsonl`, `.fi/branch_ledger.jsonl`, `.fi/model_calls.jsonl`, `.fi/shadow_recommendations.jsonl` and `.fi/literature_queries.json` (every one required: a record file the quest never wrote to is an empty file with a hash — but not the record of model calls of a quest that made calls, whose absence is itself the gap — and a seal that leaves one out, or names it without a hash, is a gap). Research quests sealed before the seal named the record of model calls, or the record of the search queries, read one level down. The evidence record is written just before the seal that names it, so it says `trace_seal: pending`, and whatever shows it (the CLI summary, the web quest page, `--why`, the to-do card, the knowledge-base write-back) checks the seal as it reads it (`core/evidence.py::read`, which never writes): under `rigor_profile: research` a trace whose last event is not the seal, whose seal lost events or records, does not count them right or lacks the write or review step, or a file the seal names that changed since, takes `publication_ready` away (the result is read one level down, with the reasons, and `trace_seal` says `not_verified`). What the record says about its own seal is not believed, since it is in a file the seal protects; a research record from before the seal named these files is read one level down too. An attempt record that could not be written in any run of the quest (`records_not_written`) also keeps it from `publication_ready`: run the quest again from a clean start to get a complete record. Recording approved settings after the quest finished (`--update`) adds an event after the seal, so the quest is sealed again when it is resumed. The seal is needed because a hash chain that checks out shows only that what is there was not changed. Reasoning a connection hands back (Kimi/DeepSeek `reasoning_content`, Claude CLI thinking, Codex's `reasoning` items, the VS Code chat model's thinking parts, whose value may be a list of strings) is kept apart in `.fi/thinking.jsonl` (`core/thinking_capture.py`; `output.save_thinking`, on by default): one line per model call with its `call_id`, redacted, labelled as the model's own account and not evidence, outside the seal. What it holds is the reasoning summary the provider returned, not the model's hidden or complete chain of thought, and it is never evidence for the result: no check reads it. It can quote your data and the paper's text, so for a quest that handles sensitive data set `output.save_thinking: false` (nothing is kept, and the VS Code connection stops asking for it); a step whose connection returned none is said once in `run.log`. On the VS Code connection every step's call asks Copilot for the model's thinking with Copilot's undocumented internal model option `_enableThinking` (Opus then returns a summary, not its full thinking; a Copilot release may drop the option), plus `includeEncryptedThinking: true` so a GPT model's reasoning summary, which Copilot otherwise keeps from other extensions, reaches FI (only the readable summary is kept); a model that refuses these options is asked again once without them and `run.log` says so once; `output.save_thinking: false` stops asking. `--why <quest> <step>` (web **Why?**, `@fi /why`) says whether the file holds that step's reasoning and how many characters, and `--why <quest> reasons` (web **Model's reasons**) lists every step's stated reasons; each says that a reason or a reasoning summary is the model's own account, not its hidden reasoning. Every `model_claim` also names the `call_id` of the call it came from (when the engine recorded that call), the `requested_model` when one is set, and the `finish_reason` when the connection reported one (HTTP providers do; the Claude CLI and the VS Code bridge do not). See docs/trace.md.
- **Lines another program added to the quest's records** — `core/record_anchor.py`. What you see: when FI finds a line it did not write in the trace or a record file, `run.log` and the console say so, the line goes to `.fi/<name>.outside_fi.jsonl` (never used), and the result is not `publication_ready` in any profile. How: the simulation runs as the same user as FI, so it could append a line chained onto the trace's last one (the chain checks out) or a line to a record file and stop FI. For `.fi/audit.jsonl`, `attempts.jsonl`, `branch_ledger.jsonl`, `model_calls.jsonl`, `model_calls.after_seal.jsonl` and `shadow_recommendations.jsonl` (`AuditLog._write` and `attempt_records.append`, through `record_anchor.appending`), FI notes in `.fi/record_heads.sqlite` each file's size, line count and a running digest of its lines: the note moves on and says the line is on its way before the write, and says it landed after (a failed write is cut back and the note put back; a note that cannot be written is counted in `.fi/record_heads.lost`, itself a gap). At the end of every step the engine copies the note into its checkpoint (`QuestState.record_anchor`). Before each line it appends, and when a process first opens the trace (`record_anchor.before_reading`, so `--update` or a title change never chain onto an added line), FI compares the file's size with the note: bytes past it are moved aside, a line of FI's that is gone is recorded. When a quest starts again (`Engine._check_records_changed_outside` → `record_anchor.check_on_start`, before anything is written), every file is compared line by line with the note and with the checkpoint's copy: what FI did not write is moved aside (a whole file FI never wrote, too), changed or removed lines of FI's and a note that disagrees with the copy are reported, a removed note is rebuilt from the copy and recorded; each is said in `run.log` and recorded in the trace (`record_changed_outside_fi`). A line FI noted but never wrote is no alarm; a half-written last line is left for the trace's own repair (`audit_repair`) and cut off a record file. `record_anchor.evidence_gaps` (in `core/evidence.py`, any profile): a recorded finding, a trace event, a moved-aside file, a file that does not match the note now, or a line FI could not note. A quest from before the note is noted as it is. Not covered: a same-user program that rewrites the note and the checkpoint too (the simulation reaches `.fi/` under every sandbox today, Docker included); a line added while FI runs can be read by a step before FI next writes that file; the `needs/` check records rewritten as a whole (named by hash in the trace at every step's end; a stopped step runs again from its start). About 5 ms per line written on Windows.
- **What a quest tried, and under which conditions** — `core/attempt_records.py`. Two more files in `.fi/`, written as the quest runs; every line carries `schema` (now 5), its own `record_id` and the `quest_id`. `attempts.jsonl` has one line per run of the experiment (an outcome — `process_error`, `protocol_mismatch`, `oracle_failure` (a known-answer check failed, including one that only warned) or `inconclusive` — the raw check statuses, the hash of every file in `code/`, subfolders included, each read whole, when the run's result was collected (a cluster job's code is also hashed when it is submitted and compared when its results are collected; a file other than the analysis and submit scripts that changed in between is named in `.fi/trials/cluster.json` and leaves the context incomplete), the frozen protocol's `run_id`, and `parent_id`, the design revision or repair recorded last, also across a pause), one when the quest stops for a check it failed (`execution_status: stopped`, the run it follows; the known-answer checks, the protocol or run-record checks, the run's numeric warnings, scripts that break the two-script layout, a seed that could not be repaired; not a stop that waits for a decision, nor a check that could not be asked), and one when the quest ends or fails, with four separate fields instead of one label: `execution_status` (`completed`, `no_result`, `data_analysis` for a quest that analyses given data, `no_experiment_by_design` for a literature survey, `crashed` only for a quest that failed), `review_status` (`accepted` only for an accept a reviewer really gave, by the same test the automatic accept and the write-back use; `revise`, `unavailable`, `none`; a person's reject is in `person`, which is empty for an automatic accept; `accepted_by` says `person` or `automatic` as the evidence record does), `evidence_status` (the evidence level) and `claim_outcome` (always empty for now: the analysis does not state whether the result supports the hypothesis, and guessing from its prose would mislabel results), plus the run lines of this run of the quest and how many records of the quest could not be built or written over all its runs (`records_not_written`, counted in `.fi/attempts.lost`). A failed quest's line carries the conditions last worked out while it ran. Each line carries the conditions it ran under: provider, model and the settings that change its answers; for each step asked through the engine's one-call path, who answered its last call (`models_used`: provider, model, the prompt's and reply's hashes); a hash of the prompts; FI's identity (a hash of its loaded source, plus the git commit and whether the checkout had changes when FI runs from its own checkout); the question (topic, hypothesis and design hashes); the policy (`result_use`, rigor profile, review panel, the approved-settings record's hash, a hash of the whole configuration); the selected skills by name; a hash of the environment's Python, platform, isolation and packages (not its folder); the dependency lock; the inputs and the data folder (each one hash over every file, with the file count, total bytes and `complete`: false above 2000 files, for a file over 64 MiB — hashed by its size and first and last MiB — or an unreadable file); the protocol and its metric definitions (the same hash before and after it is frozen); the budget; and the iteration / design revision / repair it belongs to. Each context names when it was worked out (`context_kind`: `in_progress`, `after_run` or `quest_end`). `missing` lists what keeps a context from being complete (inputs or data not all hashed, no model call yet, FI's source unreadable, after a run or at the end of a quest meant to run an experiment no script, environment or protocol (both read again, whole: a file cache keyed by size and modification time is not trusted there), code that changed while a cluster job was queued, a failure before any state), and `complete` is true only when that list is empty: only a complete context of the current schema may ever count as the same conditions as another; any other is information only. A cluster job still running is recorded when it finishes. `branch_ledger.jsonl` has the candidate ideas and how one was chosen (`ensemble`, `tournament`, `reflection` or `model`), each design revision numbered as in `DESIGN_HISTORY.json` with its reason, and each script repair with the hashes before and after. Both files' hashes are logged in the audit trace after each step and named by the quest's final seal (a quest that fails logs them with their line counts as `attempts_sealed`); a file changed after the seal is a gap when the evidence is read. Every model call a quest makes is also one line of `.fi/model_calls.jsonl` per attempt: the step, which call of that step, the model asked for and the one that answered (named by the connection, or not), whether a fallback took it, the hashes of the prompt and the answer (never their text; the prompt hash is the same one the trace and `models_used` carry, so they can be joined), the outcome (each attempt that failed before a retry or before the fallback chain moved on is its own line, as is a final failure) and this call's own token counts. It covers the engine's steps (the source router and ensembles included). The engine also keeps its own count of the calls it made per step, in the quest's state (so a resumed quest keeps it). At the quest's end the context and the seal name this record's hash, line count and those counts, and list as missing: lines that could not be written, a step with fewer lines than calls, a step's last call that is not among the lines, and answered calls on a connection that names the answering model (an HTTP API, the claude CLI) whose line names none; under `rigor_profile: research` any of these keeps the result from `publication_ready`. Outputs made after the quest sealed its record (slides, poster, talk, the visual check, a later `--emit`) have their calls in `.fi/model_calls.after_seal.jsonl`, which the seal does not name: it covers what was made from the closed research record, not the research. Calls of those generators that fail, and tools with no quest (digest, portfolio, proposal), are not recorded; a plan revised (`--revise-plan`) on a quest already sealed has its calls in the after-seal record too, until the quest is run again and sealed again. A streamed call (Kimi) counts as naming its model only when the stream said which model answered. The knowledge-base summary a quest writes back (only when accepted, unless `knowledge.write_back_only_on_accept` is off) also carries the chosen idea, the alternatives and how the choice was made. These are records only: nothing that decides a route reads them yet, nothing in them supports a claim, and a record that cannot be built or written never touches the quest. A failure is recorded as a fact about one attempt under one set of conditions, not about a method in general.
- **What past attempts would recommend (recorded only)** — `core/attempt_memory.py`, `engine.attempt_memory: shadow` (the default; `off` reads and writes nothing). At four decisions — a plan put forward, a design about to be implemented, an experiment about to run, a script about to be repaired — FI compares what that decision depends on with the failed attempts of the quests under the same output folder (this quest's earlier ones included) and writes what it *would* recommend to `.fi/shadow_recommendations.jsonl`. Each decision is compared only on its own conditions: a plan on the topic, the inputs and data, the skills, the policy (rigor profile, review panel, what the result is for), the design prompt and the model that answered the plan; an implement on the design, the protocol and its metrics, the packages asked for, the implement prompts and the model it is asked on; an experiment on the code, the packages, the inputs and data, the protocol, runs per setting, the time limit, the replicate seeds and FI's trial harness (not the model or the prompt: the same code is the same test whoever wrote it); a repair on the code and how the run failed. The installed environment is not compared (a decision is made before the packages are installed and a past record was written after; the packages asked for stand for it). How an attempt failed is its signature: the return code, the exception type its traceback names, and the check that stopped it; a time limit, running out of memory, a killed process or a provider or connection error is transient. `BLOCK` only before an experiment runs, when the same conditions failed with the same non-transient signature at least twice; `VERIFY` when they failed once, under another model, or at a plan, implement or repair; `INFO` for a similar failure (the same design, protocol or code under other conditions) or one that rests on a condition not known on one side or an older record; `IGNORE` when nothing comparable failed. A repair is never compared with the run that triggered it or with the repairs of the same design. Each line names the decision, what FI actually did, the value of every condition compared, what the quest's conditions could not work out, the matched attempts, which conditions matched, differed or were not known, and the failure signature, so the rules can be scored again later without running anything; the run, stop or end of quest a decision led to names that line (`parent_shadow_ids`). **Nothing reads these to decide anything**: no route, state, prompt or trace changes, no model is called, and the recommendation is worked out on the quest's own background thread within 5 seconds — later, it is dropped, never written, and counted (`.fi/shadow_recommendations.lost`), as is any failure. To work it out FI reads the failure records of the other quests under the same output folder; only their record ids, hashes and exception type names are copied into this quest's file. The other quests' failure records are read through one read-only cache per output folder, kept in the process (a fleet reads each file once); it holds only failure summaries of the newest 500 quests, is locked, and can be rebuilt from the records at any time. The file is one of the records the final seal names (a seal made before this record existed is checked against the files it did name). `fi tools shadow-report` counts, by decision and action, how often what followed a decision failed (a plan fails when the quest stops at a check or ends without an accepted, finished result; the other decisions when the run they led to failed), with the failure rate after an `IGNORE` as the base rate, and by model. Whether acting on the recommendations would raise the share of usable results or lower cost, without more false accepts and without suppressing exploration that would have worked, has not been shown; that takes a planned comparison (the recommendations on and off, exploration constrained and free) before any of them may change what a quest does.
- **Explore, then confirm** — `core/phased.py`, `engine.phased: true` (on by default under `rigor_profile: research`, where a config may still say `phased: false` and keeps it; off under the default profile, where off changes nothing). The model may try designs and look at results in an exploration stage (the protocol is not frozen yet); when the quest is about to write on an accepted result, the protocol is frozen and the frozen design runs once more in a confirm stage (evidence gate → `execute` → … → evidence gate → write). The confirm run gets a part of your data held back before exploration first read it, when the quest has one CSV/TSV table you supplied with at least 40 rows (`inputs/data/`; for a quest that analyses data, `data/` only, which is what its data-reading step reads), and always a seed base exploration never used. Rows are set aside at the start; which rows are held back is decided right before the data is first read (`phased.decide_split`, `core/phased_data.py`), by which rows belong together: the plan's `protocol.split` (`strategy: row | group | time | spatial`, `unit`, `time_column`, `embargo`, `stratify_by`, `min_groups`, `seed`), else plan.md's line *Rows that belong together:* (a column, or `independent`), else the table's column names when they leave one reading (one time column: the latest period, never rows at random; one unit column such as `subject_id` or `site` whose values repeat (a column that numbers the rows is not a unit): about 30% of the units, all of a unit's rows on one side, at least 10 units and 3 on each side); otherwise a research quest stops once to ask (pause `data_split`, answered in plan.md) and any other quest holds nothing back and says why. Rows are split one by one only when said to be independent; spatial blocks are never guessed from coordinates. The rule, its source and a manifest with a zero-overlap check (no unit in both parts; any overlap leaves nothing confirmed) are kept in `.fi/phased.json`. The whole files and the held-back part are kept outside the quest folder, in `<output_dir>/_held_back/<quest_id>/`; whether they were out of the code's reach is recorded as `isolation` (`core/phased_isolation.py`, also in `needs/EVIDENCE.json` `phased.isolation`): `docker` (every run in a container that mounts the quest folder and nothing holding the kept files), `encrypted+scanned` (without a container: the kept files encrypted with Fernet under a key made for each run of FI and kept only in its memory, and, before the confirm run, no path out of the quest folder found in `code/` by a static read of its scripts: an absolute path, `..`, a parent of the working folder or of the script past the quest, the home folder, the name `_held_back`), or `isolation_unverified`, a `publication_ready` gap; a new run of FI rewrites the kept files with its key from the whole file in place (checked against the recorded fingerprints), a killed run's encrypted rows cannot be read back (the next start says so and nothing is confirmed), and a background job without a container holds nothing back. The whole files are in place again whenever the quest is not running; once exploration has run only rows held back before can be held back again; without such data it runs on new random seeds and the paper says so. A background job's confirm run submits its own job with the new seeds (exploration's is set aside as `job/state.explore.json`; FI's job array plans new tasks), and checking on it after a resume is not a second run. In the confirm stage a redesign or a new literature pass is not followed; a protocol change is an amendment; any second run on the confirm data or seeds (a repair, a later re-run) makes the result preliminary; a held-back part that could not be put in place, a result that is not the one the confirm run produced, turning the setting off during the confirm stage, leaves nothing confirmed (`compromised`); with no data held back, a study whose result new seeds cannot change (no source of random numbers in its code, runs that were one run repeated because the seed reaches none of its random numbers, `run_cell`, or an experiment whose generators never take `FI_REPLICATE_SEED` and draw only from fixed seeds; seeds that merely gave the same numbers do not count) is `not_confirmable` and is written up with its protocol frozen, as is a new-seeds confirm run that gives exactly exploration's numbers (a run that ignores its seed would give the same). A background job is told to pass `FI_REPLICATE_SEED` on to its job only when the setting is on. Turned on after the experiment ran (even a paused run), or off and on again while exploring: nothing is held back. `plan.md` has a section *How the result will be confirmed*: with it on, what the confirm run is given and what it costs (one more full run of the frozen design, every setting with as many runs as an exploration run), or, when it can no longer be confirmed, why; under research with it off, a plain sentence that the result is not confirmed on unseen data (`run.log` says it too). A quest that analyses data instead of simulating (`data_quest` in the record) is confirmed on held-back rows of its table: the evidence gate's `confirm_data` edge runs `data_load` once more, by the engine, with the frozen protocol and design (a different design confirms nothing), on the held-back rows only; the reading is counted like a confirm run, and its numbers are set beside exploration's (`phased_data.compare`: a number that changes sign or by more than a quarter; counts of rows are left out), said in run.log, the paper's note, the writer's note (names only) and `needs/EVIDENCE.json` `phased.confirm_differs`, never a gap. A data quest whose data cannot be split (too few rows or units, an unordered time column, more than one file, a table in `inputs/data/` instead of `data/`, only what the quest gathered itself) says why in one sentence and is `not_applicable`, so a design revised after the analysis stays below `publication_ready`. A survey and `--analyze` hold nothing back, say in one sentence that it does not apply (`not_applicable` in `.fi/phased.json` and `needs/EVIDENCE.json`), and get no stage gap and no paper note. A research quest that began before this default, whose config does not set it, goes on without the two stages and says so in `run.log` at every start (`--update` approves it as it runs; a start with it on writes `phased_started` in the trace, so a quest that ran with it is never taken for an older one). It is asked in the interview on every interface (advanced: *Confirm the result on data it never saw*; on by default for research and a decision, off when exploring; off under research writes `phased: false`), fixed for the quest, and recorded with the approved settings (`core/plan_settings.py`). Only a confirmed result can be `publication_ready` (`needs/EVIDENCE.json` `phased`), and one confirmed on held-back rows only with `isolation` `docker` or `encrypted+scanned` and a decided split; run.log names the stage (`[phased] ...`), the paper carries a note on which numbers are exploratory and which confirmed, and `.fi/phased.json` keeps the record (seeds, rows held back, exploration's result). It reads no attempt record and no shadow recommendation. Each frozen version of the study is a *candidate*, confirmed once: when the confirm stage begins the hashes of its code (`code/`, without the redraw and run helpers and notes; line endings and comments do not count), frozen protocol and environment are recorded (`frozen_at_confirm`), and its confirm run's verdict is appended to `.fi/confirmations.jsonl` (`core/confirmations.py`: never rewritten, each line chained to the one before and named in the decision trace by its hash, `confirmation_recorded`; a changed or removed line is a `publication_ready` gap). The same version run again on the confirm data or seeds adds a second line and the failure counts; a version whose code or protocol (a data quest: its design) changed after its confirm run began (improve, a repair, review → implement, a redesign, `--from` with changed code) is a new candidate (`phased.new_candidate`, called before the experiment runs and before a data quest's data is read): back to exploring, confirmed on its own on seeds no earlier run used, and only its own verdict counts (`needs/EVIDENCE.json` `phased.version`, `phased.earlier_versions`). A new candidate of a quest that held rows back is `not_confirmable` (the held-back rows were read) and gets the whole files back. The improve loop does not run in the confirm stage, and a search for the best design is not run again there (`optimise.run_search(frozen=True)` keeps exploration's best design; only the check at finer settings runs, on the new seeds). A version whose confirmation did not hold puts a line on the to-do card (*the confirmation on unseen data did not agree; this version cannot be confirmed again — a changed version is a new candidate and is confirmed on its own*), and the methods paragraph counts the versions confirmed and the confirmations that did not hold.
- **A frozen protocol, and amendments a person must approve** — `core/frozen_protocol.py`. Right before the first full run (after the oracle gate has settled the protocol) the protocol is frozen into `needs/FROZEN_PROTOCOL.json` (`schema`, `version`, `run_id`, the protocol, its SHA-256, `approved_by`, `approved_at`, `source`, and `needs/protocol_versions/v<n>.json` per version). An oracle the gate had the plan rewritten with (`_declare_oracles`, a missing oracle or one without numbers) came after the person read the plan, so it is recorded in `.fi/oracles_added.json`: with `pauses.plan: ask` the quest stops again before the freeze (the same `plan` pause, headline *read the checks FI added to the plan*, naming them in `NEXT_STEP.md`, the web quest page and VS Code) and `approved_by` says they were held for the person too; with the plan stop off, `approved_by` says the engine added them after the plan was written and nobody approved them, and so does a rerun from a step approved with `--approve-as` before those oracles existed. Only oracles still in the protocol being frozen are named. `approved_by` names a person for the plan only when the quest really stopped at the plan (`.fi/paused_at_plan.flag`), not from `pauses.plan` alone. The engine's rewrite is kept in the plan's history as `engine` ("checks added by FI" on the quest page), not as a person's request. `Engine._protocol_block`, the protocol check, the oracle gate, the design prompt and `core/evidence.py` read the frozen record, never `state["design"]`, which a redesign can leave without a protocol (before the freeze existed it did, and the gates then saw nothing). A record whose hash does not match is a gap of the evidence level; a run that was never frozen (a quest begun before this) cannot reach `protocol_runtime_matched`. After the freeze a redesign keeps the frozen protocol; asking for another (a `protocol_amendment: {protocol, reason}` in the design; a `protocol` that merely differs, as when the methodology audit amends it in place, is put back and the log says what it wanted) writes `needs/PROTOCOL_AMENDMENT_PENDING.json` and stops the quest (`amendment` pause). Approval is a separate act: `launch.py --approve-amendment <quest_id> --approve-as <who>`, `POST /api/quests/<id>/amendment/approve` (the quest page's Approve button; `GET .../amendment` shows the request and the frozen record) or VSCode `@fi /approve-amendment <id>`; resuming without it keeps the frozen protocol and records the request as declined (`PROTOCOL_AMENDMENT_<n>_declined.json`). An approved amendment writes `PROTOCOL_AMENDMENT_<n>.json` (changes, reason, approver, `prespecified`), freezes a new version, and starts run `run_<n+1>`; when results had been seen the raw outcomes are moved and the code, paper, summary and evidence copied to `archive/run_<n>/`, the writer is told to state the change as post-hoc, and `publication_ready` names it as a gap. `plan.md` is not rewritten by an amendment: the frozen record is the source of truth. **Tamper detection runs unconditionally at execute's entry** (`Engine._node_execute` calls `_resolved_frozen()` before anything else, every run), regardless of `engine.protocol_check` / `oracle_check` / `run_manifest_check` — each of those can individually be turned off outside `research`, and used to also mean nothing ever validated `needs/FROZEN_PROTOCOL.json`'s own checksum while off, so a hand-edited record ran completely unchecked. `_resolved_frozen()` stops the quest (an `amendment`-kind pause) the same way regardless of which checks are on, until a person approves restoring the version saved at the freeze.
- **FI runs the trials and keeps the record (the trial contract)** — `core/trial_runner.py`. With two scripts, `simulate.py` defines `run_trial(cell, trial_id, seed) -> dict` (or `run_cell(cell)` for a deterministic study) and, only when an oracle has no `case`, `oracle() -> dict` (FI runs every oracle that has one itself); it has no loop, writes no file and prints no result. FI (`TrialsRunner`, standing in for the executor) runs every setting of the frozen protocol's grid in its own process through the quest's executor (venv, shared interpreter or container), `runs_per_setting` trials each (the trials are the replicates: `engine.execute_replicates` does not rerun the whole study), each with a seed FI derives from the base seed, the setting and the trial number; a small harness FI writes fresh for every run calls the function and returns each trial's values, status, time and warnings (printed back to stderr, so the numeric-warning check reads the simulation's warnings too). FI alone writes `raw/ledger.jsonl` (a `planned` line per setting, then one line per trial) and `raw/trials.json` (per setting: each metric's values, count and total; and the protocol's thresholds), which the analysis reads through `FI_TRIALS`. A list of values the analysis prints for one of the trials' own outcomes (`<outcome>_values`) is checked value by value against the values FI recorded (each trial's values are hash-checked against FI's ledger first), so numbers the trials never produced, or one trial's value counted twice, are a difference that sends `experiment.py` back; a list of something the analysis derived itself is not checked this way. A trial the setting's process never reported (it crashed or ran out of `timeout_s`) is recorded as failed with why. When `simulate.py` and the protocol are unchanged, the trials already run are used again and only the analysis reruns. The static protocol check does not ask the scripts to contain the grid, run count or thresholds, since FI hands them in. `Engine._run_manifest_problems` compares FI's record with the frozen protocol through `run_manifest.manifest_from_ledger` and `run_manifest.problems()` (every setting exactly the protocol's Cartesian product, `runs_per_setting` per setting, every failed trial listed, a `failure_policy` when any failed, and each `kind: "mean"` metric's `<id>_values` in the analysis output backed by that many successful trials); a difference is repaired through `execute_reflect` (`engine.run_manifest_repair_attempts`, default 1), then stops the quest (`manifest` pause, `engine.run_manifest_check: block`); `warn` records it, `off` skips. A simulation on the older contract (its own loop, its own `trial_ledger.jsonl` and `run_manifest.json`) is still counted from its ledger as before, and its status is `self_reported`, which the evidence level names as a gap; under `rigor_profile: research` it is sent back once to be rewritten on the trial contract and then stops. `needs/RUN_MANIFEST_CHECK.json` holds the status (`ok`, `self_reported`, `repairing`, `warned`, `stopped`, `single_script`, `not_applicable`, `not_run`, `off`); `core/evidence.py` requires `ok` before `protocol_runtime_matched`. A `run_cell` simulation whose protocol still asks for several runs per setting is held to one run per setting once FI finds no randomness in it (`Engine._run_cell_randomness`: no source of random numbers in its code, and up to three of the fastest settings called a second time through `trial_runner.run_cell_again` return the same numbers, so randomness only a setting not called again has can be missed; a cluster job is judged by its code alone, which can miss a library that samples inside): `run_manifest.once_per_setting` leaves the count out of the comparison, and `.fi/run.log`, the record's `note` and the analysis (so the paper) say each setting ran once; `protocol_check.run_count_notes` says it in the plan when the design's description names nothing random. A `run_cell` with a sign of randomness is sent back to define `run_trial`. `execution.split_failure` (`warn` default, `block`) decides what a reply without both scripts, or scripts that break the contract (`run_manifest.split_lint`), do. On a cluster (`execution.background_jobs`) the settings run as a **job array**, one task per setting: FI writes its harness, one spec per setting and the task list into `job/fi/` (`FI_TASKS` names `job/fi/tasks.json`), the experiment's third script `code/submit.py` submits the array the way the cluster's skill says and reports it pending or done (`fi_job`, as for any background job), the quest waits (`--watch` or a resume checks it), and when it is done FI reads every task's results and writes the ledger and the summary itself, with the same checks as a local run; a setting whose task reported nothing is recorded as failed trials with why. The protocol's oracles still run first, here, on the machine FI runs on (each oracle's `case` through the simulation function, `oracle()` only for one without a case): they are the small cases with a known answer, and one that needs the cluster's resources stops at the oracle check (set `engine.oracle_check: warn` to go on with it recorded). Tasks of one job share a filesystem, so FI's per-setting checks keep out a mistake, not a simulation set on forging another setting's results. A deterministic background job runs the same way, one `run_cell` call per setting. Real HPC has been exercised only against a fake cluster.
- **The run's own environment, recorded (and, under `rigor_profile: research`, isolated)** — `Engine._record_environment` (called right after the executor's `setup()` and again after the experiment's packages are installed; a resumed run, such as one that only takes the accept at the review pause, keeps the record made after the installs). Writes `needs/ENVIRONMENT.json` at the start of the run and replaces it after the installs (`recorded` says which of the two it is), so the record left is the environment the experiment ran on: interpreter (`sys.version`, `sys.executable`), `platform.platform()`, the sandbox, `execution.shared_interpreter` / `system_site_packages`, `isolated` (`sandbox == "docker"` or both of those `false`), and a `pip freeze` of the interpreter the experiment actually ran on (`packages`; when the freeze itself fails — an editable install whose folder was deleted makes it crash, for example — the interpreter's installed distributions are listed without pip, with `packages_source` saying so and an editable or direct install written as `name @ url`; `packages_error` only when neither works — a non-zero exit is not read as "zero packages"). `core/evidence.py` lists a non-isolated environment as a gap toward `protocol_runtime_matched` (a previous quest's installed package could have affected this run); `rigor_profile: research` forces isolation (`execution.shared_interpreter: false`, `system_site_packages: false`); outside it the shared interpreter is only recorded. A `packages_error` is a gap toward the same level (which packages the run had is unknown), and so is a list made without pip (an editable or local install in it may not be installable again).
- **Metric specs and estimator dispatch** — `core/metric_spec.py`. `protocol.metrics` is a list of `{id, estimand, kind (proportion|mean), unit, cluster (true | list name), paired, family, given}` (`given`: for a mean over a subset of the trials, the id of the declared proportion whose successes are that subset; each place the analysis reports the mean must list exactly `<given>_count` trials beside that count, `null` for a trial whose value is undefined, with a count no larger than its `<given>_total`. A result reported per stratum puts each stratum in a mapping of its own keyed like `R0=1.5` (or `R0=1.5,N=500`) with the metrics' own names inside; such a stratum needs `<given>_total`, equal to FI's own count of the trials that ran in those settings; a key shaped like a stratum that the grid does not have is a problem; what names no stratum is still counted as a whole against the trials the run reports; a result for some strata only is allowed and listed as a gap ("reported for 2 of the protocol's 10 settings"); and a stratum written into the name (`p_outbreak_R0_1_5_count`) sends the analysis back to be rewritten, with how. Under `rigor_profile: research` the mean is also checked trial by trial against FI's own record: `run_trial` returns, under the proportion's own id, 1 or 0 for every trial (the implement prompt says so; a cut-off that decides it is read from the protocol's thresholds, which FI hands every trial as `FI_THRESHOLDS`), FI takes the trials that are 1 in each reported stratum (or the run), and the stratum's `<given>_count` must be how many there are and its `<metric>_values` those same trials' values (or each divided by its own size setting), in any order — two separate pools of values no longer let a failed trial's value stand in for a successful one's. The values are one quantity (or that quantity divided by its size setting), the same in every stratum, and never a quantity that is only 0 and 1 (an `ok` flag); a mean under a wrapper key (`summary`, `results`) is held to the whole run; a record without the 1 or 0 sends the simulation back to return it, and a missing record of the trials' values while the ledger says they ran sends the trials to run again. In every profile, a count or total that is not a whole number (a fraction, NaN, a negative number, true/false, text) is a problem, never truncated. Under the trial contract each value must be a trial's own value from that stratum's own settings, or that value divided by the trial's own size setting (an axis named like a size, such as N or population, with whole-number values: a final size divided by N), equal within a relative 1e-6 (a float32 passes, a rounding does not); where the trials return the proportion itself as 0/1, each stratum's count must be FI's own count of them. When FI cannot count the subset itself and the strata hold under half the run's trials, a selection of the trials cannot be told from all of them: the analysis is sent back to report every setting as its own stratum, or the simulation to return the proportion as 0/1. In every profile, when FI cannot count the subset itself, a stratum whose values are exactly the largest (or exactly the smallest) of that setting's trials (as they are or divided by the size setting; only for a setting with enough trials to make that unlikely by chance) is sent back, saying which end and asking the simulation to return the proportion as 0/1 so FI can count the subset; a cut-off the protocol fixes in `thresholds` that falls between those values and the rest is accepted, since that is what the trials beyond a cut-off look like. This is a deterrent, not a proof: a pick that is not exactly the top (the top nine and the median), spread over pooled strata, or returned under another quantity's name passes it, and only the 0/1 record settles it. A result for some settings only that passes is still passed to the analysis, which is told to say so in the paper's limitations and summary; in a model's draft, one entry that fails the check is left out and named in the plan rather than the whole list; `id`, `kind`, `estimand` and `unit` are required (`estimand` and `unit` as non-empty text — a spec naming only an estimator, with nothing saying what it is an estimator *of*, is refused by name, the same as a protocol missing anything else a gate needs); `cluster`, `paired` and `family` are optional (`plan.normalize_protocol` checks the shape; `protocol_check.metric_notes` says in `plan.md` when a protocol with runs declares none). `metric_spec.statistics` returns `estimates` (per setting: Wilson from pooled counts, bootstrap of pooled values, or a cluster bootstrap over whole clusters, with the estimator named), `contrasts` between the settings of each `by_*` factor (two-proportion z test with Newcombe's interval; bootstrap interval with a permutation p-value; paired sign-flip permutation of the per-pair differences when `paired`; a cluster bootstrap of the difference when clustered) with `p_holm` inside the spec's `family`, `unsupported` (a paired design over clusters, a design whose `<id>_values` / `<id>_clusters` the script did not give, unequal trials per setting for a pair) and `undeclared` (metrics with counts or values and no spec). A **paired** contrast joins trial *i* of one setting to trial *i* of another by an explicit `<id>_pair_id` (one id per value, the same id naming the matching trial in every setting) when the script gives one — a mismatch between the two settings' id sets in the same seed is refused by name — falling back to raw list position (unverified) when it does not; every contrast carries `paired_id_verified` for the paired case, and a contrast joined by position is a gap in the evidence (`metric_spec.coverage_gaps`). Under `rigor_profile: research` a paired metric printed without `<name>_pair_id` is sent back to the analysis to add it (FI's per-setting summary gives the trial behind every value, `metrics[...]["trials"]`), and the quest stops if the repair does not (a setting run once has nothing to pair and is not asked). When FI runs the trials (`run_trial`) and a metric is `paired`, trial *i* of every setting gets the same seed (`trial_runner.trial_seed` with no setting in it: common random numbers), so the trial number FI hands the analysis is a real pairing. `protocol.contrasts` (`[{metric, a, b}, ...]`) lets the plan **prespecify** exactly which pairs of a factor's settings to compare instead of every pairwise combination being generated after the fact; unlisted pairs for a metric that has a prespecified list are skipped outright (not reported as unsupported), and the top-level `contrasts_prespecified` flag (and a `coverage_gaps` sentence when it is false) says which happened. All resampling is seeded — including the thinning of an oversized sample down to a cap, which draws a seeded random subset rather than every Nth row in whatever order the values arrived, so a script that writes trials in run order or by difficulty does not bias which ones get kept. `tests/test_stats_validation.py` (slow tier) validates the estimators themselves: a null-simulation / coverage check per method (Wilson, two-proportion, bootstrap mean, paired permutation, cluster bootstrap) confirming each behaves the way its "95%" / "p < 0.05" claim says, not just correct arithmetic on a fixed input. `Engine._comparison_stats` puts it under `comparison_stats.spec_statistics` for the analysis prompt and for the number and statistics audits (so the paper may quote those numbers); `agents/analyze.md` tells the model to quote them and never to judge significance from interval overlap; the code-writing prompts ask for `<name>_values`, `<name>_clusters` and (for a paired design) `<name>_pair_id`, and for trials in the same order in every setting when paired as good practice (the pair id is what actually protects a design whose loop order isn't guaranteed to match). `core/evidence.py` puts undeclared metrics, unsupported estimates/contrasts, and a factor whose contrasts are not prespecified under `statistically_adequate` (`the statistics are not shown to be adequate`).
- **Intervals that say what they are an interval of** — a probability that a script estimates from repeated trials is reported with its counts beside it in `RESULT_JSON` (`<name>`, `<name>_count`, `<name>_total`; asked for in the outline and both code-writing prompts). The engine runs the script once per seed, and `_aggregate_result_json_replicates` pools the seeds' counts: `ci_method: wilson_pooled_counts` gives a 95% Wilson interval for the pooled proportion (with `n_trials`, `n_successes`, `se`, and the seeds' own `batch_mean` / `batch_std`), where the t interval over three batch proportions used to be the headline uncertainty (for three batches of 300 runs at R0=1.5, N=5000 that interval was 0.3218-0.3382 for a run whose 900 trials give a Wilson interval of 0.3001-0.3614, and 0.2280-0.4453 for another whose 900 trials give 0.3065-0.3682). A mean over selected trials that the script reports with `<name>_values` beside it gets `bootstrap_pooled_values` (a deterministic percentile bootstrap over the pooled values, thinned to 20000; the long lists are left out of the analysis prompt and counted in `n_values`). Every other metric keeps the t interval and is labelled `t_between_seeds`, and the analyze and write prompts say that interval is a spread across seeds, not the number of trials. Counts that do not add up (not whole, more successes than trials, missing from a seed) are not pooled. Which of the two a metric is comes from the protocol's declared `kind` when it has one, and only otherwise from the names: a mean printed with `_count` equal to `_total` beside its `_values` (a conditional mean's own denominator) is pooled as values, not as a probability of 1.0. The protocol's `precision` target is held against the metric its `precision.metric` names (every pooled probability when that names none of them). The statistics check's `interval_method_mismatch` (a paper interval labelled with a named method whose endpoints ARE FI's seed-level t interval) is asked only about the intervals whose `ci_method` is `t_between_seeds`: a pooled Wilson or bootstrap interval printed under its own name is that estimator, and was condemned as a mislabel by a real quest until the interval handed to the check said which kind it was. Not done: a single-replicate run does not use counts, and the intervals of the between-strata comparisons (`comparison_stats`) still come from the seeds.
- **Pause-drop-anytime** — opt-in user-input gate at up to three natural pause points: `pauses.supply: never | after_literature | before_build | before_review | both | all` (default `never`; `both` is before_build + before_review, `all` is all three). `after_literature` splits a quest in two: it stops once the literature is saved, and `--resume` starts the second half — skills, design, experiment — with that literature already in state, without searching again; papers dropped in `inputs/papers/` meanwhile join it. When set, the engine pauses after the named stage (LangGraph `interrupt()` → clean rc=0 exit with the unified `<quest_root>/NEXT_STEP.md` explaining what to drop where), the user drops reference papers into `inputs/papers/` (PDFs/MDs, ingested by the literature node on resume) and/or datasets into `inputs/data/` (CSVs/JSON/Parquet/etc., walked into `state['user_supplied_datasets']` and surfaced in the analyze prompt under the `_user_supplied_datasets` key inside its `result_json` block — no new `$placeholder` in `analyze.md`, so the prompt stays backward-compatible), then runs `fi --resume <quest_id>`. A disk marker at `<quest_root>/.fi/paused_at_<stage>.flag` is the authoritative "already paused here" signal so a re-entry doesn't loop the user through the same drop screen (state-only tracking can't survive a LangGraph `interrupt()` raise — the partial state-list update never lands in the checkpoint). Datasets are picked up by both the design node (on after-design resume) and the analyze node (catches drops staged at after-paper), so the analyze prompt always sees fresh data regardless of which pause stage was used.
- **Auto-collect before pause** — `auto_collect_data` queries Axon and the dataset adapters with the keyword query the literature step derived from the topic, or the requery that found sources (`literature_query`; `topic + hypothesis` when there is none), and judges what it finds against `topic + hypothesis` and drops up to `engine.auto_collect_top_k` (default 5) hits into `data/auto_collected/` before pausing for the user.
- **Dataset adapters** — `engine.dataset_adapters` opts into structured external lookups (`worldbank`, `wikipedia`). Writes evidence into `auto_collected/<adapter>/`.

### Knowledge layer

- **Axon-backed retrieval + write-back** — literature retrieval and cross-quest memory.
- **Web research toggle** — `knowledge.web_search` (interview question **Web research**, default on, independent of the Axon layer) runs a general web search (Brave when `BRAVE_API_KEY` is set, else keyless DuckDuckGo) in parallel with academic retrieval, and `knowledge.web_fetch_pages` (default on) fetches each hit's full page text so the writer can quote it and the plotter can mine numbers.
- **Literature downloaded for every quest** — the literature node writes each retrieved source to `data/literature/lit_NNN_<slug>.md` (provenance front matter + content) for **every** quest, simulation and observational alike — so the collected corpus is always auditable on disk even on the simulation path (which never reaches `auto_collect_data`).
- **Bot-wall handling + open-access full-text cascade** — many academic sources (PMC, MDPI, …) serve a reCAPTCHA / "checking your browser" wall on a `200`, and paywalled publishers (ScienceDirect, Wiley, IEEE, Springer) serve an abstract-only stub; both are detected and discarded (paywall stubs via a schema.org `isAccessibleForFree` / known-paywall-vendor / call-to-action check) rather than stored as content. For an academic source the fetcher prefers the clean open-access full text directly: it resolves the article's `PMCID` / `DOI` / arXiv-id (cross-resolving via Europe PMC when a web result carries only one), then tries, in descending order of text quality, **the PMC BioC API** (clean section-labelled passages, no HTML cruft), **Europe PMC** `fullTextXML`, the **preprint** server (arXiv / bioRxiv / medRxiv), **Unpaywall** (every OA location, published-version + direct-PDF + repository copies first), and finally **Semantic Scholar** / **CORE** (when an API key is set). Whatever is recovered lands as real full text instead of a snippet. **Formulae survive.** BioC hands back its passages with the mathematics removed, so a sentence that stated a formula arrives truncated to its punctuation — a stored record read `the probability of no outbreak is either 1 or .` where the paper says `(1/R₀)ⁱ` — and a claim the source genuinely supports then cannot be verified against it. So when a recovered PMC body has no mathematics left in it *and* that article's XML does carry formula markup, the Europe PMC XML is kept instead; an article that never had formulae keeps its BioC text and pays one extra keyless request. Flattened XML also keeps its paragraph breaks (and drops journal front matter and the reference list), so the excerpt chunker splits on paragraphs instead of mid-word.
- **Clean main-content extraction** — HTML pages are run through `trafilatura` (when installed) to isolate the article body and drop nav / menus / related-article / reference cruft, falling back to a built-in tag-strip extractor otherwise. Figure captions + image alt-text are appended so the writer is aware of a source's visuals.
- **Relevance-selected excerpts** — full text is fetched and stored uncapped on disk, but each node's prompt receives only the passages most relevant to the quest question (`knowledge.literature_excerpt_chars`, default 4000). Ranking is `knowledge.passage_ranking`: **`auto`** (default) blends sentence-transformer cosine with lexical term overlap so a result stated in *paraphrase* ("death rate" for a "mortality" query) is still surfaced — `FI_OFFLINE` loads the model from the local cache rather than skipping it, falling back to pure lexical only if no model loads at all. `hybrid` and `embed` request the semantic path explicitly (still falling back to lexical if the model is unavailable); `lexical` forces the dependency-free term-overlap path. So a result or number buried mid-document reaches the writer / plotter — not just the abstract that happens to sit in the first 2000 characters.
- **Independent Axon vs external caps** — `knowledge.top_k` (default 8, RAG precision) is independent of `knowledge.external_top_k` (default 20, web breadth). The literature node passes both; ideate/cross_check seeds respect the smaller per-call cap.
- **Audience-aware citations** — `output.audience: "external" | "internal"`. External papers drop FI-internal cross-quest entries from References; internal papers keep everything.
- **Further reading** — web pages are listed apart from the scholarly sources a claim is cited to. The writer's prior-work block numbers papers `[1]`, `[2]`… and labels web pages `[W1]`, `[W2]`…; the engine writes the paper's `## References`, listing the papers the text cites, and a `## Further reading` section listing every web page; a list the writer wrote itself is replaced. Every output labels from one de-duplicated list: claim grounding accepts a `[W<k>]` source, the poster's reference band lists the web pages its text cites as `[W<k>]` with their site name, the slide deck leaves the web pages to the paper, and the pages are exported to `paper/further_reading.bib` / `.csl.json`. The review's depth floor (at least 3 sources discussed by content for a journal-length paper) counts References and Further reading together. Applies to every quest.
- **Axon sidecar auto-launch** — every CLI / `--serve` boots `python -m axon.api` if one isn't already listening. Skip with `--no-axon-sidecar` or `FI_NO_AXON_SIDECAR=1`.
- **Axon through the running service** — `knowledge.axon_mode` (default `http`) makes every FI process (a quest, `--serve`, `--ingest` / `--summarize` / `--digest`) use the Axon service that is already listening instead of building an Axon of its own; `in_process` is the old behaviour for a machine that cannot run the service. `core/axon_http.py::AxonHTTPBrain` has the three methods FI uses of a brain (`ingest`, `finalize_ingest`, `search_raw`) over `/add_texts`, `/graph/finalize` and `/search/raw`. The service has one active project and refuses a request that names another, so each operation switches it to FI's project (`frontier-insight`, created on first use) and back to whatever was active, under a lock that is held across processes and threads (so two quests cannot switch under each other, and a killed process cannot leave it held); a run of operations can share one switch with `AxonHTTPBrain.session()`. Anything else on the same service (an editor's Axon tools) sees FI's project for the length of one operation. If the service is down FI starts it, and if that fails the quest runs without the knowledge base and its log says why (no second Axon is built inside the process). A store lock left by a killed server is removed before starting one (Axon's own start-up refuses and exits on it, so the wait ended in a silent timeout), and looking for the service no longer prints Axon's warning for every retired key of its config file. `knowledge.axon_config` applies only to `in_process`; a running service keeps the configuration it was started with. `--serve --no-axon-sidecar` no longer starts a service.
- **Axon endpoint discovery** — the sidecar's port isn't fixed (Axon resolves it as `--port` > `AXON_PORT` > its `config.yaml` `api.port` > `8420`), so FI searches instead of assuming: the running server's own lock file (`<projects_root>/.axon-api.lock`), then Axon's `config.yaml`, then the built-in defaults, probing each until one answers. All three surfaces use the same search, so a sidecar you started by hand is found without configuring anything. `AXON_API_BASE` (or the VSCode `frontierInsight.axonUrl` setting) pins FI to one specific instance — useful for a remote or containerised Axon, and deliberately *not* a fallback, since two Axon instances hold different corpora. When nothing answers, the error names every endpoint tried and where each came from. In VSCode the search also runs patiently at startup — the extension keeps watching for `frontierInsight.axonStartupWaitSec` (10 minutes by default) and stays silent throughout, so starting Axon minutes after opening the editor produces no notice at all; only a sidecar that never appears does.
- **Offline / air-gapped models** — `knowledge.offline` + `knowledge.models_dir` (or `FI_OFFLINE` / `FI_MODELS_DIR` env) load the embedding + reranker weights from a local HF cache with no network call. Produce a shippable cache on a connected machine with `launch.py --export-models <dir>`. Both the in-process layer and the sidecar honour it. See `docs/INSTALL.md`.
- **arXiv request queue** — every connection FI makes to arXiv (full-text HTML and PDFs, abstract pages, figures, arXiv copies found through Unpaywall or Semantic Scholar, and the headless-browser fallback) goes through one queue: one connection at a time, at least 3 seconds apart, as arXiv asks of automated clients. The queue's state is a lock file under the cache directory, so it holds across processes too — the web UI and VSCode run each quest in its own process. A rate limit (HTTP 429) pauses the whole queue for 1, then 2, then 4 minutes; if arXiv is still refusing, that quest stops contacting arXiv and the other sources carry on, and the source failure report says so. Work with a time budget (full-text enrichment has 90 s) gives up on a slot it cannot get in time rather than sleeping through a backoff. Successful responses are cached on disk for 24 hours (`FI_CACHE_DIR`, default `~/.frontier-insight/cache`; `FI_ARXIV_CACHE=0` disables), so a resumed or repeated quest does not download the same papers again.
- **Multi-source literature router** — arXiv / OpenAlex / Crossref / Semantic Scholar / PubMed / CORE / OpenAIRE / DOAJ / Google Scholar in parallel, deduplicated by id and normalized title. CORE, OpenAIRE and DOAJ need no key; they carry the open-access humanities and social-science books, theses and journals the STEM-leaning indices cover thinly, and the router is told to use them for those topics. CORE also accepts an optional `CORE_API_KEY` for a higher rate limit. OpenAIRE and DOAJ require every query word to match, so when a longer query finds nothing FI retries once with its first three words. arXiv is searched through OpenAlex's arXiv source rather than arXiv's own query API, which has been capacity-throttled for everyone since arXiv's move to the cloud; full text still comes from arxiv.org. `knowledge.openalex_api_key` / `OPENALEX_API_KEY` unlocks OpenAlex's full daily budget (keyless is about 100 searches a day, and arXiv searches count against it); it is sent in a request header, so it never shows up in a logged request URL. And `knowledge.semantic_scholar_api_key` / `SEMANTIC_SCHOLAR_API_KEY` gets Semantic Scholar off its mostly rate-limited shared pool.
- **General web search** — `knowledge.web_search` (default on) adds a general-web layer (Brave when `BRAVE_API_KEY` / `knowledge.brave_api_key` is set, else keyless DuckDuckGo) that runs **in parallel** with Axon + the academic adapters for every quest and is merged + deduped by URL/DOI/title. This is what lets non-academic topics (company financials, markets, current events, culture, how-to) retrieve real sources instead of irrelevant nearest-neighbour papers. Web hits carry their `url` / `site` as citation metadata. With `knowledge.web_fetch_pages` (default on) the result pages' readable text is fetched (not just the snippet) so the writer can quote real content and the plot step can mine numbers from it.
- **Blocked pages render one at a time** — `knowledge.headless_fetch` (default on) retries a page that answered 403 or came back empty by rendering it in a headless browser, which clears the Cloudflare / JS-only walls a plain request cannot. Those renders **take turns**: one browser, and one browser driver, at a time for the whole process. Each render starts its own driver process and drives it over a pipe, and several of them running at once can break that pipe — a failure Node reports as an unhandled `'error'` event, which ends the process outright rather than raising it, so nothing downstream ever gets the chance to record it: the quest is simply gone, without even a source failure report to say what happened. Taking turns is slower for a quest with several blocked pages, since the renders now add up instead of overlapping, and that is the deliberate trade — a slower literature step instead of a lost quest. **Only the render takes turns.** Everything else in the same batch still runs in parallel: the ordinary page fetches, the open-access cascade (Europe PMC, PMC BioC, OpenAlex, arXiv, Unpaywall) and the downloads of free copies. A render that cannot get its turn before the full-text budget expires gives up, keeps the search snippet, and is listed in the source failure report like any other source that could not be fetched.
- **Relevance guard** — `knowledge.relevance_guard` (default on) scores the auto-collected sources against the topic and drops confident-but-off-topic hits; if nothing is on-topic it pauses for user-supplied sources rather than writing an honest-but-useless "pipeline failure" paper on garbage. Fail-open on any judge error.
- **Literature relevance floor** — `knowledge.relevance_min_score` (default 0.20) is a cheap, deterministic complement to the LLM relevance guard, applied in the **literature node for every quest** (the guard runs only on the auto_collect path, which survey/simulation quests skip).
