Each retrieved doc is scored by embedding cosine (the shared all-MiniLM model) against the **topic**, and the off-topic tail is dropped before it reaches analyze/write — so a "sculpture in pop culture" quest no longer carries change-point-math papers. Never-starve: `knowledge.relevance_min_keep` (default 3) always retains the top-scoring docs; fail-open when embeddings are unavailable (FI_OFFLINE); `0.0` disables.
- **Literature screen** — `knowledge.literature_screen` (default on). The embedding floor scores word overlap, so a journal's table of contents, or a paper on another system that shares the search terms, still passes it. After the floor, one batched call (node `literature_screen`, run at temperature 0 like the other gates) grades every retrieved source 0–3 on one question: could the paper cite this for a claim? Scholarly records need a 2 to stay. Web pages are dropped only at 0, because they are kept for the text the writer quotes. The rubric tells the model that for a quest without an experiment, books and chapters count as fully as articles. At least `knowledge.relevance_min_keep` sources survive, best grades first. A failed or unreadable call keeps everything, and a source left ungraded is kept. Sources the user supplied (`knowledge.local_papers`, `inputs/papers/`) are never screened. Kept sources carry `screen_grade`. **What each source got is kept:** the on-topic score the floor gave every retrieved source and the screen's grade for every source, with whether it was kept and the reason in plain words (for example "graded 1; needs 2 or higher", "kept to reach the minimum of 3 sources", "the screen call failed, so every source is kept"), are written to `.fi/literature_queries.json`, the file that already lists the search queries, as entries of stage `floor` and `screen` (one per search pass; a resumed or re-run literature step adds another set; the screen entry also names the model call that graded, in `.fi/model_calls.jsonl`). Each entry carries a digest, and the file is the one the seal already names, so a verdict edited after the quest is sealed is a gap. Dropped sources are listed there too; the paper's own reference list is unchanged. No interface shows this record yet (the web quest page's file list and zip download leave `.fi/` out): open the file in the quest folder.
- **Foundational works** — `knowledge.foundational_works` (default on). A search engine matches every word of a query, so the original papers and standard textbooks a topic rests on rarely come back: for a quest comparing integrators on a damped oscillator, none of the three facet queries returned Verlet (1967), Hairer, Lubich & Wanner, or Butcher in OpenAlex's first 200 hits, and sorting by citations only pulled in famous off-topic work. After the relevance floor, one call (node `literature_foundational`) asks the model for up to eight foundational works (any beyond the eighth are ignored); each is looked up by title in OpenAlex (sorted by citations, books and chapters allowed) and kept only when a record's title matches and its year is within two or its authors include the suggested first author. **A suggestion whose title matches nothing is looked up once more, by author and year.** The model names works whose author and year it knows and invents the title: for one topic it named Whittle (1955) under four different titles, none of them real, and it paraphrased Gillespie (1977) and Kermack (1927) so that their titles matched nothing, while OpenAlex holds all three. So the second request asks OpenAlex for the works by that surname (a whole word of an author's name, so "Ball" is not "Ballesteros") published within a year of the suggested one, most cited first, 25 of them, books and chapters allowed, and takes the first that shares a word of subject matter with the suggested title (a word of three letters or fewer and a short stop list do not count, and only a word's first six letters are compared, so "epidemic" and "epidemics" are one word). The suggested title is used only to tell that author's papers apart, never to match. The record found is labelled `suggested as a foundational work; found by author and year, the suggested title did not match`, the title the writer sees and cites is the record's own, and the run log's outcome line reads `<suggested> -> <the record's title>, by author and year`. One record at most per suggestion, and no second request after a lookup OpenAlex refused with HTTP 429, since a spent budget refuses it as well. What it takes is a candidate, not a finding: the literature screen grades it like any foundational candidate, and that grading is what keeps off-topic work out. Tried without the word check on the 19 suggestions the model's titles failed to find across 76 logged runs of one topic, and on five other topics (lithography, quantum noise, optical imaging, mammal evolution, numerical integration), 92 candidates went to the screen (the shipped lookup applies its word check first and would have thinned them, so this measures the screen's judgement of an author's works, not the check's): every work from another field (surgery, genetics, marketing, atmospheric reanalysis) was graded 0 or 1 and dropped, and 14 were kept, of which 6 were the canonical works (Gillespie 1976 and 1977, Kermack 1927, Whittle 1955, Abbe 1873, Simpson's *The Major Features of Evolution*), 7 were real neighbouring papers by the same authors (sequels of Kermack's, a Diekmann textbook) and 1 was a wrong paper of the right field (*Sauropoda*, for a topic on mammals). Replayed through the changed lookup and the real screen on the suggestions three quests had lost, it found and kept Whittle (1955, graded 3) and Gillespie (1977), the works that cost those quests canonical-works points. It does not always find the work meant: a common surname puts a more cited paper of another subject ahead of it (Bernstein 1993 returned *Quantum cellular automata*, which the screen dropped, not the Bernstein-Vazirani paper), and a wrong year returns a neighbouring paper of the author's instead. It has not been run through a whole quest: whether the writer then cites what it finds, so whether a canonical-works score moves, is not measured. The works that at least two retrieved papers cite (two OpenAlex requests over their reference lists) are added too. Works already retrieved are dropped. That is up to eighteen OpenAlex requests per literature pass (eight title lookups, up to eight author-and-year lookups for the ones that matched nothing, and the two over the reference lists), sent one at a time with a second between them; without an `OPENALEX_API_KEY` the keyless daily budget covers fewer quests. A lookup OpenAlex refuses with HTTP 429 looks exactly like a work it does not hold, so the lookup logs a warning with the number of refusals and the `OPENALEX_API_KEY` hint: on one real day four concurrent quests each met 13–15 refusals once the keyless daily budget was spent, and found no foundational work. The rest join the candidates labelled foundational, and the literature screen grades them with one extra rubric line: a method's original paper or textbook is a 2 when the paper would cite it for something it uses, however old, and a famous work the question does not use is still a 0 or 1. Their `data/literature/` files record how each was found (`foundational:`). A failed call or lookup adds nothing. The run log lists every work the model suggested as `title (year, authors)`, untruncated, and then which were found in OpenAlex and added, which were already among the search results, and which were dropped because OpenAlex had no matching record, so a work the model never named can be told from one OpenAlex does not hold. **The writer is asked to cite them.** Being retrieved, screened in and labelled foundational does not make a paper cite a work, so the writer's prior-work block ends with a short note that lists the foundational works in the citable set (authors, year, title, whether it is a book or how many retrieved papers cite it) under the labels the writer cites them by, with one instruction: cite each one that bears on the paper's claims where it belongs (the original paper for a method or relation the paper uses, the standard textbook for the field), and none that does not bear on the paper just to cite it. The note is left out when the citable set holds no foundational work. The review prompt carries the counterpart as advice, never as a forced hit: an advisory list of the foundational works in the citable set that the paper does not cite, which the reviewer may ask for under its suggestions and is told not to flag or to send the paper back for on that alone. The run log records, after each draft, how many foundational works were in the prior-work block, how many the draft cites and which it left out.
- **Thin sources are marked for the writer** — a retrieved work whose stored text is only its title, or a book with a short blurb and no full text, cannot show a finding, a number or a mechanism, yet a graded quest cited a 57-character title-only record for "stochastic extinction" and a book blurb for "the effective R0 drops more rapidly". The writer's prior-work block (in the write prompt and in the edit prompt of a revise) puts `[title only]` or `[short blurb only]` after the title of such an entry, and so does the foundational-works list; the write instructions say once that such an entry may be cited only to say the work exists or for what its own title states, never for a specific finding, number or mechanism. The work stays in the list and every other prompt that reads the literature is unchanged. The test is on the stored text: a record with full text is never marked; one whose text after the title is under 100 characters is `title only`; a book without full text is `short blurb only`. Over the 2,321 sources of the 131 stored quests that kept their literature, 513 are title-only (508 with nothing after the title, five with 22–85 characters, none with 86–99) and 62 are book blurbs of 760–1,376 characters: 575 in all (25%), and none of them has an abstract or full text. A length cut alone could not separate a blurb from an abstract (137 articles have an abstract of 700–1,499 characters), which is why a book is decided by its type. On the write prompt of a stored quest with 19 marked entries the marks and the sentence add about 120 tokens to 21,800. A record with only its title is also checked in code after the claim check: a sentence that cites only title-only records and says at least four content words that those records' titles, authors and stored text do not contain ("Kermack and McKendrick [1] introduced the SIR model" adds one or two to a title that names its subject; a specific result adds many more) is marked `unsupported` in the claim ledger, however the check judged it. A claim the check grounded in such a record is changed to unsupported (the quote it gave can only have been the title), a sentence it did not list as a claim is added as one, and a claim it already marked unsupported, or grounded in the run's own results, is left as it is; a book's blurb is text the check can read and is not covered. On the 72 stored SIR papers this finds 9 sentences: the check had marked 6 of them unsupported and had not listed the other 3 as claims at all, and read by hand each of those 3 attributes a specific statement to a record that holds only a title (one was the graded citation loss of a final draft). The review's advisory about foundational works the paper does not cite carries the same `[title only]` / `[short blurb only]` marks and says to ask for such a work only to say it exists or for what its title states: in one stored quest a reviewer's advice to cite a title-only book for a claim led a rewrite to cite it twice, and the claim check then marked both sentences unsupported. All three interfaces share the engine.
- **Retracted sources are marked and never count as support** — after the literature search de-duplicates what it found, every source with a DOI is looked up in Crossref (a paper you pinned in `knowledge.local_papers` or dropped into `inputs/papers/` usually has none, so its DOI is first looked up by title: a Crossref record counts only when its title is the same once case and punctuation are ignored and, where the paper states them, the first author and the year (within one) agree, and when no single record fits it is "could not be checked", never a guess), which holds the Retraction Watch database and publishers' own notices as update notices on the retracted work (one request per 40 DOIs, one request at a time within a quest, no email sent; it runs whenever `knowledge.enabled` is on, with no separate switch). A source whose latest retraction, withdrawal or removal notice is not followed by a reinstatement gets `[retracted]` after its title wherever a prompt lists it (the prior-work block of every step, and the claim check's list of sources); the write instructions say not to cite it, it is left out of the foundational works the writer and the reviewer are pointed to, and it is left off the list of papers to download (`needs/WANTED_PAPERS.md`) that the same literature pass writes, so the papers pause never asks for it. After the claim check, any claim grounded in it, and any sentence of the paper that cites it, is marked `unsupported` in the claim ledger, however the check judged it, and the review must flag unsupported claims. A paper therefore cannot cite a retracted work at all, not even to discuss the retraction. A correction or an expression of concern is recorded but does not mark the source. A lookup that gets no answer (no network, a timeout, an error status, an answer in a shape FI does not know), a DOI Crossref does not hold (arXiv DOIs are not sent) or a DOI that cannot be read is "could not be checked", never "not retracted", and is asked again on the next literature pass; when Crossref cannot be reached at all, the remaining requests of that pass are not sent, so an offline machine loses one request's timeout (15 s to connect, and up to 15 s more to read through a proxy that accepts the connection but never answers), and nothing about the lookup stops a quest. Several quests of a `--fleet` each ask on their own, so a busy moment can leave some sources "could not be checked". `run.log` gets one line, for example "Checked 23 sources for retractions: 1 retracted (…), 2 could not be checked (Crossref has no record of this DOI), 20 not retracted", or "Could not check 23 sources for retractions (Crossref could not be reached: no connection)" (the reason names no connection, a proxy or a certificate problem, with "mostly:" before it when the sources failed for different reasons, and arXiv preprints are counted apart so they never hide it); each source's answer, with the notices, is kept in `.fi/literature_queries.json` (stage `retractions`), which the trace's seal covers; and the to-do card that the CLI, the web page and VS Code show names the retracted sources.
- **Topic-aware LLM source routing** — agent picks venues (web + academic) from the source catalog.
- **Citable record types only** — scholarly indices also hold datasets, figure components, peer-review reports, dictionary entries and journal issues, none of which a claim can cite. Crossref, OpenAlex and OpenAIRE searches keep journal articles, conference papers, preprints and reviews. A quest without an experiment (a survey, a history, a humanities or policy question — `no_simulation` resolved on) also keeps books, book chapters and dissertations, because much of that scholarship is published there. The rule follows the resolved run mode rather than a separate subject classifier. Each retrieved record carries its `work_type`.
- **Keyword search queries, three facets** — the literature node does not hand the topic statement to the search engines, because a paragraph-long topic tends to match nothing in keyword-based academic search. One small call (node `literature_query`, routable through `provider.node_models` like any other) turns the topic, the chosen idea and, on a re-entry, the design's hypothesis into three short keyword queries, one per facet: the core subject, a specific angle and the wider frame. A single query reaches only the work written in its own words. The wording follows the kind of quest: methods, systems and measured quantities when there is an experiment; the names scholars write under (works, people, periods, places, movements) when there is not. The facets share one source-routing decision and only the first also runs web search. Their results are interleaved and deduplicated, and legal full text is fetched once, for the sources that survive the relevance screen. DOAJ requests are paced to its two-per-second limit. Any failure falls back to the previous title + topic string. The queries sent are kept on the quest state as `literature_queries`, and the first also as `literature_query`; every derivation is listed in `.fi/literature_queries.json` with why it was made, which model answered and which line of the record of model calls it came from, each entry with a digest over all of that (a failed derivation is listed too, with why it gave no query, and never reused); FI only adds entries. The literature step derives them once per search pass and set of inputs: run again with the same topic, chosen direction and hypothesis (a resume after a pause runs the step again), it reuses the queries it derived — only FI's own entry, whose digest still checks out and which names an answered call in `.fi/model_calls.jsonl` for this same prompt, a call no other entry names: one that fails any of these is not used, `run.log` says so and the queries are derived again (an entry from before entries carried a digest is derived once more) — so no second call is spent and the search does not drift to other papers; a new pass (broadening the literature), changed inputs or another model for `literature_query` derive them again. To search with your own queries, write them in `inputs/search_queries.txt`, one per line (the first three are used; `#` lines are skipped): the literature step uses them instead of deriving any, and records them as your revision (the trace names the file and its hash); remove the file to go back to FI's own. What protects this record: the digest has no key, so it catches an accidental or uninformed edit, not a deliberate one that recomputes it (the call check narrows this, but the record keeps hashes, not the answer's text, so an entry that copies a real call id and its prompt hash with other queries, while the genuine entry is gone, cannot be told from the real one); the trace hashes the file after every step, so a change between steps shows there; and under `rigor_profile: research` the seal names it, so once the quest is closed any change is a gap. "Only adds entries" holds for FI itself, not for the file: before the seal a person can still delete it (below), and an unreadable copy is set aside. The file grows by one entry per derivation (a few per quest). To force a fresh derivation, delete `.fi/literature_queries.json`; a copy that is not a readable record is kept as `.fi/literature_queries.json.bad.<time>` and the queries are derived again (one that is only locked for a moment is left as it is). The `ideate` step's few grounding sources are searched the same way, with the first query derived from the topic alone (one more small call, only when the knowledge layer is on); the no-simulation data collection reuses the literature step's query.
- **Local paper feed** — `knowledge.local_papers` pins paywalled PDFs / MDs to the retrieval head. An entry may be a **file or a directory** — a directory is scanned recursively for `.pdf` / `.md` / `.txt`, so a quest can be pointed at a folder that already holds enough research material.
- **Pause-for-user-papers gate** — `pauses.papers` (interview question **Supply paywalled papers**; default **on**, `false` for an unattended run, which does not pause but logs one line saying how many papers were left for a person and still writes the list) pauses when a real, resolvable paper (DOI / arXiv-id / PMID or an academic adapter) came back as abstract-only — the cases the open-access cascade genuinely couldn't fetch (SPIE / IEEE / Elsevier …). It writes a single ranked **`needs/WANTED_PAPERS.md`** — most relevant first (scored against the topic + hypothesis), each with a download link, what it's about, and its status — plus per-paper `needs/<slug>.json`. The user downloads the few that matter, drops the PDFs into `inputs/papers/`, and runs `fi --resume`; the literature node ingests them as `source=user_supplied` full text. Resuming from that pause without adding any is the other answer it offers: the quest goes on with those papers read from their abstracts, records the decision in the trace (`papers_declined`) and in `.fi/papers_declined.json`, lists it on the to-do card (*N paper(s) you went on without are read from their abstracts only*), and does not ask for those papers again. A later pause asks only for papers not declined and says how many were. An unanswered pause is never taken for an answer: changing the quest (`--update`) forgets both the pending request and the papers declined, and running it again from a step or re-opening it forgets the pending request but keeps the declined ones (those do not search the literature again). In the **Web GUI** the quest page shows the wanted list with an upload box + Resume; the **VSCode** extension surfaces the same list with drop-and-resume instructions.
- **Full-text fetch** — `knowledge.try_fetch_full_text` (default on) downloads the full text of the scholarly sources that survive the literature screen, **only when the paper is free to read**: a source that says it is open access (arXiv, PubMed Central, bioRxiv / medRxiv, a fully open-access publisher such as MDPI, PLOS or Frontiers, or an OpenAlex / Semantic Scholar / CORE / OpenAIRE / DOAJ record that names a free copy) or one whose free copy Unpaywall, Europe PMC, Semantic Scholar or CORE confirms. For a free paper FI requests only that free copy, never its DOI or publisher page. A paper that is not confirmed free is not requested from the publisher, and a web hit that looks like a journal article (a DOI, an article path, a known journal site) is not opened either, even from a network with a subscription (VPN, campus login): it goes on the wanted list and a person downloads it. Two more checks back this up: every redirect is checked again before it is followed, in the free-copy lookups (Unpaywall, Semantic Scholar, the preprint servers) and in the headless render too, so a redirect to a journal page is never requested (a redirect that stays inside one site is checked by its final address instead); and if a page or PDF from a site FI does not recognise says in its own metadata (or, for a PDF, on its first page) that it is a journal article and nothing confirms it free, its text is discarded unread and the paper goes on the list. For a site FI does not know, one request for that landing page can still happen before the check, and a second when that page answers 403 and is then rendered in the headless browser. Login walls that still slip through are rejected by a content-type and `%PDF` check. The whole batch shares `knowledge.full_text_fetch_total_s`; set `false` for abstracts only. Every PDF FI reads — fetched, dropped in `inputs/papers/`, or listed in `knowledge.local_papers` — is read by `core/pdf_text.py`: every page, up to `knowledge.full_text_max_kb` (10 MB), with words split by the typesetter's line-end hyphen joined and ligatures spelt out (PyMuPDF when you have installed it, otherwise pypdfium2; a pypdfium2 page whose two columns were stored line by line is re-read column by column). A scanned page (a page image with no text layer, as most papers from before the 1990s are) is read by OCR — tesseract when it and `pytesseract` are installed, otherwise RapidOCR (`pip install rapidocr onnxruntime`, in `requirements.txt`; its default models, about 31 MB, are inside the wheel, so once installed it needs no network; for a machine that is never online, `pip download rapidocr onnxruntime -d wheels/` on one that is and `pip install --no-index --find-links wheels/ rapidocr onnxruntime` there, or install tesseract) — never by a language model. A fetched scan is set aside during the fetch and read after it, within ten minutes for the batch, and each OCR result is cached by the PDF's SHA-256 under `~/.frontier-insight/ocr_cache/`. With no OCR engine a scanned page is named as unread in the run log, never passed off as text. What a source's text is, is recorded as `content_quality`: `full_text`, `abstract_only` (a fetch that brought back little more than the abstract), `preview_only` (mostly a table of contents), or `snippet_only`; only `full_text` counts as a source read in full. The whole text is kept in `data/literature/.full_text/` and in `data/literature/lit_*.md`; the quest state holds each source's first 64 KB, and the passage selection and the claim check's quote check read the whole text back from disk.
- **Figures from the papers** — the captioned figures ("Figure 3.", "Fig. 3:", "FIG. 3 ..."; up to 40 a PDF) whose drawing sits above its caption are cut out of each PDF FI reads, fetched or dropped in `inputs/papers/`, by `core/pdf_figures.py`, from the page's own layout and never by a model: the drawing above the caption (its panels, axis labels and legend), stopped at a table's or another figure's caption above; on a scanned page, the region between the caption and the body text above, found in the OCR lines. They are saved as `data/literature/figures/lit_NNN_fig<N>_p<page>.png`, listed with their captions in a `## Figures` section of each `lit_*.md`, and cached by the PDF's SHA-256 under `~/.frontier-insight/figure_cache/`. With `knowledge.read_figures` (default on), right after the literature a model picks, from the captions, the figures that show values this question can use (one text call for every 150 captions), then reads every one it picked, four images a call (each decision and reading is kept in `.fi/figure_readings.json` at once, so a stop part-way through never pays for a figure twice; a call that fails is tried once more, then the figure is left for a later pass); what it read is appended to that source's text under its caption, so the design, the analysis and the writer see it like the rest of the paper. The model is the one set for the step `figures` in `provider.node_models`, else the main model. Measured on the ResNet paper's three result plots: a mid-size model's values matched the curves and the paper's own tables (ResNet-110 read as about 6.5% error, the paper's table says 6.43%); the smallest model was several points off, so a cheaper model is not automatically good enough here. A model that cannot take images stops the quest and says how to set one that can, or to turn `read_figures` off; a stop there resumes at this step, not the search. What a model reads off a figure is its observation, not the source's words: the design, the analysis and the writer see it, but the claim check looks a quote up in the source's own text only, shows the readings beside it labelled as a model's, and a sentence resting only on a reading is recorded as an unverified model observation, never a grounded citation. The data step does not list the figure images (their captions are already in `lit_*.md`).
- **What a source says is read, never obeyed** — text planted in a paper or web page ("ignore the previous instructions and ...") reaches the model like FI's own words unless something says otherwise. Every block of retrieved text a prompt carries (the prior work that ideation, the design, the analysis, the cross-check, the writer and the claim check read; the candidates the literature screen and relevance guard grade; figure captions; collected web pages and data files; earlier FI results read back from the knowledge base) sits between two markers, after one sentence saying it is material to read and cite, not instructions. Both markers carry a key made from the block's own text, so a source cannot write the closing marker, whatever letters it uses; a copy of the markers' words in a source is also removed, and nothing else in its text is changed. Each source is also checked for text addressed to an AI model (a narrow list of phrasings such as "ignore all previous instructions" or "as an AI language model, you must"; ordinary sentences such as "the instructions given to participants" or "participants were told to disregard the previous instructions" do not count) and for text hidden from a reader (invisible Unicode characters; text a PDF draws in white on the page, invisibly on a bare page, or below 2 pt, in a fetched paper, one you drop in `inputs/papers/` or one in `knowledge.local_papers`). A hit only flags the source: it is kept whole and used as usual, its entry in the prompts is marked `[flagged: may contain hidden instructions]`, the trace records which source and why (`check source_text: flagged`) and `run.log` says so in one line. The quest never stops for it and there is no switch. A paper about prompt injection quotes such phrasings and is flagged too; nothing is lost when it is. Not found: an order worded outside the list, near-white text, text covered by a white box, white or invisible text placed over an image (that is where a scan keeps its searchable text, so it is left alone), and (without PyMuPDF) text inside a figure or a whole page the PDF includes as one unit. Data files you supply are fenced, not checked. Measured on 2,942 source texts stored by earlier quests (66 million characters, none of them planted): none was flagged and none was changed; the hidden-text check found nothing in 108 real PDFs either. Figures read from images are covered by an instruction in the figure-reading prompt, not by the markers.
- **Structured ingest** — title-searchable spine docs + citation-header'd body + topic rollups.

### Outputs

- **Paper PDF** via pandoc + LaTeX — real venue-flavored templates for `generic` / `neurips` / `iclr` / `ieee_access` two-column / `nature_mi`. Preprocessor lifts `# H1` into YAML `title:`, lifts `## Abstract` into YAML `abstract:` and the `**Keywords:**` line under it into `keywords:` (printed under the abstract and set as the PDF's keywords), shifts heading levels by −1, dedupes `"Title. Title."` patterns, puts a float barrier just above the reference list so a figure LaTeX is holding back for a later page is placed before that list instead of printing among the references (with no figure waiting the barrier does nothing, so a paper whose figures already sit beside their text keeps its layout unchanged), gives a figure line a blank line before and after it in the render copy when it lacks one (pandoc gives an image a numbered caption only when the image is a paragraph of its own, and three figures written one under the other printed with no caption at all in a stored quest: 5 pages with the captions, 4 without; the page-limit check renders the same copy, so it counts them), sets PDF metadata via `\hypersetup`. A figure is capped at 33% of the text height in every paper, so two figures and their captions fit on a page with text above LaTeX's `\topfraction`: the deferred-float queue drains beside the text that discusses it instead of backing up until the reference list, which is what put a figure after the references in the first place. Math is read in both spellings — `$x$` and `\(x\)` inline, `$$x$$` and `\[x\]` display — because writers use both; pandoc's markdown understands only the dollar forms on its own, so a paper written the backslash way used to reach LaTeX as text and stop at the first command (`Missing $ inserted`). The LaTeX engine is found via a 3-tier search: `pdflatex` (MiKTeX/TeX Live) → system `tectonic` → repo-local `tools/tectonic[.exe]`. pdflatex stops at the first Chinese, Japanese or Korean character, so a paper with such text in its title, body or author line compiles with **XeLaTeX + `xeCJK`** in an installed CJK font instead.
  - Font choice: Noto Sans CJK / Source Han Sans first, then Microsoft JhengHei, Microsoft YaHei, Yu Gothic or Malgun Gothic, found through fontconfig or the Windows fonts folder.
  - With no XeLaTeX or no CJK font, the paper goes straight to the HTML fallback.
  - Tables in the two-column `ieee_access` template: LaTeX's `longtable` cannot run in two-column mode, so a table that fits a column is set as a table float, and a table taller than a column gets single-column pages where it breaks across pages.
- **Selectable paper style** — `output.paper_style: latex | briefing` (default `latex`). `latex` keeps the venue LaTeX template (Computer Modern article). `briefing` renders `paper.pdf` in the Frontier Insight **"Research Briefing"** look — warm paper, deep-teal accents, serif display, the brand mark — via the HTML/Chromium backend, so it matches the slides + poster identity. It needs pandoc + a Chromium-family browser (no LaTeX) and falls back to the LaTeX path with a warning when those aren't present; it's single-column regardless of `paper_format`. Selectable in YAML and in the `--new` / `@fi /new` / web interview (the `paper_style` slot — under "Edit an advanced field" in VSCode). Works in all three interfaces because it's an engine-level config.
- **LaTeX-free PDF fallback** — `output.html_pdf_fallback` (default on) gives a 4th tier: when no LaTeX engine is reachable at all, pandoc renders `paper.md` into a Computer-Modern-styled HTML page (the `templates/paper/_html/latexlike.css` theme, with **Latin Modern Roman vendored + embedded** so the output matches the MiKTeX/tectonic `article` look — flush-left title, "Frontier Insight" byline, centered Abstract, numbered sections, justified body), then a headless system **browser** (Edge/Chrome/Chromium) prints it to `paper.pdf`. No LaTeX, no admin install — just pandoc (already needed) + a browser, which a locked-down machine almost always has; figures and fonts are inlined so the PDF is self-contained. It can't reproduce a venue's two-column class, so it always renders the single-column house style; set `output.html_pdf_fallback: false` to force the strict LaTeX-only path (skip-with-diagnostic when no engine). The pre-flight treats a present browser as satisfying the `paper_pdf` prerequisite, so `require_pdf: true` won't abort a quest that can render via the fallback. **The fallback also fires when a LaTeX engine is present but *errors*** — not just when none is found. A half-present MiKTeX that chokes on something the LLM emitted (a bare `\command` in text mode, an unsupported glyph) used to hard-skip the PDF; now the browser path — which never touches LaTeX — rescues it, so the same `paper.md` still produces a `paper.pdf` while the LaTeX error stays in run.log. The browser path reads the **same markdown dialect** as the LaTeX path and sets math as MathML, the `\(x\)` / `\[x\]` spellings included. What it cannot express it **keeps**: LaTeX written outside math prints as written and is named in a run.log warning, rather than being dropped. An HTML writer discards raw TeX silently, which is the worse failure — it turned "zero when `\(R_0\leq1\)`" into "zero when (R_0)" and a table header into `(R_0) (N) () () ()`, so the page read as a finished sentence that no longer said what the paper said.
- **Non-scientific paper formats** — `essay` / `report` / `policy_brief` / `whitepaper`, each with a full LaTeX template + per-format writer persona.
- **Author line** — `output.author`, `output.affiliation`, `output.contact_email` and `output.url` (all optional; not asked up front: the folded *Paper byline (optional)* line of the review screen's "What you get" card on the CLI, web and VSCode, and the first time a paper is written from a terminal the name is asked once (`launch._ask_byline_once`: Enter keeps the Frontier Insight byline and is remembered; no answer within 120 s goes on without one and asks next time; no terminal, as for the web page and VS Code, never asks); kept in `~/.frontier-insight/profile.json`, `core/profile.py`, which all three read; later interviews fill them from there and show them on the review screen, where a change is kept too; a blank byline is not kept while none was ever given) print under the paper title in every LaTeX template and the HTML render, set the PDF's Author field, go on the slides' title slide (HTML/PDF deck and pptx), and head the poster, whose QR code appears only when `url` is set. Pandoc receives them as metadata, which it escapes for LaTeX, so an `&` in an affiliation or a `_` in an email cannot break the compile. With no author set the byline stays "Frontier Insight". The values go only into the quest's own files and the profile file on this machine (the web page's `/api/profile` answers only a page opened on this machine, since the server has no login; a request a proxy marks as forwarded is refused, but a plain port forward looks local). `output.poster_size` (`a1_portrait` default, `a0_portrait`, `landscape_48x36`) picks the poster sheet.
- **Research poster** — `poster.pdf` follows published poster guidance instead of squeezing the paper onto a page.
  - **Header:** the main finding as the headline, the paper's title under it, the author line, and a QR code when `output.url` is set.
  - **Type:** body 26 pt on A1 (36 pt on the larger sheets), headings about 1.5× the body, numbered figure captions, about 60 characters a line.
  - **Content:** the model writes headings, short texts, bullet lists and captioned figures to a word budget for the sheet; the generator writes all the LaTeX.
  - **References:** only the sources the poster cites, at most 8, without raw URLs.
  - **Layout:** whole sections go to columns, and figures fill each column, narrowing to 70% of its width to fit. When one column is planned to end more than 6 cm above the other, the longer column's figures narrow further, down to half the column, until the columns end together.
  - **Checked:** every compile is measured (fonts, overflow, column balance), and the result lands in `.fi/poster_fit.json`. The first plan never cuts content, since the header and band heights are only known once compiled; a compile that measures more room than planned is planned again. The model's reply is kept in `.fi/poster_reply.txt`.
  - **CJK:** Chinese, Japanese or Korean text compiles with XeLaTeX and a CJK font, as for the paper.
- **Slides** — `slides.md` always; `slides.html` / `slides.pdf` (Marp CLI) when available, and **`slides.pptx` always** -- rendered in-process by `generation/_pptx_slides.py` with python-pptx, applying the same `templates/slides/fi.css` design language (warm paper ground, teal kicker rule above each serif slide title, teal list markers, wordmark footer) as the HTML deck. No external binary, so it renders on locked-down hosts; it replaced a `pandoc -o slides.pptx` shell-out that needed pandoc on PATH and produced an unthemed plain-white deck. Output is native, fully editable PowerPoint. **Each figure gets a slide of its own** (the prompt, `agents/slides.md`): the finding as the title, one bold lead sentence, the figure alone, and its discussion on the next slide; figure slides do not count toward the deck's 8-12. A figure is drawn at its slide box's width, and its tick labels shrink with it: beside bullets, a figure gets about 40% of the slide's width. Alone, the Marp theme lets it take all the room under the lead line (the 48vh cap stays for a figure that shares its slide with a list, table, code or quote), and the pptx draws it out to 0.75 in from each side edge and 0.6 in from the bottom instead of the text's margins. Measured on one stored quest's 13.1 in wide figures, a three-panel row went from 0.43 of the width it was drawn at (in the side pane) to 0.90 in the PDF and 0.87-0.90 in the pptx exported by LibreOffice. A 3x3 grid is height-bound and gains almost nothing by moving: 0.45 alone in the PDF (0.36 under the old cap) and 0.41 in the pptx, against 0.43 in the side pane. Grids are why a figure is capped at three panels (see Branded figures). A model that ignores the prompt is caught by the tick-label floor of the visual check below; whether models follow the prompt is not measured. This applies to the CLI, the web UI and the VSCode chat alike, since it is the engine's deck. Paragraphs and bullets keep the slide's order, and `**bold**`, `*italic*` and `_italic_` carry over (an underscore inside a word, as in `forward_euler`, stays as written). A Markdown **table** (`| a | b |` over a `|---|---|` delimiter row) becomes a **native PowerPoint table**, not lines of pipes: a tinted header row in the darker teal over a teal rule, hairlines between the rows, and each column as wide as its longest cell (compact when the table fits, wrapped inside the slide's body when it does not). `:---:` and `---:` set a column's alignment, and a cell reads like a bullet, so `**bold**`, `_italic_`, `` `code` `` and `$...$` math work in it; a cell that is only a formula keeps its column's alignment, which PowerPoint would otherwise set in the middle. The table counts in the slide's text height: its type is 14.5 pt and shrinks with the text down to a 10 pt floor, below which it is not shrunk further, so a table too tall even at 10 pt still runs past the bottom of the slide. `$...$` math (pandoc's rule, so "$5 and $7" stays text) becomes **native PowerPoint equations** (`generation/_pptx_math.py`: LaTeX → MathML with `latex2mathml` → OMML for rows, superscripts, subscripts, fractions and roots). Each equation carries a readable text form with real superscripts and subscripts for viewers without equation support. LibreOffice, and so the pptx visual check, shows that form. Math outside the supported set is written as that text only. A slide's text layer showing `$...$` or a LaTeX command is also measured as `raw_markup`. `poster.tex` always when `poster` is in `output.kinds`; `poster.pdf` compiles via the same 3-tier LaTeX-engine fallback the paper generator uses (pdflatex → system tectonic → repo-local `tools/tectonic[.exe]`), with a `poster_pdf_skipped.md` diagnostic on skip. Both the paper and poster run LLM-authored content through a shared Unicode sanitizer before pdflatex sees it — Greek/math glyphs become LaTeX commands, emoji are stripped, and **invisible/zero-width format characters** (zero-width spaces, joiners, BOM, soft hyphens, exotic spaces) are stripped or normalized, since a single stray U+200B otherwise hard-errors the compile. It finds math as pandoc does (`$...$` by pandoc's rule, so "$5 and $10" is text; `$$...$$`, `\(...\)`, `\[...\]`; never code), and inside a math span an en or em dash is set with `\text{}`, so a range written `$0.322–0.338$` prints a dash where LaTeX's `--` would print two minus signs, and a run of super- or subscript glyphs in math (`$10⁻³$`) is set as one script instead of stopping the compile. `speech` via single LLM call.
- **Consistent Frontier Insight design + branding** — the slide deck, poster, and paper share one editorial "Research Briefing" identity: a serif display face for titles, a system-sans body, a single deep-teal accent, and structure carried by hairline + teal rules rather than boxes. Each surface carries a subtle palette-native teal brand mark — a bottom-left corner lockup on the slides, a masthead lockup plus a reference-band mark on the poster, and a footer mark on the paper — driven from a single glyph asset so the look is uniform across all three.
- **Branded figures (matplotlib house style)** — experiment figures are no longer raw matplotlib defaults. Before the experiment runs, the engine drops a guarded `sitecustomize.py` bootstrap onto the script subprocess's `PYTHONPATH`, so Python applies the FrontierInsight house style at interpreter startup — *before* the generated code imports matplotlib, with zero edits to that code. The look: the brand teal-anchored qualitative color cycle (teal → terracotta → slate → olive → …), despined axes with a hairline y-grid, clean left-aligned editorial titles, frameless legends, and a branded teal sequential colormap (`fi_teal`) for heatmaps. The figure backdrop tracks `output.paper_style` — warm cream for `briefing`, white for `latex` — so figures blend into the paper they land in. Model-independent (it can't be forgotten or done wrong by the LLM) and failure-isolated (any error falls back to the plain environment). The same bootstrap records what each saved figure draws (`.fi/figure_records/<figure>.json`): every panel's title, axis labels, y scale and limits, and each line's or scatter's range, named by its label or by its entry in a legend given only names (`ax.legend(["euler", "rk4"])`), marked **flat** when it spans under 1% of its axis or **not shown** when it lies outside it. A series drawn by several calls with its label on the first (a scatter per point, say) is recorded as one series, and a single point is never flat. A reference drawn flat on purpose is not a flat series: an `axhline`, or a dashed or dotted line at one value, is recorded as a `reference line` at its value; and unlabelled lines drawn in exactly a labelled line's style (a family of runs with the label on the first) join that series, so its range is the family's, not the first run's. A series drawn with `ax.hlines`, `ax.vlines` or `fill_between` is recorded at the values it draws (the ends of its segments, the corners of its band), so a horizontal line at 0.94 is a series at 0.94 that shows **flat**, not one at 0; a vertical reference line (`axvline`) is recorded as a vertical line at its x, since its y is a fraction of the axes and not data (read as data it was a series from 0 to 1, "flat" on a count axis, and a caption was told to call it horizontal); a collection that cannot be read that way (a mesh, or one drawn in axes coordinates) is left out of the record rather than recorded at 0. The writer and the analysis see that record next to each figure's file name, and before review a script checks every caption against it: a caption naming a series its figure does not show, or one it draws flat without saying so in the same clause ("stay flat at this scale", "near zero", "indistinguishable" say so) (the validation quest captioned a plot "energy drift rates for RK4 and Velocity-Verlet" while both lines lay flat at 0 under Forward Euler's spike) is listed in the review prompt with an instruction to must-flag `figure_caption`, logged, and shown in the human-review panel of the web UI and the VSCode chat. The reverse is checked as well: a caption clause that names a series by its exact label and calls it flat, constant, unchanged or horizontal, when the figure draws that series varying over more than 5% of an axis's span in at least one panel, is listed the same way with the panels and ranges named (a stored caption said "deterministic final-size values drawn as flat reference lines within each R0 panel" of a series that runs from 0.002 to 0.073 in the R0=0.9 panel), and the writer is told to say that it varies there or to describe only the panels where it is flat. A clause that hedges ("nearly flat", "approximately constant"), denies ("not flat"), confines the claim to part of the run ("starts flat") or names a panel where the series is flat is not flagged. Its limits: it reads exact labels only, so a caption that says "flat deterministic predictions" of a series labelled "Deterministic ODE" is missed, and it was measured on SIR figures only, where 15 of 166 recorded figures had a caption using one of those words (11 correct, 3 describing a sloping series as flat, 1 that could be read either way) and it flags 1 of the 3 and none of the other 12; how it behaves on other kinds of study is not measured. The record also says what the finished canvas draws over what, because only the drawn positions can: on each save the bootstrap draws the figure once more and measures whether a figure title covers at least a fifth of a panel title (a suptitle with no room made for it sat over the middle panel's "N = 1000"), and whether a data line or a scatter's points run under a legend's box, measured three pixels in from its frame and only where the axes do not clip the data (a legend sat on the very lines it named). A record with no `layout` was not measured; one with an empty list was measured and is clean. A run whose figures have such a finding is sent back to the execute-repair step **once per quest**, before any paper is written: the repair prompt names the figure and what overlaps, says what to change (the legend outside the axes or in a corner the data does not reach, room for the title) and forbids touching any computation, data, seed use, printed value or `RESULT_JSON`, and the redraw's result is measured again. It costs one model call and one more run of the experiment, replicate seeds included, and only for a run that has such a finding. It is asked for only when nothing else is wrong with the run (a crash, an all-zero result or a broken bound is repaired first, and regenerates the figures anyway), only while one more attempt would be left to repair the redraw if it breaks the script, and never for a figure the engine drew itself as the mean over the seeds. A redraw that is refused, is not Python, or whose call fails leaves the script and its figures as they were, and a figure that still overlaps afterwards is logged, not asked about again. Only a legend over data lines and scatter points is measured: one over error-bar stems, bars, shaded bands or a reference line drawn with `axhline` or `axvline` is not. Replayed over the stored experiment scripts of 40 quests, which drew 102 figures, it flagged 10, each a real overlap (three of them mild). A figure planned by the design and drawn by the run that the paper leaves out is must-flagged `figure_missing` by the engine itself, whatever the reviewer says: the write prompt already asks for every figure, yet 3 of 12 real papers left one out, and one kept 1 of its 3. A planned figure the run did not draw, or a drawn one nobody planned, is not counted. With `execution.sandbox: docker` the record's path is a host path the container cannot write, so no record is made. The implement prompts also steer the model toward figures that *read well* — units on axes, direct labels over boxed legends, small-multiples for per-stratum comparisons — and away from dumping raw arrays into `RESULT_JSON`. **Text size:** the house style's text is 1.5 times the size it was first set at (`core/plot_style.py`, `FONT_SCALE`): ticks 15 pt, axis labels and body 17.25 pt, titles 20.25 pt; the canvas and the dpi did not change. A figure is shown at a fraction of the width it was drawn at, and the experiments draw 8-13 in wide (the house canvas is 7 in), so on the slides of six stored quests, ticks first set at 10 pt came out at 2.9-6.9 pt (median 4.6). Re-drawn under the larger text, those quests' papers kept their page counts (4, 4, 5, 5, 5 and 4 pages) and the recorder's layout check found nothing to flag on any of their figures; clipping starts at about 1.8x (a y label). A font size a script sets itself (a legend's `fontsize=8`) is not scaled. The recorder also writes each figure's smallest tick label size (`tick_pt`, null when it draws none), which the slide check reads. The web-only `web_plots` step keeps its own, older style (its own preamble in `core/engine.py`), which is not enlarged. **Panels:** the design, outline and implement prompts cap a figure at 3 panels side by side in one row (`plt.subplots(1, n)`, n at most 3), and a comparison that needs more (a grid over two factors) becomes several figures. This is the prompt only: no repair round enforces it, and its effect on a model is not measured.
- **Length-aware page fitting** — outputs that pack content onto a fixed page are auto-fitted so an over-long LLM result can't overflow. A slide figure takes only the room left under its slide's text (at most about half the slide, aspect kept), and a caption written on the line under a figure is split into its own paragraph, so neither the figure nor its caption runs off the bottom of the slide. In `slides.pptx` a figure goes 0.2 in under its slide's text by the text's estimated height, and the text shrinks before the figure gets less than 2.2 in. The estimate counts each line at its line spacing times 1.2 times the type size, measured against LibreOffice and PowerPoint on seven slides (within 3.3 pt of LibreOffice, up to 6 pt over PowerPoint); the old 1.0 left a figure 0.1 pt under the last bullet. The **poster** plans its columns from estimated block heights, compiles, and measures the PDF (`generation/_pdf_measure.py`). Sections stay whole where they fit, and the text or list right after a figure stays with that figure; a column that still has to open inside a section repeats the section's heading as "Heading (continued)". A first plan made for the guessed room that came out over its own limit is planned again for the measured room even when the sheet measures clean, since it may have split a section to get close. Content that runs past the sheet or into the reference band first narrows figures, then trims lists and sentences, then drops blocks from the middle; uneven columns are re-split. It stops after at most six compiles and never shrinks the type. The **paper** breaks reference URLs at any character (`xurl` + pandoc's `autolink_bare_uris` + `\urlstyle{same}`) so a long wiki-style link wraps inside the text column instead of running into the right margin.
- **Page limit** — `output.page_limit` (unset by default; the interview asks it as the advanced, tier-3 answer "Page limit" on the CLI `--new` review screen and `--update`, on the web review screen's Advanced block and the `/update/<id>` form, and under "Edit an advanced field" in `@fi /new`; blank means no set limit, and a changed limit re-runs write and review), or a limit the topic states: `≤ 4 pages`, `<= 4 pages`, `at most four pages`, `no more than 4 pages`, `up to 4 pages`, `4 pages max`, `a 4-page paper`. The config value wins over the topic, a bound in words wins over the `N-page` form (which can name a part, as in "a 2-page appendix"), and a range such as `4–8 pages` is a length, not a limit. Without a limit the layout, the write prompt and the review are unchanged. With one:
  - **Layout:** the LaTeX templates switch on the `fi-tight` variable. The templates with 1 in margins (`generic`, `neurips`, `iclr`, `report`, `whitepaper`) use 2 cm; the others keep their own (`ieee_access`'s 0.75 in is already tighter). Figure height is not part of this switch: every paper caps a figure at 33% of the text height, limit or no limit. The References and Further reading lists are set in `\small`, by raw LaTeX added to `paper_pdf_source.md` only, so `paper.md` stays as written. The final `paper.pdf` and the visual check's recompile go through the same render, so they get the same layout. On three real 5- and 6-page papers from quests whose topic asked for at most 4 pages, the layout alone took one from 5 to 4 pages and one from 6 to 5; the third stayed at 5, its last page 91% empty.
  - **Word budget:** the write prompt tells the writer the limit, which overrides the `Study depth` length, and a budget of about limit × 450 words less a third of a page (0.37 × 450 words) for each figure the run drew (or the design planned), and that the source lists added after the last section take room on the pages too.
  - **Measured after each draft:** the review renders the draft with the same template and flags as the final `paper.pdf`, into `.fi/page_check/` with the quest's figures copied beside it, and counts its pages. A draft over the limit gets the forced, text-only must-flag `over_page_limit`, which gives the writer the page count, the limit and about how many words to cut (450 for each full page past the first one over, plus the filled share of the last page, at least 100), and asks it to keep every figure and number and shorten background and discussion first. Only the paper is written again (write → claim check → review). Only the measured count forces this hit: an `over_page_limit` the reviewer or a panelist writes itself is dropped, with a warning, since it could send the paper back without end.
  - **Further reading goes first, without a rewrite:** FI writes `## Further reading` itself and sets it after the body, so on a paper cut to the limit it is what spills onto one page more. When the measured draft is over the limit, the review first renders it with as many Further reading entries removed as may go (an entry the text cites, as `[W2]`, is never one of them, since the citation would name nothing). If that still is not within the limit, the body is what is too long and nothing changes. Otherwise it renders the draft with the last entry dropped, then the last two, and so on (one render per entry at most, about 3 seconds each on a 4-page paper), and the first draft that fits is written to `paper/paper.md`: the entries dropped are the last ones, `paper.md`, `paper.pdf` and `paper/further_reading.bib` then list the same pages, one log line says how many were dropped and which, and the review's `page_limit` record names them in `further_reading_dropped`. No model is called, the body is not rewritten, and `page_limit_rewrites` is not counted; a later whole-paper rewrite writes the full list again and the same trim runs on that draft. Only a list FI wrote is touched. Measured on a stored 5-page paper whose limit is 4 (page 5 held two of its three entries): entries 2 and 3 dropped, 4 pages, three extra renders, 8 seconds; on 4-page papers nothing changes. All three interfaces share the engine.
  - **A few sentences out, without a rewrite:** when the body is what is over the limit and about 400 words or fewer must go, the review asks the model which sentences to take out instead of sending the paper back. The model reads the draft with a marker before each sentence it may choose (`<<12: 23 words>>`: its number and its length) and answers `{"delete": [12, 7, 30]}`, the numbers of sentences that together are between the words to cut and 40 more (`agents/write_trim.md`; the call is logged as node `write.trim`, runs at temperature 0 and uses the model set for `write` unless `provider.node_models` names `write.trim`). What it may choose is decided in code, not by the model. Only sentences in a section called Introduction, Background, Related work, Discussion, Limitations, Conclusion (or prior work, literature, future work, implications, context, overview) are offered, and of those never: the sentence that opens a paragraph, a list item, a table row, a heading, a figure or a caption; a sentence that refers to a figure or a table; one that defines a term, a symbol or an abbreviation (`let`, `denote`, `defined as`, `where $x$`, `(SIR)`); one that says what the paper does or finds (`we`, `our`, `this study`, `here`); in an Introduction, the sentence that says why the work is needed (`However, …`, `While …, … fails to …`); a sentence the next one points back at (`However`, `This`, `It`, …); one that states a number the rest of the paper does not state; or one shorter than 6 words. The Abstract, the Methods and the Results are therefore as written whatever the model answers. The engine takes the listed sentences out in the model's order until the words are gone, skipping any that holds the last remaining citation of a source or the last remaining statement of a number (so no source becomes uncited, no citation number changes and no number is lost), renders the draft, and, when the list fell short or the draft is still over, asks again on the paper as it now stands: at most 3 calls. The draft is written to `paper/paper.md` only when it fits. The review's `page_limit` record then carries `trimmed: {words, sentences}`, `run.log` names the sentences, and the claims the check listed for a sentence taken out leave `paper/claims.json`, `paper/CLAIMS.md` and the review's claim list, unless another sentence of the paper still says them. No claim check or review is repeated for the trim. It is tried after the Further reading and before the shortening rewrite, also when the two rewrites are used up, and does not use `page_limit_rewrites`. A reply that is not a list of offered numbers, a failed call, too few sentences that may go, or a draft still over after 3 calls leaves the paper as it was, one warning says why, and the review forces `over_page_limit` as before. Measured: the stored run logs hold 77 forced shortenings, 39 that asked for 100 or 150 words and 57 for 350 or fewer (20 asked for 600 or more), and the small ones are the cases a trim is for. Under these rules the sections that may lose a sentence offer a median of 173 words on the 73 stored SIR papers, and the most that could be taken without breaking a rule is a median of 162 (for 33 of the 73 that is under 150 words, for 14 under 100), so a paper with few sentences that may go, such as a stored one whose background and discussion offer 106 words, is not trimmed and falls back to the rewrite. With the real model and the real render, on stored papers made one page too long by added background paragraphs, 2 of 2 came back to 4 pages: 114 words taken out in one call of 3,067 tokens, 224 words in three calls of 8,983 tokens, against about 43,000 tokens for a rewrite round (write, claim check, review); no number, citation, figure line, heading, Abstract, Method, Result or reference changed. A model asked to order the sentences returned the list in document order, and asked for the least valuable ones chose the statement of the paper's aim and the Introduction's first sentences, which is why the code, not the model, decides what may go. Not measured: whether the sentences chosen read better or worse than a rewrite's shortening, and how often a real first draft is within reach of a trim.
  - **Own counter:** these shortening rewrites are counted in `page_limit_rewrites`, at most 2, and do not use `engine.max_iterations`. When `over_page_limit` is the only must-flag, the paper is rewritten even with the iterations spent and the review does not bump the iteration. With other must-flags the usual route and budget apply, and the shortening still counts. A draft still over after two shortening rewrites is not forced again; the review records `page_limit: {pages, limit, rewrites, exceeded_after_rewrites: true}`. Every measured draft records its `pages` and `limit` there.
  - **Not measured:** a paper that renders through HTML (`paper_style: briefing` with a browser present, or no LaTeX engine), or a draft that does not render; the run logs one warning and forces nothing.
- **Visual check of every rendered PDF** — `output.visual_check` (default on) runs after the outputs render, on each PDF the pass produced (paper, slides, poster). A resume checks only the outputs it made. Each PDF is measured and screenshotted; the AI step, which sends the screenshots to the provider, runs only with `output.visual_check_ai: true` (default off, about 12,000 tokens a quest).
  - **slides.pptx:** with LibreOffice installed (`generation/_office_pdf.py`, found on PATH or in its default install folder), the pptx is exported to PDF with a fresh LibreOffice profile, so a LibreOffice window the user has open is never involved, and checked as `slides_pptx` with the slides' checklist and measurements. It is checked once, after any slides redo, since it comes from the same `slides.md`. Without LibreOffice the report says the pptx was not checked. `--doctor` lists LibreOffice.
  - **What it sends (with `output.visual_check_ai: true`):** screenshots at 90 dpi (longest edge at most 2000 px) and the `_pdf_measure` measurements go to the configured provider, with a fixed checklist of what only eyes can see: cut-off text, overlaps, unreadable figures, raw markup, broken math, garbled text and empty areas, plus a check or two per output. Sizes, overflow, a figure drawn over a slide's text, a figure whose tick labels come out under 8 pt on its slide (below), and a figure starting less than 6 pt under a line of text (`figure_gap`, low severity, never a redo), and a paper page before the last with 35% or more of its text area empty (`half_empty_page`, never a redo) come from the measurements, not the model.
  - **Tick labels on a slide (`figure_ticks_small`):** a figure's tick labels must be at least 8 pt on the slide (`SLIDE_FIGURE_TICK_MIN_PT` in `generation/_pdf_measure.py`, one constant). Each image on a slide is matched to a file in `figures/` by its pixel size (by its proportions when LibreOffice has resampled it, as in the pptx), and its tick size on the slide is the figure's tick size times the width it is placed at over the width it was drawn at (the file's pixels over its dpi). The tick size is the figure's record, `tick_pt` in `.fi/figure_records/<figure>.json`, which counts a script's own `tick_params(labelsize=...)`; a record from before the recorder wrote it takes the house style's tick size, since a figure of the house style is drawn at that size unless its script says otherwise (so a quest drawn before the house text was enlarged, and checked again on a resume, is read 1.5 times larger than it was drawn, and a figure whose ticks really come out between 5.3 and 8 pt on its slide passes). Only figures the recorder saw are measured: a fetched web figure or a `web_plots` figure has no record and its tick size is unknown, and under `execution.sandbox: docker` no figure has one, so the check is silent there. The finding names the slide, the figure, how wide it is drawn and the tick size it comes out at, and tells the slides model to give the figure a slide of its own, with no bullets, and put its discussion on the next slide. That finding is medium and is redone when the figure shares its slide with text; a figure that already has its slide alone and is still under 8 pt (a grid too tall for one slide) is low: it is reported and not redone, since no new deck changes it. The redo is the existing one (`output.visual_check_max_redos`, at most 2), and the version that checks best is kept, so a model that ignores the finding uses at most two redo rounds; the pptx is only reported. On the stored quest measured, its old deck was flagged on all three figure slides (4.1-4.3 pt), and a deck with each figure alone and at most three panels is silent (13.0-13.5 pt).
  - **Grounded findings:** every finding must quote text visible where the problem is. A quote that is not on that page (or the page before or after), a check not on the list, or a finding with no region is dropped, and the report keeps it with the reason.
  - **Redo:** the slides and poster are generated again with the findings a new version can fix, at most `output.visual_check_max_redos` times (0–2, default 2). The slides' model writes the whole deck, so its layout findings count. On the poster only text the model wrote counts, since the layout is the planner's. The version that checks best is kept and a worse one is put back file for file. The paper is never rewritten: when its last page holds only a line or two (measured as `last_page_nearly_empty`), it is recompiled once with a text area one line taller (its footer stays where it was), and the better version is kept.
  - **Providers without image input** check from the measurements alone, and the report says so. [docs/PROVIDERS.md](PROVIDERS.md) lists which providers can see images.
  - **Report:** `.fi/visual_check.json`, one entry per output with its attempts, plus the screenshots under `.fi/visual_check/<output>/`. The CLI and the VSCode chat print one line per output, the web quest page shows the same lines (read from the report, so a running quest shows them too), and `frontier_insight_summary.json` carries each output's result in brief. A check never stops the quest.
- **Citations in every output** — the sources a quest actually consulted (web pages carry their URL) are rendered as References across all three surfaces, not just the paper: the engine builds the paper's numbered References section from the papers its text cites, numbered in the order the text first cites them, rewrites the citations to those numbers and drops any citation of a number the prior-work block does not have (the literature is reordered to match, so the claim check, poster, slides, bib and the next write pass use the same numbers); the poster's reference band lists the sources its text cites (at most 8, built from the retrieved literature, so it survives even when the LLM only saw a truncated `paper.md`, and showing a web page's site name rather than its URL); the slide deck ends on one appended **References** slide, in both the HTML/PDF deck and the pptx: the sources the paper cites most (at most 6, as many as fit at 18 pt, in two columns: `_SOURCE_SLIDE_*` in `core/engine.py` are counted at that size, so change the theme's 0.96em and the budget together), in reference order, and a line saying how many more are in the paper. Web URLs and paper DOIs are both first-class; FI-internal cross-quest entries are filtered out of external-facing outputs.
- **Claim grounding** — `engine.claim_grounding` (default on) inserts a `claim_check` node between `write` and `review` that extracts the paper's substantive claims and grounds each as **experiment** (it restates a number/result this quest produced), **citation** (it rests on a specific cited `[N]` paper in References, or a `[W<k>]` web page in Further reading), or **unsupported**. For each source the paper cites, the check is shown the passages of its text most related to the citing sentences (up to 6,000 characters), and a citation must quote that text: FI looks the quote up in the source (ignoring case, quote marks and line-break hyphens, at least 25 characters), and a claim with no quote, or a quote the source does not contain, is unsupported. A quote that is not there as written is looked for once more with the mathematics folded, because a PDF and the paper quoting it render the same formula differently and neither is then a substring of the other: one source wrote its reproduction number `ℛ(0)` in its abstract and `R`, a line break, `0` in its full text, against a paper that wrote `R0`; the same exponent appears as `( i )`, as `^i`, and as a bare `i`; `$\mathcal{R}$` is `R`; and the spacing around an operator is typesetting. Only layout is folded — the numbers, the operators and the order of the tokens all still have to match, so a quotation that changes one of them is still rejected, and a quote found as written is accepted without folding anything. Without this the check called a formula quoted **verbatim** unsupported, and a run could spend its whole review loop rewriting a paper around a citation that was right. The same second chance allows for what a source's text extraction does to its layout, each only for a quote that is not there as written: a space the source has inside a word (`equati on`, `p robability`) or before the hyphen that broke a word at the end of a line (`inap -` line break `propriate`); a stacked fraction the source has as a numerator and a denominator on separate lines with no bar, against a quote that writes `1/R0`. The allowance is one-directional: the source may have a space the quote does not, but a quote may not add one (`the rapist` is not found in `therapist`), and a word changed, left out or added, two sentences that are not neighbours run together, and a quote of another source are all still rejected. A formula the source has lost altogether is not a layout difference and is not allowed for: a web page's text with its mathematics taken out reads `Given  and , the epidemic ends at time t, when .`, nothing in it says what the formulas were, and matching the words either side of the hole would accept any formula a quote put there, so a quote that spans one is not found and the writer has to quote words the source has whole. The quest's own evidence has a budget of its own (14,000 characters): every key finding and every claim the analysis says the run supports is shown whole — a finding is never cut off part-way, and none is dropped for being last — and the run's results fill what is left, all of them when they fit, otherwise the top-level branches most related to those findings and to the paper itself, each one whole, with a long numeric array shown as its length and its range instead of printed. Ranking whole branches is what reaches a result a large sweep buries: the leading characters of a big result set are all sweep, so a validation the paper's claims rest on would never appear at any budget. The check is also shown how the study was run: the frozen protocol, the scripts that ran (each up to 8,000 characters), what each figure draws, and the engine's own oracle verdicts. A sentence about the generator, the solver or how many oracle trials ran is then checked against the code that ran it, and the model's own equations are its method, not a claim to ground; without them a real run had its true method sentences called unsupported. A sentence citing several sources is grounded by the one whose text supports it, and a rewrite is told which citations the check confirmed, so a rewrite for another reason (the page limit) keeps them. A transparency ledger is written next to the paper — **`paper/claims.json`** (structured) + **`paper/CLAIMS.md`** (grounded-fraction + the unsupported list) — and any unsupported claims are surfaced to the reviewer with an instruction to must-flag them. The check and the review both read the whole paper; above 120,000 characters they read the start and are told the paper continues. When the check cannot run on a draft (its model call still fails after the provider's retries), the failure is recorded and the review forces `citations_unchecked`: nothing checked that draft's citations, so it is written and checked again, and it cannot be accepted until the check runs. When a reviewer does raise a must-flag, the revise pass is then **non-bypassable and deterministic** (forced even with `review_loop` off, capped by `max_iterations`) — but note the trigger runs *through* the reviewer reading the unsupported list, not a direct `claim_check → revise` edge, so enforcement is only as reliable as the panel (the methodologist persona owns it; a single-reviewer/empty panel weakens it). When the review's own must-fix prose — its blocking sentence, its weaknesses and its suggestions — names something this run computed (a key of `result_json`, a figure's underlying data, or `experiment.py`), no rewrite of the paper can fix it, so the experiment is written and run again instead (`review` → `implement` → `execute` → … → `write`), with what the review named and its must-fix prose added to the implement prompt — which otherwise carries only the design and would regenerate the same code. This replaces the text rewrite and nothing else: a must-flag that already sends the quest back to `design` (`circular_evaluation` and the rest) re-runs the experiment on its way through `implement` anyway, and a flawed design is not fixed by writing the same experiment again; a draft merely over the page limit keeps its own route and counter. A must-flag hit is a short identifier by the review's own schema, so what it is about can only be in that prose — and the hits themselves are deliberately not read, because the ones the engine forces quote the figure they checked (`figure_caption: the caption of figures/x.png names …`, `figure_missing: figures/x.png`), which would read as a problem with that figure's data when it is a problem with the paper's words. The prose is matched against names this quest actually produced rather than a list of words, and a results key must be a compound name (`deterministic_final_size`), so a leaf called `mean` cannot match prose by accident. The advisory numeric-oracle findings are not read here, so a flagged number still never forces a re-run. A re-run costs an iteration, so it happens at most once per quest and a flag that survives it takes the text route. Otherwise, when every must-flag is a problem with the text (`unsupported_claim`, `figure_caption`, `figure_missing`), the experiment stands and only the paper is written again (`review` → `write` → `claim_check` → `review`, with the review, the unsupported claims and the user's feedback in the write prompt; when each of those hits names a passage, the write node edits those passages instead, next bullet); any other must-flag sends the quest back to `design`. Set `engine.claim_grounding: false` to make the node a no-op passthrough (no LLM call, no ledger). The ledger records each citation's quote.
- **A revise edits the passages the review flagged** — a whole rewrite of a reviewed draft does more harm than the flags it answers: on the stored quests each one added background sentences whose citations their sources do not back (28 of 38 graded papers ended at the iteration cap still flagged `unsupported_claim`), one swapped two citation numbers in a paper whose first draft had them right, and one dropped all three figures. So when every must-fix hit names one passage of the paper — an `unsupported_claim` (the claims the check listed), a `figure_caption` (the figure its finding names), an `unsourced_number` or a `mislabelled_statistic` (the sentence it quotes) — and nothing the paper was written from has been run again since the draft (design, results, analysis, figures, cross-check and the user's feedback are fingerprinted in `paper_basis`), the write node does not write the paper again. It gives the writer the earlier draft and only those passages (`agents/write_patch.md`; the call is logged as node `write.patch` and uses the model set for `write` unless `provider.node_models` names `write.patch`) and asks for edits, `[{"find": "<text copied from the draft>", "replace": "<new text, or empty to take it out>"}]`. The engine applies an edit only where its `find` occurs exactly once in the draft's text before the source lists, applies all of them together, and leaves every other character as it was: the figures, the headings and the figure numbers included. The References and Further reading are then rebuilt from the citations in the edited text by the same step a whole paper goes through, so an edit that leaves a source uncited renumbers the sources after it, in the text and in the list together, and a citation of a number the prior-work block does not have is taken out. The checks paraphrase rather than quote, so the engine finds the passage a flag means: the flagged text as it stands, else the one sentence (or two adjacent ones) whose content words cover at least 80% of it, and never one of two that fit equally. That sentence is handed to the writer as the draft's own words; a flag the engine cannot place is handed over as text and the writer finds it. On the 314 unsupported claims in the final drafts of the stored quests, 13% were an exact substring of the paper and 81% could be matched to a sentence this way; a caption is found by the figure file its finding names. A round writes the whole paper instead when a hit is about the whole paper (`over_page_limit`, `figure_missing`, `citations_unchecked`), when a hit names no passage, when the study has been run again since the draft, or when the draft is over 120,000 characters, and the same fallback follows a reply that is not JSON, is a whole paper or holds no edit, an edit whose `find` is missing or occurs more than once, edits that overlap or replace more than 40% of the draft, and an edit that changes a figure link or a heading. Each of those is one line in `run.log`: `[write] writing the whole paper again: …` or `[write] the edits could not be used (…); writing the whole paper again`.
